## [DP-MIND Enables Differentially Private Million-Token Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13132) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DP-MIND introduces a memory-guided framework that scales differentially private inference to million-token contexts by locally perturbing inputs and processing them chunk by chunk with a compact memory that preserves task-relevant information. It optimizes the memory construction policy on perturbed inputs using reinforcement learning guided by answer correctness and memory consistency, and adds a local denoising module to recover perturbation-corrupted answer spans. This addresses a critical gap in privacy-preserving LLM-as-a-Service, where existing DP inference methods fail on million-token inputs due to limited context capacity and severe utility degradation under DP noise. It could enable confidential long-document analysis, such as legal or medical records, without exposing raw context to external models. The framework combines local input perturbation, chunk-wise processing with a compact memory, reinforcement-learning-optimized memory construction, and a local denoising module, with theoretical privacy analysis and empirical evaluation on long-context benchmarks up to the million-token scale. It reportedly outperforms existing DP and long-context inference baselines in both scalability and utility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Differential privacy (DP) is a mathematical framework that adds calibrated noise to data or computations so that individual inputs cannot be distinguished, commonly used to protect sensitive information in machine learning. Long-context inference in large language models involves processing very long input sequences, which strains memory and computation, especially when the context reaches millions of tokens. LLM-as-a-Service lets users query remote models without local deployment, but sending sensitive contexts to an external provider raises privacy risks, motivating DP inference methods that perturb inputs before they leave the user's device.

## [Competence-Aware Orchestration Boosts LLM Multi-Agent Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13144) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13144) proposes a two-stage competence-aware framework that infers an LLM backbone's task-specific competence for a given query and then dynamically adapts role activation and the communication graph in a multi-agent system. Across six benchmarks it achieves 93.81% average performance, and with higher-competence backbones it improves average accuracy from 87.22 to 89.97 while cutting token cost by 36.26%. This work tackles a central inefficiency in LLM-based multi-agent systems: more collaboration is not always better, since richer coordination can add token cost and redundant interactions. By making orchestration conditional on inferred backbone competence, it offers a practical way to scale multi-agent reasoning while controlling cost, which matters for both high- and low-competence models and even unseen backbones. The framework operates in two stages: first modeling the backbone's task-conditioned competence for the current query, then using that signal to adapt role activation and the communication graph. Experiments span six benchmarks and six LLM backbones of varying competence, with lower-competence backbones gaining an additional average 4.58 points over existing methods, and the same trend holding on unseen backbones.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) use multiple role-specialized LLM instances that collaborate to solve complex reasoning tasks, and prior work has shown they can exhibit collective intelligence beyond a single model. A key design question is how to coordinate these agents, often represented as a communication graph that determines which agents exchange messages. Competence-aware orchestration means deciding how much collaboration to enable based on how capable the underlying LLM is for the current query, rather than always maximizing interaction.

## [VIGOR: Verifiable Geometry Optimization and Repair for LLM-Generated Industrial Scene Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13145) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2610.13145) introduces VIGOR, a method that combines supervised scene-program generation with execution-grounded verification and staged geometric refinement. The authors also release an execution-grounded industrial scene-program dataset containing 1,749 constructible scenarios, complete executable programs, and 94 final-state geometric checks per scenario, with VIGOR reaching 96.86% Complete Feasibility and an Overall score of 91.20, outperforming the strongest baseline by 10.42 points. The work formalizes the "executability-engineering-feasibility gap": an LLM-generated industrial layout can run without errors yet still violate coupled boundary, collision, clearance, aisle, or structural constraints. By making this gap measurable and repairable, the paper argues that reliable scene-program generation for industrial automation requires explicit verification and coordinated conflict resolution, not just executable code. VIGOR uses supervised fine-tuning for complete program generation, local projection to fix independently correctable violations, and a joint conflict-graph repair stage that coordinates residual multi-object conflicts while preserving structural relations. The evaluation is deliberately controlled, using 94 final-state geometric checks per scenario, so the reported 96.86% Complete Feasibility reflects performance under these specific industrial layout conditions rather than open-world settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used to turn natural-language instructions into structured layouts and executable scene programs, for example in industrial or building automation. Execution-grounded verification means checking model outputs by actually running them in a controlled environment and inspecting concrete state transitions, rather than trusting the generated text. Geometry optimization and repair refers to adjusting the positions and shapes of objects so that spatial constraints such as clearance, collision, and aisles are satisfied. VIGOR sits at the intersection of these ideas, adding a verification-and-repair layer on top of LLM-generated scene programs.

## [TQS: Scoring Coding-Agent Trajectory Quality for Better Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13152) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13152) proposes TQS (Trajectory Quality Score), a structural scoring framework that evaluates coding-agent interaction trajectories by process quality rather than only whether the final patch passes the benchmark test suite. Using 155,144 successful trajectories from a public open-license corpus, the authors rank trajectories by TQS and show that models fine-tuned on top-ranked subsets consistently outperform those trained on random or bottom-ranked subsets on SWE-Bench-Verified. This work addresses a known blind spot in coding-agent training: outcome-level filtering treats every passing trajectory as equally good, even when it contains inefficient patterns like repetitive file inspection or delayed edits that get imitated by the model. By offering a scalable, linear-time, judge-free alternative to LLM-based data selection, TQS could improve how the community curates supervised fine-tuning data for software-engineering agents. TQS operates directly on trajectory structure and characterizes quality through structural signals organized around "structured convergence": information focus, intent-action alignment, and policy dynamics. It requires no LLM judge and runs in linear time, making it practical for large corpora, though the evaluation is limited to SWE-Bench-Verified and a single public corpus of successful trajectories.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the process of further training a pre-trained language model on labeled task-specific examples, and for coding agents the training data typically consists of interaction trajectories — sequences of reasoning steps, tool calls, and file edits. The common practice is to keep only trajectories whose final code patch passes the benchmark's test suite, which ignores how the agent got there. SWE-Bench-Verified is a curated benchmark of real GitHub issues used to measure whether an agent can produce correct patches.

## [AdaC-GRPO Uses Adaptive Curriculum to Solve Visual Sudoku Puzzles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13184) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose AdaC-GRPO, an adaptive curriculum learning method that dynamically tracks a model's Zone of Proximal Development via online rollout accuracy to overcome reward sparsity in long-horizon visual Sudoku puzzles. They also introduce FG-Sudoku, a fine-grained benchmark categorized by grid size and required deductive steps, showing that frontier MLLMs fail on complex 9x9 puzzles. This work addresses a key bottleneck in applying reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR) to long-horizon multimodal reasoning, where near-zero success rates cause severe reward sparsity and halt standard training. The approach enables Qwen2.5-VL-7B to approach Gemini-2.5-Flash performance and bridge 62.2% of the accuracy gap to Gemini-2.5-Pro, with generalizability validated across InternVL-3.5 and Qwen3-VL. AdaC-GRPO dynamically constructs training batches by tracking the model's Zone of Proximal Development through online rollout accuracy, unlike rigid or prior-based schedules. The FG-Sudoku benchmark is categorized by grid size and required deductive steps, and the abstract lacks full experimental details such as exact training configurations or ablation studies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR) is a technique that trains large language models using automatically checkable ground-truth rewards, and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is a critic-free RL algorithm that samples groups of outputs and scores them relatively. The Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD) is a pedagogical concept describing the gap between what a learner can do alone and what they can achieve with support; adaptive systems use ZPD to select optimally challenging tasks. Visual Sudoku puzzles require multi-step deductive reasoning over both local constraints and global dependency chains, making them a representative long-horizon multimodal task.

## [JANUS Adds Co-Evolving World Models to Long-Horizon Autonomous Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13194) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13194v1) introduces JANUS, a framework that replaces the reactive Generate-Execute-Feedback paradigm of autonomous research with a Predict-Verify-Evolve loop driven by a co-evolving world model. Within an evolving graph, the research agent proposes candidate branches while the world model scores them by forecasting value improvement, information gain, and exploration utility, then selects, refines, or prunes branches before execution. Long-horizon autonomous research is bottlenecked by costly, slow execution feedback, so JANUS's ability to forecast and prioritize research branches before running them could substantially raise both the efficiency and the ceiling of AI-driven scientific discovery. The reported gains on MLS-Bench-Lite and real research tasks suggest the approach is relevant to anyone building autonomous research agents or AI-for-science systems. After receiving ground-truth feedback, JANUS distills experience into evolving skills: hierarchical skills for the research agent, exploration-strategy skills for the world model, and coordination protocols between them, enabling continual co-evolution. The abstract is truncated in the provided content, so full experimental numbers, baselines, and limitations are not yet visible.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Autonomous research agents typically operate in a Generate-Execute-Feedback loop: they propose hypotheses, implement and run experiments, then use the results to guide the next round. Because each execution can be slow and expensive, this reactive trial-and-error approach suffers from severe feedback delays, especially as the search space expands. World models — learned predictors of future outcomes — are increasingly used in AI to enable look-ahead planning and reduce reliance on real execution, and JANUS applies this idea to the research loop itself.

## [DREAM: Neuro-Inspired Memory Repair for Poisoned LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13267) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced DREAM (Dynamic Reactivation for Engram Attenuation in Memory), a training-free, agent-intrinsic memory repair framework that selectively forgets poisoned memories in LLM agents by mimicking neurobiological memory decay. Experiments show it cuts backdoor poisoning attack success rates by over 95% while preserving benign utility, achieving up to 2.13x speedup and 37.7% token reduction versus A-MemGuard, and a 92.96% task success rate on poisoned multi-agent systems. Memory poisoning is a persistent and stealthy threat to LLM agents, since corrupted memories can silently hijack retrieval, reasoning, and actions long after the initial attack. A training-free defense that works inside the agent without external auditing modules could make agent deployments more secure and efficient, benefiting developers building long-running autonomous systems. DREAM uses a three-stage pipeline: perturbation-induced implicit reactivation to expose the structural fragility of malicious memories, adaptive anomaly diagnosis based on multi-dimensional activation patterns to detect topological anomalies, and a dynamic repair module that suppresses harmful memories while reinforcing benign ones. It is training-free and agent-intrinsic, though the abstract does not detail scalability limits or performance on non-backdoor attack types beyond noting competitive robustness against injection attacks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents often maintain persistent memory to store past interactions, which lets them act coherently over long tasks but also creates an attack surface: adversaries can inject malicious content into that memory so it influences future behavior, a problem known as memory poisoning. Existing defenses typically rely on external modules for static memory isolation or continuous online auditing, which can be inefficient and costly. DREAM draws on neuroscience research suggesting that weakly reactivated memories can become unstable and decay without reinforcement, and applies this idea to selectively weaken poisoned memories.

## [BoundaryEvo Co-Evolves Data and Model for Long-Chain Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13285) ⭐️ 7.0/10

BoundaryEvo introduces a two-stage data-model co-evolution framework for long-chain reasoning distillation that first primes basic reasoning capability and then repeatedly selects questions at the student's evolving capability boundary. Using only 1,000 training examples, it raises Qwen3.5-4B accuracy from 62.9 to 75.3 on IMO-AnswerBench and from 26.8 to 46.0 on AMO-Bench across 11 reasoning benchmarks. This addresses a key limitation of fixed data selection in reasoning distillation, where examples mastered by the student provide little new supervision and examples far beyond its ability are hard to learn from. The approach is especially valuable for small language models and low-data regimes, offering a more efficient path to strong reasoning performance. The framework has two stages: Capability Priming directly distills a small set of teacher-generated long reasoning trajectories without a separate judge model, and Capability-Boundary Co-Evolution repeatedly selects questions the current student has not mastered but the expert can reliably solve. The code and data are promised to be publicly released.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Long-chain reasoning distillation transfers long reasoning trajectories from large teacher models to small student models to improve their reasoning ability. Existing methods typically select a fixed training set before distillation, ignoring that the usefulness of a reasoning example changes as the student evolves. Capability boundary refers to the frontier of problems a model can currently solve, and data-model co-evolution means the training data and model improve together in a closed loop.

## [ChemDiffAgent: Masked Diffusion Language Agent for Chemical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13330) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ChemDiffAgent, described as the first masked diffusion language agent for tool-integrated chemical reasoning, which reformulates reasoning as iterative denoising over interleaved reasoning and tool-use trajectories. Using a two-stage post-training framework of agentic supervised fine-tuning and variance-reduced preference optimization, the 8B model reportedly outperforms autoregressive agents by roughly 20% and rivals much larger frontier models like GPT 5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. This work challenges the dominant autoregressive paradigm in AI agents by showing that masked diffusion can improve global tool-use planning and multi-source evidence integration in scientific domains. If validated, it could shift how AI-for-science agents are built, enabling smaller open models to compete with much larger proprietary systems on complex chemistry tasks. The approach benefits from harder infilling subproblems during training and flexible arbitrary-order decoding at inference, allowing tool-use decisions to be made under full-trajectory context. The authors also built a benchmark covering six chemistry tasks in both single-turn and multi-turn tool-use settings, with code released at https://github.com/bilemond/ChemDiffAgent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Autoregressive language models generate text left-to-right, which is inherently sequential and unidirectional, making bidirectional understanding and global planning difficult. Masked diffusion language models instead generate text by iteratively denoising masked tokens, similar to how image diffusion models turn noise into images, enabling parallel and arbitrary-order decoding. Tool-integrated agents combine language models with external tools such as calculators or chemistry software to solve tasks requiring precise computation or domain knowledge.

SWE-Critix introduces a post-training pipeline that combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with reinforcement learning (RL) to train generalist verifiers that judge and explain coding-agent execution trajectories. On a held-out test set, the 30B model improves classification accuracy from 57% for the base Qwen3-30B-A3B reasoning model to 66% after SFT and 78% after RL, with models, datasets, and training code released. As coding agents are deployed for end-to-end software engineering tasks, there is often no readily available oracle evaluator to define success, so reliable verifiers are needed to judge and explain agent behavior. This work addresses the limited exploration of training-based generalist verification, which could improve evaluation and trust in long-horizon coding agents. The verifier takes a task and its execution trajectory as input, predicts a completion label, and generates a natural-language analysis explaining its judgment. The pipeline trains a series of 30B models on trajectories from CodeForge-Preview, and the paper notes that coding-agent trajectories can be extremely long, containing hundreds of iterative interactions with code repositories and external tools.

SWE-Critix targets this gap by post-training a generalist verifier that both classifies outcomes and explains them.

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2610.13416v1) introduces Web$hell, an interface that unifies web search, browser actions, and evidence revisiting within a single agent trajectory. It uses a common schema called WebContract, with WebCompiler compiling live webpages into addressable information and action objects that are stored in WebCache and manipulated through a shared set of operations. Existing agentic web access models — search-augmented, browser-based, and filesystem-based agents — typically support either information gathering or browser interaction, and combining them incurs substantial cost or performance trade-offs. Web$hell claims strong task performance at lower cost across web search, navigation, and deep research benchmarks, which could make long-horizon, end-to-end LLM agent tasks more practical. The system's core abstraction is WebContract, a common schema under which WebCompiler turns live webpages into addressable information and action objects; these are maintained in WebCache and handled through shared operations to provide consistent, operable, and persistent web access. The paper reports extensive experiments across web search, web navigation, and deep research benchmarks, though as an arXiv preprint it has not yet undergone peer review.

## [Local Diagnostics of Learned Event Representations for HL-LHC Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13437) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates supervised and self-supervised neural embedding strategies for anomaly detection on COLLIDE-2V, a 750-million-event dataset simulated under realistic High-Luminosity LHC conditions, using autoencoder reconstruction error in latent space as the anomaly score. It also introduces a local diagnostic pipeline that fits a Gaussian Mixture Model to the Standard Model latent space, profiles each component through high-level observables without process labels, localizes flagged events, and ranks observables by their local discriminating power. This work addresses a key weakness of model-agnostic anomaly detection at the LHC: while learned low-dimensional representations remove the need to commit to hand-crafted observables, they sacrifice immediate physical interpretability. By providing a way to locally diagnose flagged events, the method helps distinguish potential new physics from mismodeling or detector effects and identifies observables for targeted follow-up searches, which is essential for anomaly detection to enter a real discovery pipeline. Across the configurations and benchmark signals considered, supervised objectives that exploit simulation process labels during training generally outperform their self-supervised counterparts, and in their best configurations improve on an autoencoder trained directly on kinematic features, raising signal efficiency at 10% background acceptance on all held-out processes. The diagnostic ranking recovers the defining kinematics of held-out processes, and correlating the anomaly score with the same observables reveals cases where the most discriminating observable is not the one the score follows most closely.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: The High-Luminosity Large Hadron Collider (HL-LHC) is an upgrade to the LHC that will increase its integrated luminosity by a factor of 10 compared to the original design, greatly increasing the number of collisions and making rare new-physics events both more accessible and harder to find. Anomaly detection is a model-agnostic search strategy that flags collision events deviating from Standard Model expectations, often by training an autoencoder on known physics and using its reconstruction error as an anomaly score. COLLIDE-2V is a large-scale dataset of 750 million simulated LHC events designed for low-latency machine learning and foundation-model research in collider physics.

A new arXiv paper (2610.13438) introduces Rollout-Time Intervention (RTI), a method that uses reward-side evidence such as evaluation rubrics during rollout to convert erroneous trajectories into successful ones, providing learning signals for tasks the policy rarely solves. Experiments across five benchmarks show RTI consistently outperforms RL baselines and enables the post-trained policy to transcend its original capability boundary, with code released at github.com/Alibaba-NLP/qqr. This addresses the on-policy reasoning bottleneck that causes training collapse when LLM agents face tasks beyond their current capability, potentially expanding what agentic RL can achieve in reasoning, planning, and tool use. If validated, it could let smaller or weaker models approach the performance of substantially stronger contemporary LLMs. Intervention trajectories are inherently off-policy, but the authors find they can still guide policy optimization effectively when trajectories with excessive distributional shifts are filtered out. The abstract is truncated and does not report specific benchmark numbers or comparisons, so quantitative gains remain unclear.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning has become a key paradigm for improving LLM agents, but most agent RL methods are on-policy: they learn only from trajectories the current policy itself generates. When a task exceeds the model's capability, successful trajectories become extremely sparse, leaving little signal and causing training to collapse. RTI instead injects reward-side evidence (evaluation rubrics) during rollout to create successful trajectories, then filters them to keep learning stable.

## [SqueezeBench: A Self-Improving Lean Benchmark for Math AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13461) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SqueezeBench introduces a saturation-resilient benchmark where each accepted submission must prove a strictly better computable Lean bound on an integer function, verified by two independent Lean kernels. Ten problems from extremal combinatorics are released with twenty proved initial records, and frontier-model agents had 82 submissions accepted, improving every record. Traditional math benchmarks saturate once problems are solved, and open-problem benchmarks can only be resolved once, so SqueezeBench offers a self-improving evaluation that keeps raising the bar and could better track AI mathematical progress. It also shifts verification burden from human referees to machine-checked proofs, potentially enabling scalable, referee-free evaluation. Each submission must prove its function bounds f, is no worse than the current record at any n, and is strictly better at some n; the system rejects functions that compute f by exhaustive search at small n, and a maintainer checks for such search at larger n. For eight of ten problems, at least one record remains asymptotically behind the best published bound.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Lean is an interactive theorem prover based on dependent type theory that allows mathematical proofs to be machine-checked, and its Mathlib library supports formal verification. Benchmark saturation occurs when models score near the ceiling on a benchmark, making it unable to discriminate further progress, a known issue across LLM benchmarks like MMLU and GSM8K. SqueezeBench combines these ideas by keeping lower and upper records on integer functions and requiring Lean proofs that each new record improves the bound.

<a id="item-103"></a>
## [BranchOPD: Branching On-Policy Distillation for Efficient LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13481) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose BranchOPD, a method that selects informative decision points using student uncertainty and teacher-student disagreement, then resamples student-policy continuations from shared prefixes to apply token-level teacher supervision to both main trajectories and branches. It reduces student-generated token costs by approximately 19% on ALFWorld and 31% on WebShop compared to seven independent trajectories, and outperforms Vanilla OPD by 18.55 percentage points in mean mathematics accuracy (avg@16) and 13.8 points in mean ALFWorld ID/OOD success. This work addresses a key inefficiency in on-policy distillation—limited supervision coverage under a finite sampling budget—by branching at uncertain or disagreement points, which could make LLM training more token-efficient and effective for both reasoning and agent tasks. The demonstrated gains suggest that smarter sampling strategies can outperform simply increasing the number of rollouts. BranchOPD operates at token-level decisions in mathematics and within-turn decisions in agent tasks, and introduces protocol-boundary repair (PBR) to mitigate repetition and wasted generation under protocol constraints. Ablations show that branching at uncertain decisions expands subsequent behavior coverage and outperforms random selection and turn-initial branching.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on its own generated trajectories while receiving teacher supervision, but a single trajectory only realizes one continuation per decision, limiting coverage. Knowledge distillation typically involves a larger teacher model guiding a smaller student model, and on-policy variants aim to align training with the student's own distribution. ALFWorld and WebShop are benchmarks for text-based embodied agents and web navigation, respectively.

## [HMA: Multi-Agent Session Alternation Beats Single-Agent MLE Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13494) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13494) introduces Humanize MLE Agents (HMA), a workflow-level approach that alternates two agents on a shared workspace, caps each session at k experiments, and resets context between sessions. On 75 Kaggle tasks in MLE-bench, HMA achieves a state-of-the-art any-medal rate of 78.2%, beating the strongest open-source baseline by 8.0 points using only a quarter of its time budget. The work identifies two failure modes of single-session agents—long-horizon deceleration and harness staleness—and provides a theoretical framework (CR-SMDP) showing when multi-agent alternation can exceed single-agent limits. This could shift how LLM agent systems are designed for long-horizon engineering tasks, moving away from custom harnesses toward simpler, more general workflow-level alternation. The paper models alternating sessions as a context-reset semi-Markov decision process (CR-SMDP), derives sufficient conditions for alternation to outperform single-agent reference limits, and establishes an explicit experiment-budget bound. HMA requires no task-specific harness or prior knowledge supplements, and across six configurations spanning five heterogeneous agent pairs it improves any-medal rate by 6.4 points on average; a supplementary kernel optimization case reaches 878 cycles, within 1.6% of the community leaderboard leader.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon machine learning engineering (MLE) tasks require LLM agents to run many experiments over extended periods, and current approaches often use custom harnesses to manage tools, skills, and accumulated experience. A semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) extends the classic Markov decision process by accounting for the duration of time spent in each state, making it suitable for modeling sequential decisions with variable timing. This paper argues that as single-session execution lengthens, reasoning intensity declines and harness benefits fade as models improve, motivating a workflow-level multi-agent alternative.

## [PRISM: Retrieval-Augmented Multimodal Model for Clinical Prognosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13510) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PRISM (Population-Retrieved Inference for Structured Multimodal Prognosis), a multimodal language model that integrates structured measurements, ECG waveforms, longitudinal history, and population-level retrieval for clinical prognosis. On the 1,443-task MDS-ED benchmark, PRISM achieved AUROCs of 86.2% for diagnosis, 93.1% for deterioration, 91.8% for ICU admission, and 92.8% for mortality. This work shows that structure-aware multimodal integration and retrieval can substantially improve clinical prognosis predictions, potentially supporting earlier and more accurate risk stratification in hospital settings. It also demonstrates that supervised external adaptation can boost performance on new cohorts, which matters for deploying such models across different healthcare systems. PRISM uses per-feature numerical tokenization, recency-weighted aggregation of longitudinal ECGs, and a dual-modality population memory bank where retrieval operates in representation space, feeding mean-pooled embeddings of similar encounters rather than individual records into the prompt. Ablations show that replacing numerical EHR encoding with text serialization reduces AUROC by 3.66 points, removing pre-arrival history by 1.97 points, and zeroing retrieval by 0.99 points, though a paired task-level sign test does not establish a uniform retrieval advantage (p=0.067).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a technique that lets large language models pull relevant information from external data sources before generating a response, reducing hallucinations and avoiding costly retraining. Multimodal language models extend this by processing and reasoning across multiple data types such as text, images, and waveforms. AUROC (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve) is a standard metric for evaluating binary classifiers in clinical settings, where higher values indicate better discrimination between positive and negative cases.

## [Causal Effect Identifiability in Causal Abstractions: A Strict Hierarchy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13520) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formalizes several notions of causal effect identifiability directly at the level of causal abstractions, which are collections of compatible causal graphs, and organizes them into a strict hierarchy. It proves that a causal effect can be identified over a collection of graphs even when no single causal derivation holds uniformly across all compatible graphs. This resolves a known gap and ambiguity in the causal inference literature, where identifiability is usually defined for a single fully specified causal graph. It could influence future research on causal effect estimation under partial causal knowledge, which is common in complex or high-dimensional settings. The hierarchy clarifies relationships and differences among identifiability notions, and the key result shows that identification over a graph collection does not require a uniform causal derivation across all compatible graphs. The work is theoretical and does not provide empirical validation or algorithms for practical estimation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Causal effect identifiability asks whether a causal quantity, such as the average treatment effect, can be uniquely computed from observational data and assumptions encoded in a causal graph. Classical results assume a fully specified causal graph, which is often unrealistic. Causal abstractions address this by retaining only partial causal information, defining equivalence classes of compatible graphs, and this paper studies identifiability directly at that level.

## [MAGMA-Bench Tests Long-Horizon Multi-Robot Agents Under Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13534) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced MAGMA-Bench, an architecture-agnostic benchmark for persistent task reasoning in dynamic multi-robot environments, evaluating agents across increasing task horizons and controlled online perturbations such as execution failures, task interruptions, and mission updates. Alongside the benchmark, they released 261,646 task-level training examples and an architecture-agnostic expert-trace generation pipeline, and found that even the strongest evaluated models fail on a substantial fraction of clean episodes, with success rates dropping further under perturbations. This benchmark targets a gap where existing evaluations cover dynamic task execution and embodied multi-agent coordination separately but rarely their intersection, which matters as embodied agents are increasingly expected to operate over long horizons in real, failure-prone settings. The finding that frontier models still struggle under online perturbations suggests current planners are far from robust for real-world multi-robot deployment. MAGMA-Bench uses a structured skill-level interface that separates task-level reasoning from perception and low-level control, and its scenarios require coordination across robots with heterogeneous capabilities. The authors report that supervised adaptation greatly improves a small model, but performance remains substantially lower on longer and multi-robot missions.

**Background**: Long-horizon embodied agents are AI systems that control robots over extended sequences of actions, needing to retain task-relevant information, coordinate multiple robots, and adapt decisions as execution unfolds. Benchmarks are standardized test suites used to compare system performance, and an architecture-agnostic benchmark is designed so that different agent designs can be evaluated under the same conditions. Prior work such as LLaMAR has explored long-horizon planning for multi-agent robots in dynamic, partially observable environments, but evaluations combining dynamic execution with multi-robot coordination and controlled failures have been limited.

## [New Framework Quantifies Overthinking in Reasoning Models via Pareto Frontiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13570) ⭐️ 7.0/10

An arXiv paper (2610.13570) introduces Step-wise Truncation Probing (STP), which repeatedly generates answers from multiple retained prefixes along a fixed sampled reasoning trajectory to characterize overthinking at the model-task level. It defines two new metrics, Overthinking Ratio and Accuracy Degradation, and unifies dynamic routing, fixed budgets, and dynamic early exit into a single framework for constructing token-accuracy Pareto frontiers of termination strategies. Overthinking is a growing problem in test-time scaling for large reasoning models, where extra thinking tokens can waste compute or even reduce accuracy. By providing a unified way to measure overthinking severity and the theoretical mitigation potential of termination strategies, this work could guide more efficient reasoning deployment and inform future early-exit research. The framework uses each trajectory's earliest measured position attaining maximum empirical accuracy as a reference, defining Overthinking Ratio as the fraction of thinking tokens generated afterward and Accuracy Degradation as the accuracy drop from this reference to full thinking. The authors also developed OTBench, a reusable benchmark built from the STP process that produced 70.5B reasoning tokens and corresponding probing measurements across six models and four benchmark groups.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Reasoning models such as those using chain-of-thought can improve accuracy through test-time scaling, but generating more thinking tokens is not always beneficial. Overthinking refers to continued reasoning that becomes ineffective or harmful, wasting computation without improving answers. A Pareto frontier, borrowed from multi-objective optimization, represents the set of optimal trade-offs between two competing objectives, here token usage and accuracy.

A new arXiv paper (2610.13594) introduces MENTOR, a multi-agent framework that performs on-policy pedagogical intervention to recover from zero-reward collapse in Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards (RLVR). MENTOR diagnoses the root cause of failure in real time and dynamically generates targeted instructional prompts to guide the learning agent's next attempt, achieving up to 78.3% average relative gains on Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct over its base model. Zero-reward collapse is a critical failure mode in RLVR training of large reasoning models, where a sudden increase in task difficulty causes the learning signal to vanish. MENTOR's approach of leveraging failed trajectories for adaptive guidance could significantly improve the stability and efficiency of RL training pipelines, affecting researchers and practitioners working on large reasoning models. MENTOR is a plug-and-play module that integrates seamlessly into existing policy optimization pipelines like GRPO without modifying the training infrastructure. Extensive experiments on math and code generation show it effectively mitigates zero-reward collapse and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, though the abstract is truncated and full details are not yet available.

## [HOA: Multi-Session LLM Agent Framework for Olympiad Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13632) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced HOA (Humanize Olympiads Agent), an open flow-level framework that coordinates multiple LLM agent sessions through three feedback channels: an independent reviewer, a structured wiki, and a Lean proof verifier. With GPT-5.6 Sol, HOA scores perfectly on IMO, IPhO, and IOI 2026, solves all 672 PutnamBench problems, and ranks first on Lean-Eval with 251/304 (82.6%); with the open Kimi-K3 model it also achieves full IMO/IPhO/IOI scores and 97.1% on IChO. The work shows that systematic multi-session feedback can substantially close the gap between open and closed base models, since Kimi-K3 with HOA matched or exceeded the closed GPT-5.6 Sol + /Goal baseline on several competitions. It also releases an open framework at a time when leading systems for long-horizon scientific and olympiad tasks keep their models and inference stacks private. HOA's three channels address distinct failure modes: an independent reviewer catches early errors, a structured wiki supplies task-specific knowledge missing or unreliable in pretraining, and a verifier checks generated Lean proofs against trusted challenge statements to prevent misinterpretation or weakening of the original task. The abstract is truncated and reports no per-channel ablations, so the individual contribution of review, wiki, and verification is not yet quantified.

A new arXiv paper (2610.13663) introduces a multi-agent LLM framework with analyst, coder, and recorder roles that automatically tunes key components of specialized neural network models. Validated on spiking neural networks and physics-informed neural networks across five tasks, it achieved up to 13 percentage points higher classification accuracy and reduced solution error by roughly 2491-fold. This work shows that LLM agents can automate the manual, expert-driven selection of critical components in specialized neural networks, potentially accelerating hyperparameter optimization and neural architecture search. The transferable framework could lower the barrier for non-experts and speed up scientific machine learning workflows. Unlike systems that optimize entire ML scripts or search architectures in a discrete space, this framework operates over an open but semantically narrow space, generating code only for specified components while leaving the overall network structure unchanged. It includes an experiment log and a sandboxed execution environment, and was tested on MNIST/Digits spiking classification plus boundary-value problems for the generalized nonlinear Schrödinger equation (GNLSE) and Kuramoto–Sivashinsky (KS) equations.

## [Adaptive DHiSparse Combines Speculative Decoding with Load-Adaptive Hierarchical KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13708) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13708) introduces Adaptive DHiSparse, which enables speculative decoding over a hierarchical sparse KV cache that adapts residency to load. At 100K context and concurrency 128, GLM-5.3 with DFlash2 reaches 793 output tokens/s per decode GPU — 1.45x HiSparse and 1.27x non-speculative adaptive serving — while on DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash at 500K context and concurrency 512 it delivers 1.85x the output of resident serving with DSpark. Long-context LLM serving typically exhausts KV memory before hardware throughput is reached, so this work directly targets a real bottleneck in production inference. By combining speculative decoding with a load-adaptive hierarchical KV cache, it shows substantial throughput and concurrency gains that could improve the cost-efficiency of serving long-context models. Adaptive DHiSparse protects the required working set, retains extra history when GPU memory is spare, and recovers the rest via exact fused retrieval; DSpark and DFlash2 keep their proposal and acceptance rules, with queries sharing missing-row retrieval, draft KV using a window-bounded ring, and verification staging reused across layer groups. Under the same latency bounds as HiSparse it sustains up to 4x the concurrency and 2.3–3.7x the output, and optional residency cuts host fetches from 14.2% to 1.4% of selected rows at low load.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive LLM inference by having a smaller draft model propose multiple tokens that a larger target model verifies in a single forward pass. KV cache stores the key/value state tensors generated for every token at every transformer layer, and it grows with context length, making memory the key constraint in long-context serving. Hierarchical KV caching moves history to host or remote storage and fetches only selected entries, while sparse attention restricts each query to a small subset of important KV entries. HiSparse is a prior system that stores history in host memory but uses a single cache budget at every load and does not support draft-model speculative decoding.

<a id="item-114"></a>
## [Scoring Teachers Can't Localize Errors, Critique-Conditioned Self-Distillation Can](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13744) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13744) shows that on-policy self-distillation with a scoring teacher fails on two key properties: it neither localizes errors in student rollouts nor pushes probability away from erroneous tokens, and it cannot reliably distinguish correct from incorrect rollouts within the same problem. The authors argue this failure is structural—error localization is a search problem that a scoring teacher is never asked to solve—and propose critique-conditioned self-distillation, where a critique is first generated and then used to condition the scoring teacher. This critique matters because on-policy self-distillation has been promoted as a way to replace sparse outcome rewards in verifiable-reward RL with dense token-level credit, a core problem in credit assignment for LLM reasoning. If scoring-based teachers structurally cannot provide corrective credit, then a large body of work on self-distillation and GRPO-style training may need to be rethought, and the proposed critique-conditioned variant offers a concrete alternative. The authors test the two signals consumed by self-distillation objectives—the full-distribution KL divergence and the token log-ratio—against gold error spans in student rollouts, and find neither localizes errors nor pushes probability away from erroneous tokens. The critique-conditioned signal not only localizes errors and is corrective within error spans, but also shifts probability toward backtracking tokens such as "Wait", "But", and "Actually"; the critique further helps at test time by resuming wrong rollouts and in cold-start GRPO by supplying an advantage signal where verifiable rewards alone give none.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy self-distillation trains a student model on its own rollouts while a teacher—often the same model with access to privileged information such as the reference solution—provides token-level supervision, typically via KL divergence or log-ratio objectives. This is meant to solve the credit assignment problem in reinforcement learning, where a single outcome reward must be attributed to many intermediate reasoning steps. The paper's key insight is that scoring a rollout and searching for the error within it are different computational tasks, so a teacher that only scores cannot perform the search needed for corrective credit.

## [PAGE Framework Uses AI to Analyze Thousands of Comics at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13840) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes PAGE, an AI framework that parses comic scans into panels, text, and bibliographic records, annotates each page with a vision-language model following a written codebook, grounds samples in human annotation, and explains change across an archive. The authors demonstrate it on 3,929 American comic books from 1938–1956 and 87 volumes of Japanese manga across three studies. This work shows how AI can transform comics scholarship from one-book-at-a-time close reading into large-scale computational analysis, potentially benefiting digital humanities and multimodal AI research. The same instruments could make archives searchable by visual content, open comics to blind and low-vision readers, and help preserve fragile collections and credit uncredited artists. The framework combines panel and text parsing, vision-language model annotation guided by a codebook, human annotation grounding, and statistical controls for publisher and genre when estimating change. The three studies respectively pair text search with visual reading to recover portrayals of political power, measure visual form across an industry to identify individual artists' hands, and track who speaks across two comics traditions against human annotation.

**Background**: Comics are a major visual record of the twentieth century, but most of this vast archive has never been studied as a whole because comics scholarship traditionally proceeds one book at a time. Vision-language models are AI systems that jointly interpret images and text, extending text-only large language models; prior computational comics work has focused on lower-level tasks like panel detection and character analysis, often limited by small datasets.

## [X-AgentRed: Self-Evolving Red-Teaming for Personal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13886) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced X-AgentRed, a self-evolving red-teaming framework that discovers harmful tool call paths in personal agents by building a directed graph of tool dependencies and refining it with execution feedback. Across four frontier agentic models, it achieved 1.77x the harmful path rate of STAC and 2.99x the tool coverage on average. As personal agents connect to more web and app services, benign user requests can combine into unintended harmful outcomes across services, and existing red-teaming methods miss these compositional failures because LLM generation is biased toward familiar tool combinations. X-AgentRed offers a more systematic way to proactively surface novel cross-service risks before deployment. The framework analyzes each tool's effects, potential harms, and execution preconditions to build the dependency graph, then samples recipes that are instantiated as realistic scenarios; execution feedback drives self-improvement via tool substitution and precondition refinement at blocked steps. The abstract is truncated, so full evaluation details and limitations are not yet available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Red-teaming AI agents means testing them as complete runtime systems rather than isolated model endpoints, simulating an intelligent adversary with goals and creativity. A tool dependency graph is a directed graph that models tools, APIs, and their prerequisite relations, where one tool's output can satisfy another's execution preconditions. Compositional failures arise when individually safe agents or tools produce misaligned or harmful outcomes once combined in multi-service workflows.

## [RATED: Reliability-Aware Tool Retrieval for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13889) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose RATED, a tool retrieval framework that combines a frozen dual-encoder with contrastive fine-tuning for semantic matching and a lightweight contextual bandit module that re-ranks candidates using real-time execution feedback. It achieves a 52.2% relative MRR improvement over the ToolLLM API retriever and cuts fault selection rate from 86.2% to 2.9% under a 30% API failure scenario, while adding less than 1 ms of latency. Existing tool retrieval methods optimize only semantic relevance and ignore whether a tool is actually available at execution time, which can cause AI agents to repeatedly select broken or failing APIs. By adapting to runtime failures without retraining, RATED points toward more reliable agent behavior in production settings where external APIs are frequently unstable. The framework introduces EcoAgent, a benchmark of 5,172 real-world tools and over 210,000 query-tool pairs for offline retrieval and dynamic reliability evaluation, with code and data released on GitHub. The contextual bandit design enables rapid adaptation to tool failures at negligible latency cost, though the work remains focused on the niche of tool retrieval for agents.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents often need to select the right external API or tool from thousands of candidates before executing a task, a step known as tool retrieval. Most prior work, such as the ToolLLM/ToolBench line of research, trains retrievers to match a query to a tool by semantic similarity alone. A contextual bandit is a reinforcement learning technique that balances exploration and exploitation by choosing actions based on contextual features and observed rewards, which here are execution outcomes such as success or failure.

A new arXiv paper (2610.13948) uses the Wikipedia Game—navigating from one article to a target article using only internal links—to benchmark sentence embedding models and LLMs across five Wikipedia languages and five academic-literature datasets. ## [IG-VLA Internalizes Future Imagination into a Scene Gist Token for Efficient Robot Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02626) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose IG-VLA, a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) reasoning framework that learns to imagine task-relevant future scene evolution directly in latent visual representation space, avoiding costly pixel-level video generation. A Scene Gist Memory then internalizes these reasoning-derived scene-behavior associations into a compact Scene Gist Token, so inference can bypass explicit future imagination entirely. Explicit future rollouts at every inference step are a major computational bottleneck for VLA models, so internalizing spatiotemporal reasoning could make embodied AI deployment far more practical. On the LIBERO-Plus Language suite the gist policy beats the strongest baseline by nearly 6% in success rate while achieving up to 6.38x speedup, cutting latency from 1081ms to 169.5ms per action chunk on a single NVIDIA A6000 GPU. The Latent Spatiotemporal Reasoning module guides action prediction without generating pixels, and the Scene Gist Token captures general scene context and action patterns rather than preserving the full future reasoning trajectory. Experiments span LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and VLABench, though the work is still an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion.

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models take images and a language instruction, often with proprioception, and output robot commands, reducing reliance on hand-designed perception-to-planning pipelines. Recent VLA research adds intermediate reasoning to improve manipulation, but most approaches reason only about observed states rather than anticipating future scene evolution. Gist tokens, originally introduced for compressing LLM prompts into reusable cached representations, provide the conceptual basis for the compact Scene Gist Token used here.

## [Spiking Neural Networks Cut Beam Prediction Energy by 10x](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12507) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12507) develops spiking neural network (SNN) architectures for sensing-aided beam prediction in millimeter-wave (mmWave) systems and systematically compares them against conventional artificial neural networks (ANNs) across position-only, vision-only, and multimodal inputs. The multimodal SNN reaches 58.08% top-1 accuracy, beating the closest-energy ANN by 18.11 percentage points, and SNNs require up to an order of magnitude less energy than ANNs at comparable accuracy for vision and multimodal sensing. Beam training overhead is a major bottleneck in mmWave and emerging 6G networks, and sensing-aided prediction pipelines that rely on vision or multimodal data can be energy-intensive at the edge. Demonstrating that SNNs can match ANN accuracy with up to 10x less energy suggests a practical path toward low-power, always-on beam management for mobile and edge devices. The study finds that compact ANNs consistently outperform SNNs when only position information is available, and that SNN accuracy is limited by the binary (spike-based) communication between layers rather than by model size. This highlights a fundamental accuracy ceiling for SNNs in this task, even as their energy advantages hold for richer sensing modalities.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Millimeter-wave (mmWave) systems use narrow directional beams to achieve high beamforming gain, but finding the best beam through exhaustive training scales linearly with antenna array size and creates significant overhead and latency. Sensing-aided beam prediction addresses this by using environmental data such as camera images, LiDAR, or user position to predict the optimal beam index directly, avoiding exhaustive search. Spiking neural networks (SNNs) are biologically inspired models that transmit information as discrete spikes only when a neuron's membrane potential crosses a threshold, which can make them far more energy-efficient than conventional ANNs on neuromorphic hardware.

## [AutoIndexer Enables Flexible-Order Decoding via Chain of Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12545) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from Autodesk AI Lab introduced AutoIndexer, a generative model trained and decoded via a Chain of Edits that lets it insert, substitute, and delete tokens in its own prior outputs. Training sequences are built by perturbing ground-truth labels with inverse chained edit operations, and the model learns to undo those inverses using marker tokens that open and close edits while moving a cursor through the existing sequence. Autoregressive LLMs cannot revise earlier outputs, and diffusion LLMs still cannot flexibly insert or remove arbitrary numbers of tokens from a pre-generated context, which limits tasks with cross-references or future dependencies. AutoIndexer offers a post-generation context-editing capability that most autoregressive and diffusion models lack, potentially influencing future research on non-causal generation and code correction. The model predicts marker tokens for opening and closing edits while simultaneously moving a cursor to positions in the existing token sequence, analogous to a text editor. Perturbation training makes AutoIndexer robust to erroneous context and adaptable to editing tasks such as code correction, and the authors report it does not compromise performance on standard LLM benchmarks; code is available at https://github.com/AutodeskAILab/autoindexer.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Autoregressive language models generate tokens left-to-right and cannot revise earlier outputs, which is problematic for data with non-causal ordering such as cross-references or future dependencies. Diffusion LLMs use bidirectional attention to partially address this, but their mask-unmask paradigm still cannot flexibly insert or delete arbitrary numbers of tokens from a pre-generated context. AutoIndexer instead simulates a Chain of Edits, treating generation like editing a document with a cursor.

## [Language Models Can Guide Their Own Preservation via Task Banks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12602) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12602) introduces the concept of 'self-preservation' in continual learning, where language models help select and combine task descriptions from Task Banks to generate replay data that protects their own prior capabilities. Experiments with Qwen models of varying sizes show that the new task and learning rate determine which capabilities degrade, and that generated replay reduces forgetting on tasks outside the bank, with partial-bank combinations improving mean retention of previously correct answers. This work matters because it reframes continual learning as a self-directed process, where the model itself helps decide what to preserve rather than relying solely on a fixed set of provided tasks. It also highlights that average accuracy can mask specific capability losses, a caveat that could affect how practitioners evaluate fine-tuned models in deployment. The study uses a frozen model to generate replay guided by Task Banks, and finds that selection informed by the new task improves mean held-out retention over random selection at 4B parameters, though smaller models show no such advantage. Under a fixed example budget, replacing new-task examples with replay improves preservation but reduces new-task gains, revealing a trade-off.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Continual learning aims to let pretrained models acquire new tasks without forgetting old ones, a challenge known as catastrophic forgetting. Replay methods mitigate this by revisiting past data or generating pseudo-samples, but the tasks chosen for preservation typically cover only part of what a model knows. This paper explores whether models can supplement that limited evidence by generating their own replay and even choosing which tasks to replay.

The attack relies on invisible tokens that embed hidden stages into the model without appearing in the user's visible request, while a stage controller tracks the attack's progression during inference to realize gradual activation. ## [Kronecker Product Attention Cuts KV Cache Memory by Up to 10x](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12657) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.12657) introduces Kronecker Product Attention (KPA), which represents key and value activations as sums of Kronecker products of compact, token-dependent matrix factors. KPA strictly generalizes Tensor Product Attention (TPA) and achieves up to 10x KV cache compression while matching or exceeding TPA, MHA, MQA, GQA, and MLA across four model scales and nine benchmarks under identical training conditions. KV cache memory is a major bottleneck for efficient autoregressive inference in large language models, growing linearly with sequence length, batch size, and model depth. KPA offers a tunable compression-accuracy trade-off without architectural changes, potentially enabling longer contexts and cheaper deployment for transformer-based models. KPA uses a structured rotary embedding operator matched to the Kronecker representation, preserving RoPE's relative-position property while rotating only the compact factors, thus eliminating the need for a decoupled RoPE branch as in MLA. The Kronecker head split is a tuneable hyperparameter that lets practitioners navigate the compression-accuracy trade-off at design time.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: The KV cache stores key and value activations during autoregressive generation to avoid recomputation, but its memory footprint grows linearly with sequence length, batch size, and model depth. Existing methods like Multi-Query Attention (MQA), Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA), and Tensor Product Attention (TPA) reduce this cost through various trade-offs, often sacrificing expressivity or requiring additional positional-encoding machinery. The Kronecker product is a mathematical operation that combines two matrices into a larger block matrix, enabling structured factorization.

## [ReHarness Enables Backward Revision for Agentic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12668) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12668) proposes ReHarness, a recurrent harness framework that adds backward revision to the forward read/write flow of language model agent harnesses under a strictly online streaming protocol. After each instance, the resulting trajectory is used as feedback to revise the reflections retrieved for that instance, propagating feedback backward through the textual harness state. Existing agent harnesses typically evolve only through forward information flow or require extra replay or validation data, which limits online adaptation. ReHarness shows that backward revision of textual states can improve both stability and plasticity, offering a practical path for LLM agents to keep learning over long interaction streams without offline data. The method was evaluated across three continual learning benchmarks and three language model backbones, consistently outperforming raw-context baselines, memory- and reflection-based methods, and prompt optimization approaches. Analysis indicates the revisions are source-grounded and meaningful, correcting overgeneralized or outdated experience under distribution shifts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: An agent harness is the software scaffolding around a language model that manages state, memory, and tool use, and it increasingly determines agent reliability in production. Continual learning for agents can occur at the model, harness, or context layer, and the stability-plasticity dilemma—retaining old knowledge while adapting to new information—is a central challenge. ReHarness targets the harness layer by treating textual reflections as revisable state rather than fixed history.

<a id="item-127"></a>
## [KITE Injects Semantic Priors into Time-Series Event Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12738) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces KITE, a knowledge-injected temporal encoder that injects human-held 'semantic priors' into a self-supervised event forecaster through two switchable mechanisms — a covariate mechanism and an attention-bias mechanism — each of which is the identity at its null, so a single architecture serves every prior type without per-dataset architecture search. Experiments show an informative prior beats no prior in-domain (a periodicity clock gives +14% pooled h-AUROC, p<0.001; a conservation residual gives +47% at a 0.25 label fraction) and transfers out-of-domain (+53%), while priors the observed window already recovers stay inert. Rare, high-cost events in multivariate time-series systems are hard to forecast because their precursors are seldom observed, leaving purely data-driven models little to learn from; KITE offers a switchable, attributable route to contextual conditioning that could improve early warning in domains like industrial monitoring, energy grids, and finance. The authors also position it as a substrate for scaling toward cross-domain, pretrained time-series foundation world models that carry their governing structure explicitly. A prior only helps when it supplies information the observed window does not already contain: in a short context an informative prior beats no prior, but priors the window already recovers stay inert, and the attention-bias mechanism shows no measurable gain in the reported experiments. The paper reports pooled h-AUROC improvements with statistical significance (p<0.001) and notes that a working prior can substitute for feature transfer in out-of-domain settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multivariate time-series forecasting models, often built on transformer-style temporal encoders, learn patterns from historical windows but struggle when target events are rare and their precursors are rarely observed. A 'semantic prior' here means pre-existing human knowledge about the system — such as a periodicity clock or a conservation residual — that is available before an event occurs but absent from the scarce labels. Self-supervised learning lets such models train on unlabeled sequences, and KITE's contribution is a way to inject these priors into that pipeline without redesigning the architecture per dataset.

## [WZDrive Dataset and Model Target Trajectory Prediction in Work Zones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12747) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced WZDrive-Data, a multimodal dataset of 1,183 work-zone driving segments collected from 33 drivers using a human-in-the-loop driving simulator with prerecorded worker animations and VR-controlled workers. Their proposed multimodal ego-trajectory predictor, WZDrive, achieves a mean minADE (K=6) of 1.09 m and minFDE (K=6) of 2.12 m, reducing these metrics by 7.18% and 13.18% respectively compared with the best state-of-the-art baselines. Road construction zones involve temporary lane layout changes and interactions with workers, making them safety-critical yet understudied for autonomous driving. This work provides a dedicated dataset and model that could improve prediction robustness in these challenging environments, benefiting autonomous vehicle developers and road safety researchers. The dataset captures driver control and gaze signals from human participants, while vehicle trajectories and scene information come from the simulator, enabling multimodal learning from human driving signals. The evaluation uses minADE and minFDE with K=6, which measure the best-of-six predicted trajectories against ground truth in average and final displacement error.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Ego-trajectory prediction estimates the future path of the autonomous vehicle itself, a core task for safe planning. Work zones are difficult because lane markings and connectivity change temporarily, and human-in-the-loop simulators let real drivers interact with virtual scenarios to collect realistic behavioral data. Metrics like minADE and minFDE are standard in multimodal trajectory prediction, where a model outputs multiple plausible futures and is scored on its best match.

<a id="item-129"></a>
## [SemViews Certifies Reuse of LLM Labels Across Semantic Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12768) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present SemViews, a certified semantic filter that reuses labels left behind by earlier queries (called semantic views) to order tuples, then certifies its answer with fresh stratified oracle samples. On four workloads replayed against 725,000 recorded oracle labels, it meets both precision and recall targets on 99.63% of queries while saving 28%-45% of oracle calls, and cuts calls by a further 27%-36% when a question returns in different words. Semantic query engines pay a costly LLM oracle call for every natural-language filter evaluation, so reducing those calls without losing accuracy is a central cost-efficiency problem for LLM-based query processing. SemViews offers probabilistic precision and recall guarantees for every workload and catalog, meaning a stale or misleading view can cost extra oracle calls but never accuracy, which could influence how future semantic query optimizers are designed. The method orders tuples using signals that cost no oracle calls — prior queries' labels, a local embedding, and a helper model's score — then refines that order with labels it must pay for anyway and certifies the result with fresh stratified oracle samples. The only prior method in the study with the same guarantee needs 1.32x-1.71x as many calls, while similarity caches and entailment-based reuse meet the targets on only 17%-47% of reworded queries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Semantic query engines evaluate natural-language filters by calling a large language model, and each such call is treated as a costly oracle. Existing optimizers face a trade-off: cheap proxy cascades miss precision and recall targets, methods with finite-sample guarantees save little, and all of them discard the oracle's labels once a query ends. SemViews instead treats those leftover labels as reusable 'semantic views' and adds a statistical certification step based on stratified sampling, a technique that splits a population into homogeneous subgroups before sampling to improve estimate reliability.

## [REDaCT: Collapse-Free Unlearning for Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12769) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose REDaCT (REgenerate via Detection and Corrective Tuning), an inference-time unlearning framework for large reasoning models (LRMs) that combines a binary leak detector with an offline-learned corrective embedding. When injected at the first detected leak during inference, the embedding steers subsequent reasoning away from forget-set information, achieving perfect answer forgetting and near-perfect chain-of-thought forgetting without triggering reasoning collapse. Existing unlearning methods for LRMs remove forget-set information at the cost of reasoning collapse, where the model can no longer produce a coherent chain-of-thought or terminate its reasoning properly. REDaCT shows that forgetting and reasoning ability can be preserved simultaneously, which matters for AI safety, privacy compliance, and model editing in reasoning-centric systems. REDaCT operates at inference time rather than modifying model weights, using a binary detector to flag leaking sentences in the reasoning trace and a corrective embedding learned offline to redirect generation. The authors report that it substantially improves reasoning preservation over state-of-the-art unlearning baselines while maintaining perfect answer forgetting and near-perfect CoT forgetting, with code released at github.com/jmaccoby/redact.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Machine unlearning aims to remove specific information from a trained model without full retraining, which is important for privacy regulations and compliance. Large reasoning models generate an explicit chain-of-thought before answering, so unlearning must erase forget-set content from both the reasoning trace and the final answer. Inference-time unlearning avoids weight updates by using auxiliary detectors or verifiers to suppress targeted information at runtime, but applying this to reasoning models risks breaking the CoT itself.

## [RL-Based NAS Cuts Audio-Visual Speech Enhancement Model Size 9x](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12796) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces an RL-driven neural architecture search (RL-NAS) framework that discovers compact audio-visual speech enhancement (AVSE) models. Using proximal policy optimisation with constraint-aware action masking, it evaluates only 35 candidates and finds a 2.9M-parameter model achieving 14.5 dB SI-SNR improvement, just 0.7 dB below a 22M-parameter baseline, while running 4.3x faster on mobile CPUs. This work shows that reinforcement learning-guided architecture search can make multimodal speech enhancement practical for resource-constrained edge devices such as smartphones and hearables. The 9x size reduction with minimal performance loss could accelerate on-device deployment of AVSE, where memory and compute budgets are tight. The search space preserves temporal alignment between audio and visual streams, and the reward function jointly optimises SI-SNR improvement and model compactness. Architectural analysis reveals that shallower but wider temporal convolutional blocks and aggressive visual pathway pruning maintain enhancement quality while cutting computation; RL-NAS also achieves 92% higher search efficiency than random search.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Audio-visual speech enhancement (AVSE) combines audio with visual cues such as lip movements to improve speech intelligibility in noisy environments, but state-of-the-art models are often too large for edge devices. Neural architecture search (NAS) automates the design of neural networks, and reinforcement learning is one common search strategy. SI-SNR (scale-invariant signal-to-noise ratio) is a standard metric for speech enhancement and separation that measures signal fidelity while discounting global rescaling.

## [Can Generative Models Act as Editable Simulators?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12847) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12847) investigates the conditions under which a generative model can act as a simulator by learning a mapping between its latent representation and an explicit simulator state, so that editing the latent produces the same next-step outputs as editing the simulator. The authors test two mapping strategies — training probes to decode and steer along simulator state, and learning a direct inverse map — in two contrasting environments: the discrete board game Othello and Rayworld, a continuous world of moving discs seen through partial renderings. If generative models can be reliably driven by an external simulator state, they could become controllable, editable world models rather than opaque black boxes, which matters for model-based reinforcement learning, planning, and interactive content generation. The finding that a model can be driven by a simulator it may never have learned also suggests a path toward reusing pretrained generative models as general-purpose simulators. The paper reports that which mapping succeeds depends strongly on the environment even though the simulator state is decodable in every variant: in Othello, editability degrades smoothly as the game is simplified and even small rule changes have large consequences, while in Rayworld probes fail to steer along the continuous state yet an inverse map can still set the model from that state. This indicates that decodability of a state does not guarantee steerability, and that the inverse-map approach is more robust in continuous settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: A simulator represents an environment as an explicit state that users can read and modify, whereas generative models learn implicit representations of the world without any built-in way to edit that state. Latent representations are the internal, compressed encodings that generative models learn, and prior work on probing and steering has shown that such latents can sometimes be decoded or manipulated to control model behavior. This paper asks whether that latent space can be aligned with a simulator's state space, effectively turning a generative model into an editable simulator.
