**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer neural networks by identifying which weights, attention heads, and neurons produce particular behaviors, often using causal interventions such as ablations or activation edits. Attention heads are components that route information between token positions, and prior work has found that models reuse the same heads across many tasks. Open-weight models are those whose trained parameters are publicly released, allowing researchers to run such internal experiments directly.

## [Quantile Loss Beats MSE in Tabular Regression, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13909) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13909) systematically re-evaluates regression objectives across many established tabular datasets and multiple neural backbones, finding that most proposed alternatives to MSE give limited or inconsistent gains, while quantile loss yields consistent aggregate improvements. The authors also show that regions with different target noise levels reach optimal performance at different training epochs, meaning a single global early-stopping checkpoint forces a compromise, and they propose a heteroskedasticity-aware quantile loss extension using input-dependent label smoothing as a remedy. This work gives practitioners a modern, apples-to-apples empirical picture of regression objectives for tabular deep learning, an area where the choice of loss function has been understudied compared to architecture design. The finding that global early stopping can be fundamentally limited when target noise is heterogeneous has implications beyond tabular data, since early stopping is a near-universal regularization technique in deep learning. The comparison spans a broad collection of established tabular datasets and multiple neural backbones, and the code is released at https://github.com/yandex-research/tabular-regression. The proposed heteroskedasticity-aware extension improves over standard quantile loss on several backbones, though the authors frame it as a potential remedy rather than a complete solution to the stopping-time mismatch.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Tabular deep learning applies neural networks to structured data organized in rows and columns, a setting where deep models have historically struggled to clearly outperform gradient-boosted trees. Mean Squared Error (MSE) is the default regression objective, but it implicitly assumes constant noise variance, whereas quantile loss estimates conditional quantiles and can better represent heteroskedastic (noise-varying) target distributions. Early stopping halts training when validation performance stops improving to avoid overfitting, and it is normally applied globally with a single checkpoint for the whole model.

## [Hide and Seek: Concept Attribution for Black-Box Vision Models Without Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13935) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Hide and Seek (HnS), a concept-level attribution method that uses a vision-language model to retrieve concept-aligned images, localizes the concept within each image, masks that region, and measures attribution from the change in the black-box model's output. It requires neither model weights nor labeled concept datasets, and was validated on CelebA and COCO-Stuff, showing better agreement with ground-truth attributions than existing methods. Existing concept-based attribution methods are limited to classification and regression models and typically require access to model weights or large concept-annotated datasets, which is impractical for proprietary black-box APIs. HnS removes these requirements and extends concept attribution to tasks such as image captioning and satellite image segmentation, where no existing concept attribution method can be applied. HnS performs faithful attribution by directly measuring the black-box model's behavior rather than relying on gradients or internal representations, and it supports local (image-level) attributions. The code is provided anonymously in the supplementary material, and the work is an arXiv preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Concept-based attribution explains model predictions using human-understandable concepts (e.g., "smiling" or "striped") instead of raw pixels or abstract features. Black-box vision models expose only their outputs, hiding architecture, parameters, gradients, and training data, which makes auditing, bias detection, and failure analysis difficult. Vision-language models (VLMs) can connect images and text, enabling retrieval and localization of concepts without task-specific training. Masking-based perturbation, where part of an input is hidden to observe output changes, is a common technique for measuring how much a region influences a model's prediction.

## [DeltaLLM Accelerates Edge LLM Prefilling via Delta-Key Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19608) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DeltaLLM introduces a training-free, algorithm-hardware-dataflow co-designed framework that accelerates prefilling attention in edge LLMs. It achieves up to 60% Delta-Key sparsity and 63% structured attention-score sparsity with negligible accuracy loss on BitNet-b1.58-2B-4T and LLaMA3.2-1B-Instruct, delivering a 1.62x speedup over TeLLMe-v2 on the KV260 FPGA board while reducing DSP and BRAM usage. This work addresses the critical bottleneck of attention during prefilling for on-device LLM inference, where long prompts and tight resource constraints make existing GPU-oriented pruning methods unsuitable. By enabling efficient edge deployment without fine-tuning, DeltaLLM could significantly improve the practicality of running compact LLMs on edge platforms, benefiting applications that require local, low-latency inference. The framework uses a causal-attention-aware K-only delta formulation that enables Delta-Key reuse across query heads under Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), and a context-aware hybrid strategy that preserves exact attention near the diagonal while applying delta approximation to nonlocal regions. A vectorized structured sparse online softmax (VSSOS) converts fine-grained spatial score sparsity into hardware-friendly column-level skipping, supported by a specialized vector-processor architecture and unified sparse/dense attention dataflow.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: On-device LLM inference is increasingly important for edge platforms, but high computational and memory demands make it challenging. Prefilling attention, which processes the entire input prompt at once, becomes a dominant bottleneck for long prompts. Existing attention pruning methods mainly target highly parallel hardware like GPUs and long context windows, limiting their suitability for edge scenarios. Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) is a technique that groups multiple query heads to share key and value projections, reducing memory and compute costs.

## [QXR Framework Repairs Global Inconsistencies in Natural-Language Claim Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13600) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2601.13600v2) formalizes the problem of global consistency repair for natural-language claim sets and introduces QXR, an adaptive diagnosis-and-repair framework that uses an LLM as a noisy set-level consistency judge to localize minimal conflicting subsets and delete a small hitting set of claims. Evaluated across LLM judges and benchmarks derived from VitaminC, FEVER, and conflicting-evidence RAG, QXR preserves substantially more valid claims than direct all-at-once judging while maintaining high precision. This work addresses a fundamental failure mode in NLP systems that aggregate claims from retrieved passages, documents, and generated reasoning steps: claims can be locally plausible and pairwise compatible while still being jointly inconsistent. It suggests that factuality in claim aggregation systems should be treated as a set-level repair problem rather than only local verification, which could influence how RAG pipelines and fact-checking tools are designed. The paper formalizes global consistency repair as preserving the largest coherent subset while localizing compact sources of inconsistency, and shows that pairwise checks are insufficient for global consistency. QXR treats the LLM as a noisy subset-consistency oracle, and the problem is related to the hitting set problem, with the paper providing a complexity landscape and an objective equivalence reduction to hitting set.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Factuality methods in NLP typically verify claims individually or compare pairs of claims, which misses joint inconsistencies that only appear when the entire aggregated claim set is considered. Global consistency repair is the task of taking an inconsistent set of natural-language claims and finding the largest coherent subset to keep, while identifying the minimal set of claims to remove. QXR is an adaptive diagnosis-and-repair framework that uses an LLM as a noisy set-level consistency judge to localize minimal conflicting subsets and then deletes a small hitting set of claims to restore consistency.

## [CoMMa: Contribution-Aware Multi-Agent LLM Framework for Decentralized Oncology Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09159) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CoMMa (Contribution-Aware Medical Multi-Agents), a decentralized LLM-agent framework for oncology decision support in which specialist agents operate on partitioned clinical data streams rather than sharing inputs. The framework adds agent-specific finetuning and a contribution-aware aggregation mechanism that uses deterministic embedding projections to approximate each agent's marginal utility, replacing stochastic narrative-based reasoning with explicit, interpretable credit assignment. It was evaluated on multiple oncology benchmarks, including real-world multidisciplinary tumor board datasets from large academic hospitals in North America and East Asia. Most existing multi-agent medical frameworks assume centralized data access, which is impractical in privacy-sensitive clinical settings; CoMMa shows that strong performance and generalization are possible while enforcing data decentralization. Its explicit credit assignment also addresses a core weakness of LLM-based multi-agent systems — opaque, narrative-driven coordination — which matters for clinical trust, auditability, and regulatory acceptance. The aggregation mechanism replaces stochastic, narrative-based reasoning with deterministic embedding projections that approximate each agent's marginal utility, yielding a stable and interpretable decision pathway. Evaluation covers both real-world multidisciplinary tumor board datasets from large academic hospitals in North America and East Asia and public datasets, demonstrating generalization across heterogeneous clinical settings; the work is currently an arXiv preprint (v3) without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent LLM systems split a complex task among several language-model agents that each handle a role and then combine their outputs, and prior medical versions typically let all agents see the same centralized data and coordinate through prompt-based dialogue. In oncology, multidisciplinary tumor boards already rely on multiple specialists reviewing a case together, making them a natural fit for multi-agent AI — but hospitals cannot legally or ethically pool patient records, so centralized designs are hard to deploy. Credit assignment, a long-studied problem in multi-agent reinforcement learning, asks how much each agent actually contributed to a joint decision; CoMMa adapts this idea to LLM agents so clinicians can see which specialist drove a recommendation.

<a id="item-178"></a>
## [NeuronSpark: Selective Spiking State Space Models for Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16148) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NeuronSpark introduces input-dependent spiking membrane dynamics, where each neuron's decay rate, gain, and firing threshold adapt to the current token, enabling self-attention-free language modeling with adaptive computation. At 1.1B parameters and 2.1B tokens, it reaches a 40.0 eight-task zero-shot average, retains 95.2%/93.2% of fully trained Pythia/Mamba scores, and reduces operation-energy proxy by 90.1%/67.0% versus Transformer/Mamba at 2,048 tokens. This work addresses a central obstacle in spiking language models—uniform firing sensitivity across tokens—and shows that billion-scale spiking pretraining from random initialization is feasible without conversion, distillation, or backbone transfer. It could advance energy-efficient NLP and neuromorphic computing by offering a competitive, self-attention-free alternative to Transformer and Mamba backbones. The model combines input-dependent spiking membrane dynamics with gated associative memory and adaptive temporal aggregation, and it spontaneously develops a fast–slow hierarchy. A physical FPGA path reaches 24.000s time-to-first-token and 0.082440 tokens per second, and the paper relates the neuron to fixed-parameter spiking models and the memory to linear attention.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Spiking neural networks (SNNs) are brain-inspired models that transmit information via discrete spikes, firing only when a neuron's membrane potential crosses a threshold, which can improve energy efficiency. State space models (SSMs) such as Mamba process sequences by updating a fixed-size recurrent state, offering an alternative to self-attention. Neuromorphic computing aims to emulate the brain's structure and function for efficient AI, and this paper applies these ideas to large-scale language model pretraining.

## [DEAF Benchmark Diagnoses Acoustic Faithfulness in Audio MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18048) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced DEAF (Diagnostic Evaluation of Acoustic Faithfulness), a benchmark of over 2,700 conflict stimuli spanning emotional prosody, background sounds, and speaker identity, along with a controlled multi-level evaluation framework that progressively increases textual influence. Evaluating seven Audio MLLMs revealed a consistent pattern of text dominance, where predictions are predominantly driven by textual inputs despite sensitivity to acoustic variations. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in evaluating whether Audio MLLMs genuinely process acoustic signals or rely on text-based inference, potentially influencing future model development and evaluation practices. The findings reveal a gap between high performance on standard speech benchmarks and genuine acoustic understanding, which could affect trust in audio AI systems. The benchmark uses a controlled multi-level evaluation framework that disentangles content-driven bias from prompt-induced sycophancy by progressively increasing textual influence, from semantic conflicts to misleading prompts and their combination. Diagnostic metrics quantify model reliance on textual cues over acoustic signals, and the evaluation of seven Audio MLLMs shows text dominance across all models tested.

## [ChemCost: A New Benchmark for LLM Chemical Cost Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07251) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces ChemCost, a benchmark of 1,427 evaluable reactions grounded to a frozen pricing snapshot covering 2,261 chemicals and 230,775 supplier quotes, designed to test LLM agents on chemical procurement cost estimation with exact, judge-free scoring. It also provides controlled noise-injected views that perturb chemical aliases, quantity expressions, missing fields, and input formatting to evaluate robustness. This benchmark addresses a real gap in scientific tool-use evaluation by providing exact, judge-free ground truth instead of curated demonstrations or LLM-as-judge scoring, which matters for the agentic AI and chemistry communities. It offers stage-level diagnosis of grounding, retrieval, procurement, and arithmetic failures, enabling more reliable progress toward AI systems for chemical planning and autonomous laboratories. Experiments with frontier, open-weight, and chemistry-specialized LLM agents show that tool access is necessary but insufficient: the strongest agents reach only 50.6% accuracy within 25% relative error on clean inputs and degrade substantially with realistic noise. Stage-level analysis reveals failures stem from brittle parsing, ineffective evidence integration, invalid pack selection, and non-convergent tool use.

Chemical procurement cost estimation is a practical task requiring an agent to ground chemical identities, retrieve supplier quotes, select valid purchasable packs, normalize quantities, and compute cost from a reaction description. ChemCost provides a frozen pricing snapshot and deterministic labels to enable reproducible, judge-free evaluation of these steps.

## [FactoryBench Tests LLMs on Industrial Robot Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07675) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced FactoryBench, a benchmark of over 70,000 Q&A items grounded in roughly 15,000 normalized episodes from the FactoryWave, AURSAD, and voraus-AD datasets, covering a UR3 cobot and a KUKA KR10 industrial arm. Questions are organized along four causal levels (state, intervention, counterfactual, decision) and five answer formats, and zero-shot evaluation of six frontier LLMs shows no model exceeds 50% on structured levels or 18% on decision-making. This benchmark exposes a wide gap between current LLMs and genuine operational machine understanding, giving the industrial AI and time-series communities a standardized way to measure progress on causal reasoning over real robot sensor data. It could push model developers toward capabilities needed for predictive maintenance, fault diagnosis, and autonomous decision-making on factory floors. Four structured answer formats are scored deterministically, while free-form answers are graded by an LLM-as-judge voting protocol; the accompanying FactoryWave dataset is a dense, multitask, multivariate sensor collection from a UR3 cobot and a KUKA KR10 arm. The benchmark's Q&A pairs are generated through a scalable framework built on structured question templates, and the reported results are zero-shot, meaning no task-specific fine-tuning was applied.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Pearl's ladder of causation is a three-level framework describing increasingly sophisticated causal reasoning: association (observing correlations), intervention (predicting the effect of an action), and counterfactual (reasoning about what would have happened otherwise). Industrial robots such as the UR3 cobot and KUKA KR10 generate rich multivariate time-series telemetry, but evaluating whether AI models truly understand these signals — rather than merely pattern-matching — has lacked a common benchmark. FactoryBench extends the ladder into four practical levels by adding a decision-making tier on top of state, intervention, and counterfactual questions.

## [UxSID: Semantic-Aware Modeling for Ultra-Long User Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09040) ⭐️ 7.0/10

UxSID introduces a semantic-group shared interest memory framework that uses Semantic IDs (SIDs) and a dual-level attention strategy to model ultra-long user sequences, achieving state-of-the-art performance and a 0.337% revenue lift in a large-scale advertising A/B test. Ultra-long user sequence modeling is a core challenge in industrial recommender and advertising systems, where efficiency and effectiveness are often in tension; UxSID's third path between item-specific search and item-agnostic compression could offer a more scalable way to capture target-aware preferences, and the measured revenue lift signals real production value. The framework is end-to-end and balances computational parsimony with semantic awareness by grouping items into semantic groups via SIDs rather than modeling each item individually; the reported 0.337% revenue lift comes from a large-scale advertising A/B test, though the paper is a specialized incremental advance rather than a paradigm shift.

**Background**: Modern recommender systems typically follow a retrieve-and-rank pipeline, and modeling long-term user interests from very long behavior histories has become a major research direction. Existing industrial solutions such as SIM and TWIN use a two-stage approach for efficiency, while Semantic IDs tokenize item metadata into hierarchical discrete codes (often via RQ-VAE) to enable generative retrieval. Dual-level attention is a composite architecture that combines two distinct attention processes to capture complementary relationships.

## [MAGE: Multi-Agent Self-Evolution via Co-Evolutionary Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10064) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MAGE (Multi-Agent Graph-guided Evolution) introduces a framework that externalizes self-knowledge into a four-subgraph co-evolutionary knowledge graph, storing both teacher-written failure corrections and the learner's own correct reasoning traces for task-conditioned retrieval. During evolution, the graph, a task-level search bandit, and a skill-level routing bandit are updated from the same reward stream while the learner's backbone remains frozen, achieving strong performance across nine benchmarks. This work addresses a real limitation in self-evolving language-model agents by enabling a frozen weak backbone to improve at inference time without parameter updates, which could make agent improvement cheaper and more deployable. It is a high-value contribution for researchers in multi-agent systems, LLM self-improvement, and knowledge-graph-guided reasoning. The framework provides structural analysis showing how append-only memory growth, bounded curriculum coverage, and task-filtered retrieval together support stable improvement of the retrieval substrate for frozen-learner evolution. Ablations show that self-harvested success traces and teacher-written corrections are complementary, with success memories contributing most on reasoning-template-heavy tasks and corrective memories supporting harder composition and interaction settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Self-evolving language-model agents aim to improve over iterations, but existing systems typically carry cross-iteration knowledge as natural-language feedback, flat episodic memory, or implicit reinforcement signals, which do not cleanly support a frozen weak backbone at inference time. Knowledge graphs organize information as entities and relations, and co-evolution means the agent and the graph update together. A frozen backbone means the underlying language model's weights are not updated, so all improvement must come from external memory and retrieval.

## [Adaptive Optimizer Interpolates Between SGD and Muon via Schatten-p Norms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19781) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2605.19781v2) introduces a data-driven criterion that dynamically selects proxy-optimal Linear Minimization Oracle (LMO) geometries for each neural network layer, using gradient and activation statistics derived in closed form from a single-step random feature regression surrogate. The framework interpolates between SGD and Muon updates and, by adding parameter-wise preconditioning, recovers SGD, Muon, Adam, and MuAdam as special extrema, with only about 3% runtime overhead on optimized baselines. This work challenges the prevailing practice of fixing update geometries by design or empirical tuning, suggesting that optimizer geometry can be adapted from runtime data. If validated at scale, it could lead to more robust and automatically tuned optimizers, reducing the need for manual selection between families like AdamW and Muon in large-scale deep learning training. The criterion is derived in closed form from gradient and activation statistics using a single-step random feature regression surrogate model, and the design space is navigated via Schatten-p norm geometries that generalize both vector-wise and matrix-wise constraints. As a proof of concept, the data-driven optimizer beats or remains competitive with the best of Muon and AdamW across three training scenarios, though the evaluation is limited and the paper is a preprint without peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Modern optimizers such as Muon impose matrix-wise geometry constraints on their updates, which can be unified under Linear Minimization Oracle (LMO) theory—a computational primitive used in convex optimization to find the extreme point of a constraint set given a linear objective. Schatten-p norms generalize vector ℓp norms to matrix spaces and are used to define these geometries, with SGD corresponding to one extreme and Muon to another. Adam and AdamW are widely used adaptive optimizers that apply parameter-wise preconditioning, and MuAdam combines Muon-style matrix updates with Adam-style preconditioning.

## [Latent Reward Steering Adaptively Corrects Reasoning LLMs at Inference Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00726) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Latent Reward Steering (LRS), an adaptive inference-time framework that optimizes sparse-autoencoder (SAE) latent states to implicitly promote cognitive behaviors in reasoning LLMs. Instead of relying on predefined behaviors or steering directions, LRS trains a latent reward model on reasoning traces using final answer correctness, then uses reward gradients to apply state-specific corrections to fragile latent states during inference. This work offers a technically deep approach to adaptive reasoning control, potentially improving how reasoning LLMs recover from errors without retraining. It could benefit researchers working on reasoning, interpretability, and inference-time adaptation, though it is not a paradigm shift. LRS uses a reward and confidence gate to restrict intervention only to latent states that the reward signal flags as fragile, and experiments across multiple reasoning LLM backbones and benchmarks show consistent improvements over baselines. Post-hoc analyses indicate that LRS implicitly promotes good cognitive behaviors that fix original reasoning errors, and code is available on GitHub.

**Background**: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are tools that decompose a language model's internal activations into sparse, interpretable latent features, often used to understand and steer model behavior. Reasoning LLMs generate step-by-step chains of thought, but their performance depends on deploying cognitive behaviors effectively during generation. Existing steering methods often rely on explicit behavior-level control, which can be insufficiently adaptive when failures and corrections vary across reasoning states, tasks, and models.

## [EvoDrive Uses LLM Agents to Generate Pareto-Optimal Safety-Critical Driving Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03678) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EvoDrive introduces the first automated, LLM-based agentic evolution framework for multi-objective safety-critical scenario generation, employing a simulator-grounded actor-critic architecture with a memory-driven actor, filtering critics, and a self-evolving world evaluator. Benchmark results on MetaDrive and CARLA show it significantly expands the Pareto frontier across various generators and produces valuable scenarios for policy training. Safety-critical scenario generation is essential for validating autonomous driving systems, but existing methods rely on handcrafted heuristics that limit exploration and collapse the adversariality-realism trade-off into single-scalar optimization. EvoDrive's Pareto-based approach preserves diverse trade-offs, potentially improving both the coverage and realism of validation scenarios and the robustness of trained driving policies. The framework maintains a Pareto archive of evaluated candidates to preserve diverse attack-realism trade-offs and guide future evolution via simulation feedback, while the self-evolving world evaluator routes promising proposals to optimize simulation budgets. The abstract is truncated, so full technical details and limitations are not yet available.

**Background**: Multi-objective optimization, or Pareto optimization, deals with problems involving more than one objective function to be optimized simultaneously, where the goal is to find a set of trade-off solutions known as the Pareto front. Actor-critic is a family of reinforcement learning algorithms that combine a policy-based actor, which selects actions, with a value-based critic, which evaluates them. In autonomous driving, safety-critical scenario generation aims to create challenging simulated situations that expose failures in driving systems, but must balance adversariality with realism to be useful for validation and training.

## [Why Affect Triggers and LLM Judges Fail to Time Agent Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04296) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.04296v2) evaluates four intervention trigger families—absolute state thresholds, composite state-action patterns, regex reasoning-feature extraction, and zero-shot LLM-as-judge—against human-annotated intervention points on SWE-bench-Verified debugging traces. It reports a 'State Saturation Trap' where modeled frustration pins at maximum and fires on 39–83% of actions, an LLM-judge capability floor (F1 0.17–0.40 at up to 90x cost), and human annotators agreeing only slightly above chance (Krippendorff's alpha = +0.047). The findings suggest that intervention timing for autonomous agents is a low-reliability construct, making single-annotator F1 an unsuitable optimization target for runtime safety layers. This has direct implications for AI safety research and the design of agentic systems that must decide when to interrupt long-horizon software execution. The study uses an 18-dimensional affective-dynamics engine (HEART) as a diagnostic probe, and notes a v2 erratum: the observer could not register recovery because decay never ran. LLM judges only escape zero-firing with full-trajectory context, and human annotators agree on intervention type only for 'reflect' (alpha = +0.226), with 'pause' degenerate and 'clarify' below chance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: SWE-bench is a benchmark for coding agents built from real GitHub issues in Python repositories; SWE-bench Verified is a human-validated 500-instance subset. LLM-as-a-judge is a technique where a language model evaluates another model's output against criteria, often as a cheaper alternative to human annotation. Runtime safety layers for autonomous agents need to decide when to interrupt an agent, and this paper tests whether affect-based triggers or LLM judges can reliably make that decision.

## [Study Finds Most Multi-Agent LLM Workflows Underperform a Single Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05670) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.05670v2) introduces BenchAgent, a shared evaluation framework, and compares six multi-agent workflow configurations against a single-agent baseline (BenchAgent Core) across ten reasoning, coding, and tool-use benchmarks using GPT-4.1, with 3,302 instances per configuration per run over three runs. Only EvoAgent consistently beat the baseline, reaching 79.70% mean pooled accuracy versus 76.65% for Core, while the other five multi-agent systems trailed by 1.71 to 8.76 percentage points. The findings challenge the common assumption that adding more agents improves LLM performance, giving practitioners concrete evidence that most multi-agent architectures add token cost without accuracy gains. This matters for teams deciding whether to adopt multi-agent designs, since the paper identifies answer preservation and resource allocation as key optimization directions. LLM-Debate gained 9.67 points on HumanEval but lost 1.96 points in pooled accuracy while consuming 1.39 times the tokens of Core, and ChatEval gained 22.25 points on IFEval yet trailed Core in pooled accuracy with 4.34 times the token consumption. All configurations used GPT-4.1 with common tool and scoring interfaces, and Core executions shared the same step limits.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent LLM workflows split a task among several model instances that debate, critique, or divide labor, and are widely promoted as a way to boost reasoning and factuality. BenchAgent is the shared evaluation framework introduced in this paper, while EvoAgent is a multi-agent generation method that uses evolutionary algorithms to automatically build agent configurations. The study measures both accuracy and recorded token use to assess whether extra agents justify their inference cost.

## [SkeMex: Self-Evolving Skill Memory for Medical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09365) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose SkeMex, a post-deployment self-evolution framework that distills medical agents' interaction trajectories into structured skills organized in a multi-branch repository spanning general, task-specific, and action-level experience, all without updating model weights. It uses context-dependent utility estimated from environment feedback to guide value-aware retrieval and repository governance through a closed-loop 'Read-Write-Assess-Govern' lifecycle, and experiments show it consistently outperforms representative memory-based agents across diverse clinical tasks. Medical agents are increasingly expected to support interactive clinical decision making rather than static question answering, yet existing memory mechanisms retain redundant, noisy raw traces and rarely distinguish which memories are truly useful. SkeMex's weight-free, skill-based approach could enable more compact, reliable, and generalizable long-horizon clinical reasoning, with demonstrated transferability across model backbones. SkeMex encodes reusable procedural knowledge as structured skills rather than raw historical traces, and its utility estimation from environment feedback drives both value-aware retrieval and governance actions such as promoting useful memories and removing harmful entries. The framework generalizes across model backbones and supports transferable skill memory, though it remains an arXiv preprint with all data and code promised for public release.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Large language model (LLM) agents often rely on memory mechanisms to reuse past experience, but raw trajectory storage tends to accumulate redundancy and noise that hinder long-horizon reasoning. Skill-level memory is an emerging paradigm that distills raw trajectories into reusable, compositional behavioral routines with explicit interfaces, while self-evolving systems improve agent behavior by updating external scaffolding such as prompts, skills, and memories without modifying model weights via fine-tuning.

## [TelcoAgent: Scalable, Explainable 5G KPM Forecasting via Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19821) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TelcoAgent is a foundation model-based framework that forecasts multiple 5G Key Performance Measurements (KPMs) across diverse network cells without site-specific training. It combines an automated three-agent pipeline that builds a 3GPP knowledge graph from specification documents, a time-series foundation model (TSFM) for zero-shot forecasting, and a reasoning pipeline that produces domain-grounded diagnostics, evaluated on a 3-month city-scale dataset from a U.S. operator covering 7 KPMs across 200 cells. Existing ML approaches for KPM forecasting struggle with scalability and explainability, which limits their real-world deployment in 5G and next-generation network management. By enabling zero-shot, multi-KPM forecasting with 3GPP-grounded explanations, TelcoAgent could support proactive, zero-touch network operations and reduce the need for per-site model training. The framework uses a three-agent pipeline to extract a 3GPP knowledge graph directly from specification documents, coupling a TSFM backbone with this graph to provide knowledge-grounded forecasting, causal insights, and actionable recommendations. Evaluation covers 7 KPMs per cell across 200 cells using a 3-month real-world city-scale dataset, though the paper is a preprint and has not yet undergone peer review.

**Background**: Key Performance Measurements (KPMs) are standardized network metrics used to monitor and manage the performance of 5G and next-generation telecom networks, and accurate forecasting of these metrics is essential for proactive network management. Time-series foundation models (TSFMs) are large pre-trained models capable of zero-shot forecasting on unseen data without task-specific training, while knowledge graphs organize domain knowledge as entities and relationships. 3GPP is the standards body that defines 5G specifications, so grounding forecasts in a 3GPP knowledge graph helps connect predictions to standardized network concepts and explanations.

## [HExA: LLM Agents That Learn by Designing Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29315) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Hierarchical Experimentalist Agents (HExA), a training-free, in-context self-improvement framework that lets LLM agents iteratively design query-relevant experiments, distill experience into reusable composable skills, and integrate experimental evidence to answer queries or act. The authors also release Interphyre, a set of hard procedural physics tasks, and report that HExA lifts frontier models such as GPT-6 Astra from failure to near 100% success on the most challenging tasks. Current LLM agents mostly rely on parametric knowledge acquired through imitation, which breaks down in novel domains where prior knowledge alone is insufficient. HExA shows that active experimentation and hierarchical skill transfer can substantially improve both success rates and token efficiency, suggesting a practical path toward more autonomous, self-improving agents that work even with black-box frontier models. HExA requires no external supervision, oracle solutions, or offline data, and works with any LLM including frontier black-box models; it improves token efficiency over naive agentic and memory baselines and enables generalization via hierarchical skill transfer from easier to harder tasks. The evaluation spans multi-step reasoning, interactive partially observable games, and physics law discovery, with Interphyre providing tool-calling and intervention APIs for testing when active experimentation is necessary.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are systems that use a large language model as a reasoning core to plan and act in environments, often augmented by retrieval or search over fixed data. In-context learning means the model adapts its behavior from information placed in its prompt rather than through parameter updates, while 'training-free' frameworks avoid fine-tuning entirely. Reusable composable skills are abstractions distilled from past interactions that can be recombined to solve new tasks faster, similar to how humans reuse learned procedures.

## [Harness Design Shifts LLM Agent Beliefs, New Diagnostic Shows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04528) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.04528v2) introduces a belief-rollout diagnostic that traces how a multi-step LLM agent's anticipated world state and future decisions evolve under different execution harnesses, even when the task, environment, and base model are held fixed. The authors also propose Belief-Invariant World-Modeling (BIWM), which aligns belief trajectories across harnesses by exposing evidence that harness execution hides or compresses. The finding that harness design alone can cause substantial belief divergence — often invisible from terminal success rates — suggests that benchmark scores for coding agents are not comparable unless the harness is disclosed, and it gives agent framework developers a concrete tool to audit harness policies before deployment. Experiments span controlled coding tasks on HIBench-Code, SWE-bench Verified, and Terminal-Bench, and the paper separates immediate interface-induced divergence from divergence that develops over longer horizons; the diagnostic maps each next-action recommendation to one of six coarse categories to make belief shifts interpretable.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: An execution harness (also called agent scaffolding) is the software infrastructure around an LLM that manages tool use, memory, state persistence, execution environments, and feedback loops, as opposed to the model's internal reasoning. SWE-bench Verified is a human-validated subset of 500 instances from the SWE-bench coding benchmark, where an agent must produce a patch for a real GitHub issue without seeing the tests that judge it. Prior work has argued that long-horizon agent benchmark scores are invalid for cross-model comparison unless the harness is disclosed.

## [Who&When Pro: A Large-Scale Benchmark for LLM Agent Failure Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09996) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Who&When Pro, a large-scale benchmark for automated failure attribution in agentic systems, built from 12,326 failed trajectories with golden labels spanning 3 modalities and 26 benchmarks. The benchmark uses a strictly controlled pipeline that injects a failure only after exactly replaying a successful prefix, ensuring the failure point is unambiguous. As LLM agents grow more capable, their failures become subtler and harder to trace, making automated attribution increasingly critical for debugging, safety, and reliability. This benchmark provides empirical guidance on how models attribute failures across modalities, protocols, and model families, which could shape future attribution systems and agent evaluation standards. The dataset covers 26 source benchmarks grouped into nine task categories across three modalities, and the controlled injection pipeline guarantees that each failure is preceded by an exactly replayed successful prefix. The paper also analyzes systematic patterns in attribution across modalities, protocols, and model families, though the abstract does not specify which LLMs were evaluated or the exact attribution accuracy achieved.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Automated failure attribution is the task of reading an agent trajectory that ended badly and answering three questions: who (which agent produced the decisive action), when (which step), and why (which failure mode). In multi-agent LLM systems, errors can cascade through interactions, so tracing a failure back to its root cause is essential for debugging and improving agentic pipelines. Who&When Pro builds on this problem by providing a large, controlled benchmark rather than relying on naturally occurring failures, which are often ambiguous.

## [MIRROR Uses Best Modality View as Teacher for VLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21552) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced MIRROR (Modality-Informed Reciprocal Reasoning Optimization), a reinforcement learning method that evaluates a vision-language model under text-only, image-only, and combined views of the same problem, selects the best-performing view as an internal teacher, and trains the remaining views using a GRPO task-reward objective augmented with a reverse-KL regularizer. They also released ODA-Data, a paired multimodal geometry dataset with text-dominant, image-dominant, and combined views, and reported that MIRROR improves over standard RL on multimodal mathematics benchmarks with more consistent behavior across input modalities. Vision-language models often give inconsistent answers depending on whether a problem is presented as text or as an image, and MIRROR offers a self-supervised way to close that gap without external teacher models or extra human labels. If it generalizes beyond geometry, this cross-view self-distillation approach could become a standard post-training technique for improving multimodal reasoning reliability. MIRROR builds its training signal entirely from the model's own modality-dependent capabilities, using GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) with a reverse-KL regularizer pulling student views toward the selected teacher view. The evaluation focuses on multimodal mathematics problems, and the paper's abstract is truncated, so full details on dataset scale, model sizes, and generalization beyond geometry remain to be verified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) process both text and images, but the same underlying problem can be posed in different "views" — text-only, image-only, or combined — and models often reason differently across them. Reinforcement learning post-training methods such as GRPO improve reasoning by rewarding correct answers, while self-distillation lets a model learn from its own stronger outputs instead of an external teacher. MIRROR combines these ideas by treating the model's best-performing view as the teacher for its weaker views.

## [CASE Uses Gemma 3 KV Cache Pre-filling for Tabular Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03565) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2608.03565v2) introduces CASE (Context-Aware Semantic Embeddings), a framework that pre-fills the KV cache of a custom-trained Gemma 3-based Tabular Language Model with a representative sample of rows to create a persistent semantic anchor for the dataset. Experiments on CARTE, TextTab, and TabArena benchmarks show substantial improvements for tabular learners on semantically rich datasets, especially in low-data regimes. Tabular learners have long treated textual features as discrete symbols, ignoring the semantics in feature names and cell values; CASE bridges this gap by injecting LLM-derived context into statistical models. If validated, this approach could improve performance on real-world tables with rich text, such as healthcare or e-commerce data, particularly when labeled data is scarce. Unlike prior methods that embed each row in isolation, CASE pre-fills the KV cache with a representative sample of rows so that generated row embeddings are dynamically contextualized and anchored in domain-specific semantics. The work is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and the abstract does not report specific quantitative gains or computational overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Tabular data is the dominant format in industry, but most tabular models (e.g., gradient-boosted trees) treat categorical and text columns as opaque symbols, creating what the paper calls a "semantic vacuum." Large language models such as Google DeepMind's Gemma 3 can encode world knowledge about words, and the KV cache stores attention keys and values so that context can be reused across generation steps. Pre-filling that cache with dataset rows lets the model condition new embeddings on the dataset's overall semantics rather than on a single row alone.

## [CASCADE: Agentic Framework Predicts Gene Perturbation Effects Validated on Patient Tumor Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05359) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CASCADE is a new agentic framework that predicts downstream transcriptional effects of gene perturbation using precomputed ARACNe regulatory networks exposed via MCP. For MYC, its predicted knockdown targets showed strong directional concordance with real amplified-vs-non-amplified tumor expression in TCGA data (BRCA: 90.0%, COAD: 72.0%, STAD: 85.7%; all p<=0.0013), replicating in the independent METABRIC cohort at 87.2%. This work shifts validation of perturbation-prediction tools from mere membership checks (whether predicted genes are known cancer genes) to testing whether the predicted direction of change matches reality, a more rigorous standard. It also demonstrates that LLM-based agents can ground natural-language requests into real MCP tool calls, though with notable failure modes on ambiguous queries. CASCADE's accuracy is not shown to exceed curated MSigDB gene-set baselines via Fisher's exact test, and validation is gene-specific rather than universal: proliferation-machinery regulators mostly replicate, while lineage-identity transcription factors and CCND2 consistently fail. In LLM grounding benchmarks across 35 queries, a documented local model reached 71.4% exact match (85.7% for a larger model), but both models confidently defaulted to the wrong perturbation type on ambiguous queries, a failure a targeted fix could not resolve.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: ARACNe is an information-theoretic algorithm that reverse-engineers gene regulatory networks from expression data by using mutual information and the data processing inequality to filter indirect interactions. The Model Context Protocol (MCP), introduced by Anthropic in November 2024, is an open standard that lets LLMs integrate with external tools and data sources. TCGA (The Cancer Genome Atlas) is a landmark program that molecularly characterized over 11,000 primary cancer samples, and METABRIC is an independent breast cancer cohort used here for replication.

## [Fast LapSum: Exact Differentiable Top-k at Million Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06912) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Fast LapSum introduces a scalable solver for the LapSum soft top-k formulation that preserves an exact selection mass of k while supporting end-to-end differentiation. It uses probabilistic bracketing with a binomial order-statistic from kernel-noised samples to restrict sorting to a narrow band around the threshold, then a certification pass verifies the result at the cost of one additional linear pass, with a full-sort fallback for worst cases. Exact-budget differentiable top-k is a fundamental operation for inducing sparsity in large-scale models, enabling robust expert activation, token routing, and attention pruning. By making it practical at million scale with millisecond-level GPU runtimes, Fast LapSum could enable new end-to-end training pipelines in sparse mixture-of-experts, efficient attention, and large-scale optimization. The certified GPU implementation processes 10^6, 10^7, and 10^8 scores in median times of 0.92 ms, 1.39 ms, and 7.24 ms respectively. It achieves an order-of-magnitude speedup over state-of-the-art methods in megapixel sparse adversarial examples and reduces the number of Gaussians in 3D Gaussian splatting while retaining nearly the same rendering quality.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Top-k selection is a core operation in machine learning, used to pick the highest-scoring elements for sparsity, routing, or pruning. However, hard top-k is non-differentiable, which blocks gradient-based end-to-end training, while existing differentiable alternatives become increasingly expensive as the number of coordinates grows. LapSum is a recent soft top-k formulation based on a sum of Laplace distributions that provides a closed-form inverse and low computational complexity, and Fast LapSum builds on it to scale to millions of elements.

## [SLIFT selectively learns from user feedback with dual LoRA adapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09109) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SLIFT, a selective self-learning framework that decomposes each user feedback message into atomic Fix, Spec, or Null components relative to the original task, then trains two complementary LoRA adapters on a shared frozen backbone: a Generalist that consolidates Fix requirements via feedback-conditioned self-distillation, and a Specialist that supplies residual guidance for unmet Spec refinements. Across multiple backbones, SLIFT achieves strong results on MemoryBench and WildFB, with code released anonymously. This work addresses a core challenge in LLM alignment and continual improvement: a single feedback message often implies multiple behavioral changes with different generalization scopes, and treating them uniformly can cause overfitting or underfitting. By separating task-validity fixes from condition-specific refinements, SLIFT offers a more principled way to turn everyday user interactions into persistent model improvements, which matters for human-in-the-loop fine-tuning and deployed assistants. SLIFT keeps the backbone frozen and trains only two LoRA adapters, using feedback-conditioned self-distillation for the Generalist and residual guidance for the Specialist, while Null components deliberately induce no positive update. The framework is evaluated on MemoryBench and WildFB across several backbones, and the paper includes targeted analyses of its underlying mechanisms; the code is available at an anonymous.4open.science repository.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique introduced by Microsoft researchers in 2021 that freezes pre-trained model weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices into Transformer layers, greatly reducing trainable parameters. Self-distillation is a training approach in which a model's own outputs guide its updates, and prior work such as Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning has used it to bridge distribution gaps and mitigate catastrophic forgetting. SLIFT combines these ideas to decide not just what to learn from user feedback, but at what scope each piece of feedback should generalize.

## [SSKG Uses Stochastic Knowledge Graphs to Simulate Students at Real Mastery Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21668) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.21668v2) proposes Stochastic Student Knowledge Graphs (SSKG), which represent a student's mastery profile as probabilities assigned to triples in a Curriculum Knowledge Graph (CKG) extracted from course materials. Instead of prompting an LLM to role-play a weak student, the system samples the SSKG to decide whether a problem is solved correctly, then has the LLM generate a rationale consistent with that sampled outcome; this yields 44.1-85.2% accuracy across low-to-high mastery profiles, versus 86.5-100% for seven baselines. LLMs used to generate synthetic tutoring data or stress-test educational systems tend to answer at full capability no matter what skill level they are told to imitate, which undermines the realism of simulated students. A method that produces a monotonic accuracy gradient across mastery levels could make synthetic student data and tutoring-system evaluations far more trustworthy for education AI developers and researchers. The pipeline uses human-in-the-loop workflows in which LLMs extract the CKG from course materials, decompose solution paths into CKG triples, and map incorrect distractors; an ablation study shows taxonomy awareness contributes most to fidelity gains, especially for lower-mastery profiles. The limitation is that the approach depends on the quality of the extracted CKG and the human-in-the-loop curation, and results are reported on SAT-style questions rather than broader domains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Knowledge graphs represent facts as triples of entities and relations, and in education a Curriculum Knowledge Graph (CKG) encodes the concepts and skills a course teaches and how they depend on each other. Student simulation means using an LLM as a stand-in learner, for example to generate practice data or to test whether a tutoring system responds well to novices. Prior work mostly relies on prompt-level role assignment, telling the model to 'answer like a beginner', which fails because the model's underlying capability leaks through.

<a id="item-200"></a>
## [GenCoord: Executable Commitments for Embodied LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22055) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GenCoord introduces a decentralized coordination protocol in which embodied LLM agents replace free-form dialogue with executable commitments written in a compact Short DSL, pairing the sender's skill path with the partner's continuation. In Minecraft, trained local 0.8B agents matched the task success of controlled free-form communication while cutting median commitment time by 68.2%, and multistep commitments raised held-out-template success from 91.3% to 98.1%. The work suggests a design principle for embodied cooperation: make the division of work itself the message, so inference is spent on choosing and revising work rather than on generating and parsing dialogue. This could substantially reduce token cost and latency in multi-agent LLM systems, an important concern as embodied agents move toward real-time, resource-constrained deployment. GenCoord separates fulfillment verification from original-task success, and complete requests let a deterministic receiver proceed without another model call; a same-field full-key JSON control approached free-form output length and delay, indicating that the amount generated matters even when messages are structured. With the same prompted DeepSeek model, both protocols completed nearly all tasks, but dialogue generated 2.8 times as many output tokens.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Embodied LLM agents are language-model-driven agents that act in simulated or physical environments, and in multi-agent settings each agent often holds private information about its own capabilities, inventory, or goals. Traditional dialogue-based coordination requires models to generate messages and interpret replies before the team can act, which consumes inference time and tokens. GenCoord instead treats the division of work as the message, expressing joint work in a compact domain-specific language (Short DSL) so that compatible skill paths form commitments linking one agent's delivery to another's work after verified receipt.

## [LLM Agents with Data-Driven Personas Simulate A/B Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01038) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.01038v2) proposes a simulation framework that predicts A/B test outcomes using LLM-powered agents conditioned on data-driven personas built from anonymized real user behavioral signals such as activity patterns, engagement signals, and inferred demographics. On a benchmark of 40 A/B tests spanning two metric types, the best configuration achieves 0.75–0.80 directional accuracy. If validated, this approach could let product teams pre-screen experiments without real user traffic, saving weeks of measurement time and significant engineering effort. It also signals a broader trend of using LLM agents grounded in real behavioral data for product experimentation and decision-making. The framework treats A/B test simulation as a structured question task and systematically studies question design formats, persona data source and domain alignment, the trade-off between per-persona behavioral depth and population diversity, and efficient population subsampling. The abstract is truncated and does not report confidence intervals or failure cases, so the robustness of the 0.75–0.80 directional accuracy remains unclear.

## [Convention Gap Measures Implicit Communication in Human-AI Cooperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11489) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces the "convention gap," a diagnostic that subtracts the observed failure rate of played cards from the failure probability predicted purely from the literal content of hints and visible cards in Hanabi. The authors prove the population gap is zero when partners convey nothing beyond literal content, and report +26.2 percentage points in public human-human games, +16.4 pp for human plays with AI partners, and -0.6 pp in simulated AI-AI games. The results suggest that AI-AI benchmarks can miss the implicit conventions humans rely on, so evaluating agents intended for human partners should include the convention gap on human-partnered play rather than only AI-AI performance. This has implications for AI alignment, multi-agent systems, and the design of cooperative AI teammates. The gap is a diagnostic, not a measure of how much implicit information is conveyed; a nonzero population gap only indicates that partner choices conveyed more than literal content. Across five levels of Off-Belief Learning agents, the gap rose monotonically from +1.6 to +21.7 pp, and with three AI agents from a published human-AI study, human failure ranged from 14.4% to 34.4% even though literal failure probability was similar (38-41%).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Hanabi is a cooperative card game in which players see everyone's cards except their own and can communicate only through limited hints about color or rank, making it a standard testbed for multi-agent coordination and theory of mind. Human players often rely on shared conventions—agreed-upon ways of reading hints beyond their literal meaning—which AI benchmarks trained against other AIs may not capture. The paper formalizes this by comparing literal-content predictions with observed play failures.

## [JEV-as-a-Judge Cuts LLM Evaluation Cost with Confidence Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26550) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces JEV-as-a-Judge, a decision-only judge that returns label probabilities instead of free text and uses its confidence to decide whether to accept a verdict or escalate it to a slower reasoning judge. Tested against sixteen generative and reward-model judges with blinded human adjudication, the cascade matched GPT-6's accuracy on 1,610 held-out pairs at 41% of its fee and exactly matched GPT-6 on two pre-specified live workloads. LLM-as-a-judge has become the default way to scale model evaluation, but reasoning judges are slow and expensive, which limits how often teams can evaluate. This work shows that confidence-based routing can preserve top-tier accuracy at a fraction of the cost and latency, making frequent, large-scale evaluation more practical for AI/ML teams. JEV comes within three points of GPT-6 wherever a verdict can be read off the text, at 0.36% of GPT-6's fee and a 0.15-second median latency, but falls behind where the verdict must be derived, as in math, code, and logic. Confidence routing also weakens on style-adversarial pairs and reference-free prose, and the paper closes with a recipe for validating thresholds locally.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-judge is an evaluation technique in which a large language model scores or ranks the outputs of another model against a rubric, serving as a cheaper and more scalable alternative to human annotation and word-overlap metrics like BLEU and ROUGE. Reasoning judges produce a rationale before deciding, which improves quality but adds latency and cost. Confidence routing is a decision-control pattern that separates routine, high-confidence outputs from uncertain or high-impact ones, sending only the latter to a more expensive path.

## [Dual-Agent Framework Automates MetaBBO Optimizer Design via Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35328) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.35328v2) proposes a dual-agent framework that treats Meta-Black-Box Optimization (MetaBBO) design as a coding task, using a task agent to evolve a MetaBBO codebase and a hyper agent to modify the task agent and itself. Starting from a naive MetaBBO template, the framework automatically discovers novel variants that outperform state-of-the-art human-made MetaBBO baselines and adapt quickly across different optimization domains. This is the first exploration of automating the design of complex learning-assisted optimization algorithms, potentially removing the subjectivity and case-by-case handcrafting that currently limits MetaBBO's performance ceiling and practical usability. If validated, it could shift AI for Optimization from manual, expert-driven design toward open-ended, self-improving algorithm discovery. The framework's recursive loop feeds all in-execution information back to the agents for self-referential improvement, and interpretation analysis reveals emergent design principles from this open-ended process. However, the abstract is truncated, and no community discussion or peer review is yet available to gauge reproducibility or engagement.

Traditionally, these meta-level policies are handcrafted and customized case by case, which introduces subjectivity and limits both performance and usability. ## [DREAM: Dynamic Resolution Assignment for Multimodal Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05615) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DREAM introduces a zero-shot probe round in which each agent tests multiple image resolutions, quantifies its uncertainty via Average Normalized Log-Likelihood (ANLL), and is assigned its empirically optimal resolution using an adaptive threshold. It also adds Uncertainty-Guided Rollback Aggregation, which tracks uncertainty across debate rounds and restores early low-uncertainty answers that were overridden by group pressure. The work targets two persistent weaknesses of multimodal multi-agent debate — fixed visual inputs and groupthink — and reports 1.5–3.2% accuracy gains over MAD baselines on six multimodal datasets without dataset-specific tuning. If it generalizes, it could make debate-based multimodal reasoning more reliable and efficient for practitioners building agentic vision-language systems. DREAM uses ANLL as its uncertainty signal and an adaptive threshold to assign resolutions per agent, and it improves the accuracy-token trade-off rather than accuracy alone. As an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review, and its evaluation is limited to six multimodal datasets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent debate (MAD) is a paradigm in which several LLM instances propose, critique, and revise answers over multiple rounds to improve reasoning, and it is increasingly being extended to multimodal settings where agents also see images. Groupthink occurs when agents abandon correct deductions to conform to confident but hallucinated peer responses, a failure mode related to sycophancy in homogeneous debate. Average Normalized Log-Likelihood (ANLL) is a token-level confidence measure used here to quantify how uncertain an agent is about its answer.

## [Shared Policies Can Learn Silence in Multi-Agent Safety Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09002) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.09002v2) analyzes a multi-agent game where any witness can stop an unsafe task by reporting, proving that when k witnesses share a policy and draw actions independently, the expected-reward derivative at universal silence counts the benefit of silence k times while the reporting-versus-silence comparison counts it only once, so a fully shared policy can be locally attracted to silence even when reporting is optimal. The authors derive an audit condition sufficient for exact policy-gradient updates to reach universal reporting and, outside boundary cases, necessary from near universal silence, then validate it with PPO on 24 witness graphs. The result matters for AI safety and multi-agent reinforcement learning because it shows that random audits penalizing silence can make universal reporting optimal in theory without ensuring that a silent team actually learns to report, a gap that could leave unsafe tasks unstopped in safety-critical deployments. It also gives concrete guidance on how to group agents into shared policies and allocate audits so that policy-gradient methods converge to reporting. In a balanced family, meeting the audit condition with prescribed margins costs k times as much for a fully shared policy as for one policy per role, and in PPO experiments on 24 witness graphs starting from learned silence, witness groups (co-witnesses placed in separate networks) reduced unsafe task completion by 33.59 percentage points versus shuffled groups of the same sizes under the same audits (95% graph-bootstrap interval: 21.03–45.13). Only 9 of 48 witness-group runs achieved below 1% unsafe completion while retaining at least 90% legitimate completion, and at the same audit budget a fully shared network met both thresholds in 0/48 runs with independent action draws but 48/48 with a common draw; on 24 further graphs, audits allocated by the derived condition cut unsafe completion by 42.22 points relative to audits maximizing a static coalition deterrence ratio.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: In multi-agent reinforcement learning, agents often share a single policy network to improve sample efficiency, and policy-gradient methods such as PPO directly optimize a parameterized policy by estimating the gradient of expected reward. This paper studies a safety setting where a team shares reward for completed tasks, so reporting and stopping unsafe work reduces team reward, and random audits that penalize silence are used to encourage reporting. The analysis shows that sharing a policy across witnesses can distort the gradient in a way that makes silence a local attractor, and it connects this to audit design and agent grouping.

## [Reasoning-Token Counts Spike Under Prompted Untruthful Responses in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10405) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.10405v2) reports that three reasoning-capable large language models answering 210 multiple-choice questions produced fewer reasoning tokens when instructed to respond truthfully than when instructed to lie or to ignore truth. In one model, untruthful instructions also increased the likelihood that the model would reason at all. This suggests reasoning-token count could serve as a simple, content-independent black-box signal for detecting untruthful behavior when raw chain-of-thought traces are unavailable, illegible, or unfaithful, which matters for AI safety and interpretability monitoring. The study is a proof of concept at the group level and does not yet establish reasoning-token count as a detector of spontaneous deception or general misalignment; the authors call for future work on instance-level detection, out-of-distribution generalization, learned deceptive policies, hidden objectives, and robustness under adversarial pressure.

Cognitive load theory, developed by John Sweller in the late 1980s, holds that working memory is limited and that greater mental effort is required for harder or more demanding tasks. The paper applies this idea by treating the number of reasoning tokens as a low-bandwidth proxy for cognitive load during test-time reasoning.

## [Potential-Based Estimator Cuts Variance in Language Model Expectation Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11399) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a potential-based estimator that exploits next-token conditional probabilities—already computed as a by-product of sampling—to reduce the variance of Monte Carlo estimates of expectations under autoregressive language models. The authors derive theoretical conditions under which a chosen potential provably reduces variance, and develop practical potentials for several estimands, demonstrating substantial variance reductions at comparable computational cost. Many language model applications—such as evaluating sequence-level properties, computing risk measures, or estimating partition functions—depend on expectations rather than individual samples, and reliable estimation is often prohibitively expensive. This method offers a principled, low-overhead way to make such estimates more reliable, which could benefit evaluation pipelines, reinforcement learning from human feedback, and any downstream task requiring accurate expectation estimates. The estimator relies on potentials defined as real-valued functions on prefixes that additively decompose the test functional, and its variance depends on the chosen potential; the paper provides both theoretical variance-reduction conditions and practical potential designs. The approach is validated across several estimands with substantial variance reductions at comparable computational cost, though the abstract does not specify which model sizes or benchmarks were used.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Autoregressive language models generate text token by token, and at each step they produce a conditional probability distribution over the next token; these probabilities are normally discarded after sampling. Monte Carlo estimation approximates an expectation by averaging over random samples, but its accuracy depends on variance, which can be very high for sequence-level functionals. Variance reduction techniques—such as importance sampling and control variates—aim to lower this variance without increasing the number of samples, and this paper's potential-based estimator is a new contribution in that tradition.

## [TRACE Framework Traces Emotions from Causes to Consequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11410) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes TRACE, a cognition-oriented framework that formalizes an affective episode into three stages—Condition, Affect, and Effect—and introduces TRACE-Bench, a benchmark of 3,746 structured QA pairs over 646 real-world social videos covering five affective tasks. It also presents TRACER, a cognition-grounded structured reasoning method that outperforms all evaluated model baselines on each of the five tasks. Affective computing has largely focused on categorical emotion recognition, but this work addresses the underexplored process view of emotion shaped by appraisal, regulation, and social interpretation, which could push multimodal models toward deeper social-emotional understanding. The matched human-model comparison revealing a substantial performance gap highlights how far current MLLMs still are from human-level affective reasoning. TRACE-Bench spans grounded affect recognition, regulation decoding, cause reasoning, effect reasoning, and full-chain reconstruction, and affect-specialized models generally lag behind general-purpose MLLMs. Recurring failure modes include treating displayed behavior as genuine feeling and fabricating unsupported events during long-chain generation.

**Background**: Affective computing is the field concerned with systems that recognize, interpret, or respond to human emotion, and it has advanced from categorical emotion recognition to open-ended affective analysis with large multimodal models. Appraisal theory in psychology holds that emotions arise from a person's evaluations of events, meaning the same event can produce different feelings depending on how it is appraised. TRACE builds on this process view by linking observable cues with cognitive factors such as internal stance and regulation of emotional display.

Researchers introduced MultiWorldBench, a Minecraft-based diagnostic benchmark with 495 case configurations across seven task suites and ten capabilities, to test whether independently controlled views in multiplayer world models describe one shared, persistent world. Evaluating Solaris, Gamma-World, and MineWorld against an Engine GT reference, Gamma-World led generated systems with a ten-capability average of 21.39, followed by Solaris at 20.88 and MineWorld at 1.89, while Engine GT reached 91.69. The results show that plausible individual views do not yet constitute a coherent multiplayer world, exposing a major gap between current world models and true shared-world consistency. This matters for AI/ML research on world models, simulation, and multi-agent environments, where persistent shared state is essential for realistic and reliable systems. All generated systems scored at most 8.00 on state persistence and 1.33 on structural consistency, and none succeeded in spatial reasoning or building-identity preservation. Human preferences produced the same overall ranking and aligned strongly with automatic evaluation, with a mean dimension-level Spearman correlation of 0.96.

Researchers developed InterviewPlayground, a simulation environment that evaluates AI interviewers using social theory-grounded simulated participants and produces an InterviewReportCard. They validated it by running 15 real qualitative studies with five AI interviewers, three topics, and 450 human participants, finding that simulated performance predicted human-study performance with an average Pearson correlation of 0.86 across 12 measures. A validated simulation environment could give researchers and developers a faster, cheaper way to assess conversational AI and uncover failure modes before deploying it in market research, polling, or social science. The paper also frames this work as a broader roadmap for developing validated, simulation-based evaluations of conversational AI systems.

<a id="item-212"></a>
## [Leverage SHAP Achieves Provably Accurate Shapley Value Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.01917) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Leverage SHAP, a lightweight modification of Kernel SHAP that provides provably accurate Shapley value estimates with only O(n log n) model evaluations, compared to the O(2^n) required for exact computation. The method leverages a connection to agnostic active learning and achieves approximately 50% error reduction over the optimized Kernel SHAP implementation in the widely used SHAP library. This work addresses a long-standing gap in explainable AI by providing the first strong non-asymptotic complexity guarantees for Kernel SHAP, a widely used model-agnostic explanation method. The improved accuracy and efficiency could make Shapley-based explanations more practical for high-dimensional models and real-world applications. Leverage SHAP employs leverage score sampling, a regression tool connected to agnostic active learning, to select which model evaluations to perform. The theoretical guarantee of O(n log n) evaluations is a significant improvement over the exponential cost of exact Shapley value computation, and the empirical results show a 50% error reduction compared to the optimized Kernel SHAP in the SHAP library.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Shapley values, originally from cooperative game theory, are used in explainable machine learning to attribute a model's prediction to its input features. Exact computation requires evaluating the model on all subsets of features, which is exponential in the number of features. Kernel SHAP is a popular approximation algorithm that is model-agnostic and effective in practice, but until now lacked strong non-asymptotic complexity guarantees. Agnostic active learning is a framework for learning with noisy data and minimal queries, which inspired the sampling strategy in Leverage SHAP.
