<a id="item-213"></a>
## [Conditional Vendi Score: Prompt-Aware Diversity Evaluation for Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02817) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Conditional-Vendi and Conditional-RKE, two new diversity metrics derived from the conditional entropy of positive semidefinite matrices, designed to isolate model-induced diversity in prompt-guided generative models. Conditional-RKE achieves an O(1/√n) convergence rate, while Conditional-Vendi uses a truncated-spectrum approximation for scalable and consistent estimates. Existing diversity metrics like Vendi and RKE were designed for unconditional models and cannot separate prompt-induced variability from model-induced diversity, leaving a critical gap in evaluating prompt-guided generative models. These conditional scores enable more accurate diversity assessment for text-to-image, image-captioning, and LLM tasks, and can even guide diffusion models toward more diverse outputs. Conditional-RKE enjoys a theoretical O(1/√n) convergence rate, and Conditional-Vendi's truncated-spectrum approximation provides scalable and consistent estimates. Experiments show that the conditional scores recover ground-truth diversity orderings and can guide diffusion models toward more diverse samples; the codebase is available at https://github.com/mjalali/conditional-vendi.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Generative models guided by text prompts are typically evaluated for fidelity and prompt alignment, but their ability to produce diverse outputs is less explored. Existing diversity metrics such as Vendi and RKE are based on the von Neumann and Rényi entropies of kernel matrices and were developed for unconditional models. They cannot distinguish variability caused by different prompts from variability inherent to the model itself. This paper addresses that gap by deriving conditional entropy-based measures that isolate model-induced diversity.

## [Modular Feature Inversion Reveals How Transformer Vision Models See](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06534) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2412.06534v5) introduces a modular variant of inverse-network feature inversion that reconstructs images from intermediate representations of large-scale transformer vision models, including DETR, ViT, Swin Transformer, and DeiT. The authors analyze reconstructions across network depth, finding gradual representational changes and systematic differences between DETR and ViT in preserving contextual shape, fine-grained detail, and robustness to color perturbations. This work provides an interpretability tool that makes the internal representations of transformer-based vision models more transparent, which matters as these architectures increasingly replace CNNs in detection, segmentation, and recognition. The ability to visualize what each layer encodes could help researchers debug models, compare architectures, and build more trustworthy vision systems. The modular inverse-network design makes feature inversion computationally more efficient and, in some cases, produces semantically more coherent reconstructions than prior approaches. The main analysis focuses on DETR versus ViT, while results for Swin Transformer and DeiT support the framework's broader applicability; the paper is a preprint and does not claim state-of-the-art performance on any downstream task.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Feature inversion is an interpretability technique that trains a separate inverse network to reconstruct an input image from a model's internal feature maps, revealing what information those features retain. Transformer-based vision models such as ViT split images into patches and process them with self-attention, while DETR applies transformers to object detection as direct set prediction; Swin Transformer adds shifted-window local attention for efficiency, and DeiT is a data-efficient ViT variant. Understanding what these models encode at each layer is a central challenge in deep learning interpretability.

## [CRM: Controllable Retrieval Model Steers Recommendations via Watch Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.13844) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced the Controllable Retrieval Model (CRM), an outcome-conditioned retrieval framework that adds watch time as an input to the user tower, allowing inference-time steering of recommendations without retraining. CRM is implemented as both a DNN-based two-tower model and a Decision Transformer, and is deployed in Kuaishou's short-video recommendation system serving over 400 million users, with online A/B tests showing watch time improvements of 0.372% in Kuaishou and 0.457% in Kuaishou Lite. This work shows that retrieval models can be steered at inference time by conditioning on desired outcomes like watch time, eliminating the need for costly retraining when business goals change. The successful deployment at Kuaishou's scale (400M+ users) demonstrates the practical viability of controllable retrieval for large-scale industrial recommendation systems. The item tower remains independent of the condition, so item embeddings can still be precomputed and indexed for approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) search, preserving retrieval efficiency. The Decision Transformer variant interleaves item tokens with watch-time-to-go condition tokens, using the target watch time as a numerical prompt, and for each online request the system chooses between the average and maximum watch times in the user's recent history as the condition.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval models in recommendation systems must efficiently search large item corpora to find candidates for a user, typically using a two-tower architecture where user and item features are mapped into a shared embedding space and matched via approximate nearest neighbor search. The Decision Transformer is a framework that reformulates reinforcement learning as sequence modeling over (state, action, return-to-go) trajectories, enabling goal-conditioned behavior via a target return. CRM combines these ideas by treating watch time as a controllable condition that steers retrieval outcomes at inference time.

## [WoundAmbit benchmarks segmentation models for remote wound care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06185) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers benchmarked state-of-the-art semantic segmentation models from general vision, medical imaging, and public wound challenges under standardized training and cross-validation, and proposed a reference object-based method to convert AI masks into clinical wound size estimates. The transformer-based TransNeXt showed the highest generalizability, while VWFormer and ConvNeXtS achieved the best physician-rated mask quality, and the framework was integrated into a custom telehealth system. Chronic wounds disproportionately affect the elderly and diabetic patients with limited mobility, so automated remote monitoring could reduce in-person physician visits and enable continuous tracking of wound healing. By standardizing evaluation and addressing real-world deployment factors, this work provides a practical reference for bringing wound segmentation AI closer to clinical telehealth use. All models processed at least one image per second on CPU, which the authors deem adequate for the intended application, and interpretability analysis typically showed activations concentrated in wound regions. Size retrieval accuracy was similar across the five best architectures and closely matched expert annotations, though the study is a benchmark rather than a new segmentation paradigm.

## [Unified Theory of Heterogeneous Federated Learning and FedACS Client Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11304) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper presents the first unified theoretical analysis of general heterogeneous federated learning, revealing the distinct mechanisms by which communication and computation heterogeneity cause objective inconsistency. It proposes Federated Heterogeneity-Aware Client Sampling (FedACS), which provably converges to the correct optimum at a rate of O(1/√R) even in dynamic heterogeneous environments. This work fills a gap in understanding how communication and computation heterogeneity jointly distort federated learning optimization, which could influence future algorithm design. FedACS outperforms state-of-the-art and category-specific baselines by 4.3%–36% while reducing communication costs by 22%–89% and computation loads by 14%–105%, making it highly practical for real-world deployments. The analysis is the first to handle arbitrary choices of local solvers, and FedACS is a universal method that eliminates all types of objective inconsistency. The convergence guarantee holds even in dynamic heterogeneous environments, and experiments across multiple datasets confirm consistent improvements over existing baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Federated learning (FL) trains a shared global model across many clients without centralizing their data, but clients often differ in communication bandwidth and computation speed. This heterogeneity can cause the global model to converge to an incorrect stationary point, a problem known as objective inconsistency. Prior work has studied communication or computation heterogeneity separately, but their joint effect has remained largely unexplored due to complex interactions.

## [ReasoningShield: Detecting Harmful Content in Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17244) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released ReasoningShield, a benchmark of 9.2K annotated (Query, CoT) pairs with structured stepwise risk analysis, plus lightweight 1B/3B moderation models trained with a two-stage strategy. The models achieve 91.8% F1 on the benchmark, outperforming LlamaGuard-4 by 35.6% and GPT-4o by 15.8%, while existing moderation tools degrade by up to 35.2% F1 on CoT moderation. This work exposes a critical safety blind spot in large reasoning models: harmful content can hide in intermediate reasoning steps even when final answers look benign, meaning current moderation pipelines may miss dangerous outputs. It provides both a benchmark and practical lightweight models, which could become a standard layer for safely deploying reasoning-based AI systems. The benchmark spans 10 risk categories, 3 safety levels, and 8 large reasoning models with diverse reasoning paradigms, split into a 7K-sample training set and a 2.2K human-annotated test set. The lightweight models generalize across reasoning paradigms and unseen scenarios, though the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) use Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, where the model explicitly writes out intermediate steps before giving a final answer, which improves performance on complex tasks like math and logic. Existing content moderation tools are typically trained to judge final outputs, not these intermediate traces, so they may fail when harmful content appears only in the reasoning steps. F1 score is a standard metric that balances precision and recall, and a large F1 drop indicates a moderation tool is missing many harmful cases or flagging too many benign ones.

A new arXiv paper (2505.22193v2) proposes two physics-inspired quantum diffusion model protocols: one based on quantum stochastic walks that mixes quantum and classical dynamics in the forward process, and another that generates images by exploiting the intrinsic noise of real IBM quantum hardware using only four qubits. Both approaches reportedly produce MNIST images with lower Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) than fully classical baselines. This work suggests that quantum noise, typically seen as an obstacle in near-term quantum computing, can be repurposed as a useful resource for generative AI, potentially opening new directions for large-scale quantum generative algorithms. It also demonstrates that even tiny, noisy quantum devices can outperform classical diffusion baselines on a standard image benchmark, which could encourage more hardware-aware quantum machine learning research. The first protocol uses the formalism of quantum stochastic walks to show that a specific interplay of quantum and classical dynamics yields statistically more robust models, while the second is implemented on real IBM quantum hardware with only four qubits. The experiments are limited to small-scale MNIST image generation, and the paper positions itself as a starting point rather than a scalable solution.

**Background**: Quantum diffusion models (QDMs) are a emerging paradigm that aims to use quantum properties to improve classical diffusion models, which generate data by gradually denoising random noise. Quantum stochastic walks generalize classical random walks by combining coherent quantum evolution with classical stochastic transitions, and Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) is a standard metric that compares the distribution of generated images with real images, where lower scores indicate better quality. Near-term quantum devices are noisy and limited in qubit count, which has made scaling QDMs difficult.

## [UniConFlow unifies constrained flow matching for certified robot motion planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02955) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released UniConFlow (arXiv:2506.02955v3), a unified constrained flow-matching framework that systematically incorporates both equality and inequality constraints for robot trajectory generation. It introduces a novel prescribed-time zeroing function that shapes a time-varying guidance field during inference, plus a violation-segment extraction protocol and a trajectory compression method for long-horizon, high-dimensional planning. Most existing generative planners handle constraints such as collision avoidance, actuation limits, and dynamic consistency individually or heuristically, so a unified framework could make certified motion planning more reliable and broadly applicable. Validated on a double inverted pendulum, real-to-sim car racing, and sim-to-real manipulation, it outperforms state-of-the-art generative planners and conventional optimization baselines on safety, kinodynamic consistency, and action feasibility. The prescribed-time zeroing function adapts the generation process to varying system models and task requirements, while the violation-segment extraction protocol localizes and refines only constraint-violating trajectory portions to cut computation. The trajectory compression method accelerates optimization in a reduced-dimensional space while preserving high-fidelity reconstruction after decoding.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Flow matching is a generative modeling technique that learns to transform simple noise distributions into complex data distributions by regressing a neural vector field toward a closed-form target, enabling simulation-free training of continuous normalizing flows. Constrained motion planning asks how to find a motion that satisfies constraints while avoiding obstacles or achieving other objectives. UniConFlow combines these ideas so that a single flow-matching model can generate trajectories that respect multiple hard constraints.

A new arXiv paper (2506.11849v3) introduces a method that combines Monte Carlo sampling with regression using any function family, such as XGBoost, to estimate Shapley values and other probabilistic values. The approach achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, with error up to 11x lower than Permutation SHAP, 6.5x lower than Kernel SHAP, and 3.4x lower than Leverage SHAP for Shapley values, and up to 390x lower error for general probabilistic values. The flexibility to use any efficiently computable function family makes the method widely applicable and could lead to more trustworthy AI systems. The method replaces linear regression with any function family whose probabilistic values can be computed efficiently, and with cross-fitting it produces unbiased estimates. Experiments across eight datasets demonstrate consistent state-of-the-art performance, though the approach is an incremental improvement rather than a paradigm shift.

## [LLM ORDER BY: A Semantic Operator with a Budget-Aware Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.00303) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the LLM ORDER BY semantic operator as a logical abstraction and systematically studies its physical implementations, including improved semantic sorting algorithms and a new semantic-aware external merge sort. It then proposes a budget-aware optimizer that uses heuristic rules, LLM-as-Judge evaluation, and consensus aggregation to dynamically select a near-optimal access path, achieving ranking accuracy on par with or better than the best static methods across all benchmarks. This work bridges database query optimization and LLM-powered data processing, showing that no single physical implementation of semantic sorting is universally optimal. It provides foundational insights for building robust, large-scale LLM-powered analytic systems, which is increasingly important as semantic operators become part of declarative data pipelines. The evaluation reveals a general scaling relationship between sorting cost and ordering quality for comparison-based algorithms, and the optimizer combines heuristic rules with LLM-as-Judge evaluation and consensus aggregation to pick access paths under a budget. The paper is an arXiv preprint (2509.00303v5) and has not yet undergone peer review or attracted community discussion.

External merge sort is a classic technique for sorting datasets too large to fit in memory by sorting chunks and merging them from disk. LLM-as-Judge is a widely used evaluation method where an LLM scores outputs against a rubric, and consensus aggregation combines multiple such judgments to reduce noise.

## [SPS-GAN Learns Hamiltonian Dynamics Without Known Phase Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23003) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Structured Phase Space GAN (SPS-GAN), a conditional generative model that learns structured Hamiltonian dynamics from Cartesian trajectories and video without requiring a known configuration space or fixed physical parameters. It combines a port-Hamiltonian backbone with adversarial conditional generation and uses a cyclic-coordinate loss to infer latent mechanical structure, recovering degrees of freedom and canonical coordinates directly from raw observations. This work addresses a key limitation of mechanics-inspired neural models, which typically assume a known configuration space and deterministic prediction from a prescribed initial condition. By learning latent mechanical structure from both trajectories and video, SPS-GAN could broaden the applicability of physics-informed machine learning to real-world systems where the underlying phase space is unknown. SPS-GAN represents conservative, dissipative, and forced systems within a single architecture, and experiments on simulated and real-world systems show improved trajectory accuracy over supervised dynamics baselines and better video consistency over generative baselines. It consistently recovers correct latent mechanical coordinates across observation modalities and dynamical regimes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Hamiltonian mechanics reformulates classical mechanics using generalized coordinates and conjugate momenta, defining a phase space that describes a system's state. Port-Hamiltonian systems extend this framework to model energy exchange and interconnection across physical domains, while generative adversarial networks (GANs) learn to generate data by pitting a generator against a discriminator. SPS-GAN merges these ideas to learn structured dynamics without prior knowledge of the phase space.

## [HAFixAgent Uses Repository History to Boost LLM Program Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01047) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HAFixAgent injects blame-derived repository history into an LLM-based repair loop, achieving up to 64.3% improvement over non-history baselines and outperforming RepairAgent by 56.6% and BIRCH-feedback by 47.1% on the strongest model. The study evaluates 854 Defects4J (Java) and 501 BugsInPy (Python) bugs under both perfect and spectrum-based fault localization. This work demonstrates that repository history is a widely available and complementary signal for automated program repair, significantly improving repair success even with weaker models. It could shift how agentic APR systems are designed, encouraging broader adoption of history-aware techniques in software engineering tools. The three history heuristics are complementary, each repairing different bug subsets, and their hybrid significantly outperforms non-history (McNemar's test, p < 0.001) under both localization settings. Individual heuristics cost no more than non-history in agent steps or tokens, while the hybrid cascade roughly doubles the cost of a single run.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Automated program repair (APR) aims to automatically generate correct patches for software bugs without human intervention, traditionally using search-based or template-based methods. Recent approaches leverage large language models (LLMs) and agent-based systems, and prior work has shown that repository history (e.g., commit logs, blame information) can help repair single-line bugs. Defects4J and BugsInPy are standard benchmarks for Java and Python, respectively, providing real-world bugs with test suites for reproducible evaluation.

## [COBALT: Offline Trajectories Boost Multi-Turn Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03806) ⭐️ 7.0/10

COBALT introduces a contextual bandit learning method that reuses offline multi-turn trajectories to train LLMs for code generation, treating the task as a one-step recoverable Markov decision process. On LiveCodeBench, it improves R1-Distill 8B and Qwen3 8B by up to 9.0 and 6.2 absolute Pass@1 points, matching two multi-turn online RL baselines (GRPO and VeRPO). Online reinforcement learning for multi-turn code generation is costly and unstable, so an offline-trajectory approach that matches online RL performance could make training more accessible and reproducible. The demonstrated robustness to misleading execution feedback also suggests better reliability in real-world coding agents. COBALT collects trajectories with a reference LLM, splits them into partial trajectories, and trains the model to complete each partial trajectory via single-step generation during online bandit learning. A perturbation-based analysis of in-context reward hacking shows improved robustness to misleading execution feedback, and code/data are available at the OSU-NLP-Group GitHub repository.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-turn code generation involves an LLM iteratively writing, executing, and refining code based on feedback, which is naturally modeled as a Markov decision process. Online reinforcement learning trains such models by repeatedly generating rollouts, but this is expensive and unstable; offline RL instead learns from pre-collected trajectories. Contextual bandits are a simplified RL setting where the model chooses a single action given a context, and Pass@1 measures whether the first generated solution passes all tests.

## [Offline RL Theory Extended to Parametric Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23811) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2602.23811v5) extends theoretical guarantees for offline reinforcement learning from finite, small action spaces to parameterized policy classes over large or continuous action spaces. It identifies contextual coupling as the core difficulty when extending mirror descent to parameterized policies, and shows that connecting mirror descent to natural policy gradient yields novel analyses, guarantees, and algorithmic insights, including a surprising unification between offline RL and imitation learning. Prior computationally tractable offline RL algorithms such as PSPI only worked for finite and small action spaces and required actors to be implicitly induced from critic functions, which does not match the standalone policy parameterization used in practice. By extending guarantees to parameterized policies over large or continuous action spaces, this work could influence the design of practical offline RL algorithms and deepen the theoretical connection between offline RL and imitation learning. The paper builds on the pessimism-based theoretical foundations of Xie et al. (2021) and targets oracle-efficient algorithms, but its contribution is primarily theoretical rather than an empirical benchmark. The key technical move is linking mirror descent to natural policy gradient to handle contextual coupling, which enables standalone policy parameterization instead of implicitly induced actors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Offline reinforcement learning aims to learn a good policy from a fixed dataset of historical interactions without further environment exploration, which is important for domains where online trial-and-error is costly or unsafe. A central theoretical idea is pessimism: the algorithm penalizes uncertain actions so that it does not overestimate the value of actions poorly covered by the data. Mirror descent is a classical optimization method often used to update policies state by state, and PSPI is a prior oracle-efficient algorithm that relies on this state-wise approach but is limited to small, finite action spaces.

<a id="item-228"></a>
## [GD in Two-Neuron ReLU Networks Converges to Robustness Margin at Θ(1/ln(t))](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02095) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.02095v2) proves that gradient descent in a minimal binary classification setting—a two-neuron ReLU network trained on two instances—converges to an optimal adversarial robustness margin, but at a prohibitively slow rate of Θ(1/ln(t)). This is claimed to be the first explicit lower bound on the convergence rate of the robustness margin in a non-linear model, and empirical simulations show the same tight rate across multiple natural network initializations. This result shows that even in an extremely simplified non-linear model, gradient descent can be inherently slow at maximizing the robustness margin, suggesting that slow margin convergence may be a pervasive failure mode rather than an artifact of complex architectures. It provides a theoretical foundation for understanding why adversarially robust training is often difficult and could motivate new optimization or training strategies that account for this logarithmic-rate bottleneck. The analysis is carried out by rigorously tracking GD trajectories across the distinct activation patterns of the two-neuron ReLU model, tightly bounding the decision boundary's trajectory despite the non-linearity. The Θ(1/ln(t)) rate is a strict lower bound, meaning no faster convergence is possible in this setting, and the same tight rate is observed empirically across multiple natural initializations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Gradient descent (GD) is the workhorse optimization algorithm for training neural networks, and its convergence rate describes how quickly the training objective or a derived quantity approaches its optimum. ReLU (rectified linear unit) is a widely used activation function that outputs the input if positive and zero otherwise, making networks non-linear. Adversarial robustness margin refers to the distance between the decision boundary and the training points; a larger margin generally means the model is more resistant to small input perturbations. Prior convergence-rate lower bounds for GD were mostly established for convex or linear settings, leaving non-linear models less understood.

## [Agentic P2P Networks: Sharing Capabilities, Not Just Content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03753) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2603.03753v2) outlines the networking foundations for Agentic Peer-to-Peer (P2P) Networks, in which Client-Side Autonomous Agents (CSAAs) on edge devices collaborate directly by exchanging dynamic capabilities rather than static content. The paper proposes a plane-based reference architecture that decouples connectivity, semantic discovery, and execution, introduces signed soft-state capability descriptors for intent-aware routing under drift, and presents a tiered verification spectrum (reputation signals, canary probes, cryptographic evidence) to align trust costs with task risks. This work addresses the architectural gap created by AI's shift from centralized cloud APIs to local inference on edge devices, where persistent personal agents increasingly need to cooperate without a central broker. If adopted, such agentic P2P designs could shape how future decentralized AI systems handle discovery, trust, and risk-aware execution across heterogeneous, potentially adversarial peers. The proposed descriptors are "soft-state," meaning they are periodically refreshed and expire if not renewed, and they are signed to support intent-aware routing as agent capabilities drift over time. The tiered verification spectrum ranges from Tier 1 reputation signals and Tier 2 canary probes to Tier 3 cryptographic evidence, and a proxy-level discrete-event simulation reportedly shows improved workflow success under poisoning and drift attacks with only modest control-plane overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Classic peer-to-peer overlays like BitTorrent exchange static, hash-indexed content, which makes discovery and verification relatively straightforward. In contrast, autonomous AI agents advertise dynamic, heterogeneous capabilities that may be unsafe or change over time, so the network must handle semantic discovery, capability drift, and adversarial behavior. "Soft state" is a long-established networking concept in which state is refreshed by periodic messages and otherwise expires, useful for efficiency but not essential to correctness. The paper is a preprint and has not yet undergone peer review.

Researchers released CORE-Seg, an end-to-end framework that combines reasoning with segmentation for complex lesions, along with ComLesion-14K, described as the first diverse Chain-of-Thought benchmark for this task. The method achieves a mean Dice of 37.06%, 14.89% higher than the second-best baseline, while cutting the failure rate to 18.42%. This work signals a shift in medical image segmentation from pure visual pattern matching toward cognitive reasoning, potentially improving how AI handles ambiguous or complex lesions where traditional models fail. It also provides a new benchmark that could standardize evaluation of reasoning-driven segmentation across the medical imaging and AI communities. CORE-Seg uses a Semantic-Guided Prompt Adapter and a progressive training strategy from supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to GRPO, with an adaptive dual-granularity reward mechanism to mitigate reward sparsity. As an arXiv preprint, its results have not yet undergone peer review or community validation.

## [Mixture-Greedy Beats UCB for Online Generative Model Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21716) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.21716v3) shows that a simple Mixture-Greedy strategy without any UCB exploration bonus converges faster and achieves better performance than UCB-based methods for online selection among generative models under diversity-aware metrics such as FID and Vendi. The authors provide theoretical insight showing that diversity-aware objectives induce implicit exploration by favoring interior mixtures, yielding sublinear regret guarantees. This finding challenges the common assumption that explicit optimism (UCB) is necessary in multi-armed bandits, and could influence how practitioners design online model selection systems for generative AI, where sampling from suboptimal models is costly. It suggests that simpler greedy strategies may suffice when the objective itself encourages exploration. The paper observes across multiple datasets and evaluation metrics that the UCB term consistently slows convergence and reduces sample efficiency, particularly for widely used metrics like FID and Vendi where tight confidence bounds are difficult to construct. The theoretical analysis shows that under structural conditions, diversity-aware objectives favor interior mixtures, leading to sampling of all arms and sublinear regret.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-armed bandit (MAB) is a classic reinforcement learning problem that models the exploration-exploitation tradeoff: a decision maker repeatedly chooses among multiple options (arms) with unknown reward distributions to maximize cumulative reward. Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) is a widely used strategy that balances exploration and exploitation by selecting actions based on empirical means and uncertainty estimates. In generative model selection, the goal is to choose among multiple generative models online, and diversity-aware metrics like FID (Fréchet Inception Distance) and Vendi score evaluate the quality and diversity of generated samples. Unlike classical best-arm identification, a mixture of generators can outperform any individual model under such metrics.

## [AnomalyAgent Uses Tool-Augmented RL for Industrial Anomaly Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07900) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released AnomalyAgent, an agentic system that generates realistic industrial anomalies through self-reflection, knowledge retrieval, and iterative refinement using five tools: Prompt Generation, Image Generation, Quality Evaluation, Knowledge Retrieval, and Mask Generation. It is trained with a two-stage framework of supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning, and on MVTec-AD it achieves IS/IC-L of 2.10/0.33, 57.0% classification accuracy with ResNet34, and 99.3%/74.2% image/pixel-level AP with a simple UNet, surpassing all zero-shot state-of-the-art methods. Industrial anomaly detection is often bottlenecked by scarce real defect data, and this work shows that an agentic, tool-augmented reinforcement learning loop can synthesize more semantically realistic anomalies than single-step generative baselines. If validated beyond MVTec-AD, such closed-loop synthesis could improve downstream detection and localization models across manufacturing quality control. The training uses a three-part reward mechanism: task rewards for anomaly quality and location rationality, reflection rewards for improving synthesis prompts, and behavioral rewards for trajectory adherence. Evaluation is limited to the MVTec-AD dataset and uses a simple UNet and ResNet34, so generalization to other industrial domains and larger detectors remains to be tested; code is available at https://github.com/sssjmmm/AnomalyAgent.

## [LensVLM selectively expands compressed images for efficient VLM text processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07019) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LensVLM introduces an inference framework and post-training recipe that lets vision language models scan compressed images and then selectively expand only the relevant ones to their uncompressed form using learned tools. Built on Qwen3.5-9B-Base, it maintains accuracy comparable to full-text processing at 4.3x effective compression and outperforms retrieval-based, text- and visual-compression baselines up to 10.1x compression across seven text QA benchmarks. This work addresses a key bottleneck in using VLMs to process text as rendered images: accuracy degrades rapidly as compression increases because characters become indistinguishable. By enabling selective expansion, it could make multimodal document and code understanding far more efficient without sacrificing accuracy, impacting anyone deploying VLMs for large-scale text processing. LensVLM generalizes to multimodal document and code understanding tasks, with accuracy gains over baselines growing as compression increases. Analysis shows training makes visual compression robust to rendering choices, and as compression grows the model relies more on expanded content; text expansion is preferable for rendered text, while high-resolution image expansion suits native documents where layout cues matter.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Vision Language Models (VLMs) are AI systems that jointly interpret images and text, extending large language models to multimodal inputs. A common approach renders text as images so the VLM's image encoder maps fixed-size images to a fixed number of visual tokens, avoiding long token sequences. Varying rendering resolution acts as a compression knob, but characters shrink below the encoder's effective resolution at high compression, hurting accuracy. LensVLM builds on Qwen3.5-9B-Base, a small but capable model that runs on consumer GPUs.

## [GraphDPO Generalizes DPO to Preference Graphs for LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08037) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper introduces Graph Direct Preference Optimization (GraphDPO), a structured generalization of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) that operates over directed acyclic preference graphs induced by multiple rollouts per prompt. GraphDPO encodes dominance relations as edges and optimizes a graph-structured Plackett-Luce-inspired objective that aggregates supervision over dominated neighborhoods, recovering standard DPO as a special case. This addresses a real limitation of pairwise DPO, which discards transitivity and can produce redundant or conflicting supervision when multiple rollouts per prompt are available. By exploiting richer preference structure, GraphDPO could lead to more stable and scalable alignment for reasoning and program synthesis tasks, benefiting both researchers and practitioners in LLM alignment. GraphDPO handles tied and incomparable responses via an equivalence-class construction: responses with identical preference signals form graph layers, incomparable responses share a layer without an edge, and absent edges contribute zero loss, preventing spurious gradients. It maintains linear per-prompt complexity via efficient log-sum-exp aggregation and optionally incorporates ground-truth anchoring with an annealed schedule that stabilizes early training while gradually relaxing oracle supervision.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a popular method for aligning language models with human preferences by directly optimizing on pairwise comparisons, avoiding the need for a separate reward model and reinforcement learning. The Plackett-Luce model is a probabilistic framework for ranking items based on comparisons, often used in learning-to-rank tasks. A directed acyclic graph (DAG) is a directed graph with no cycles, commonly used to represent dependency or dominance relations. This paper combines these ideas to model multiple rollouts per prompt as a preference graph, capturing transitive dominance relations that pairwise DPO ignores.

A new arXiv preprint (2605.10268v2) introduces MemReread, a method that replaces intermediate retrieval with memory-guided rereading for agentic long-context reasoning. Its MemReread-4B model outperforms strong baselines such as DeepSeek-V4-Pro by +1.6pp on 2WikiMultiHopQA while keeping linear time complexity. This addresses a core weakness of retrieval-augmented agent-memory systems, where evidence is often lost during memory overwriting or corrupted by invalid queries. MemReread builds on streaming reading and triggers question decomposition and targeted rereading only when the final memory is insufficient, recovering indirect evidence discarded earlier. A reinforcement learning framework improves length extrapolation and adaptively sets the number of rereading passes based on task complexity, giving flexible control over computational overhead.

## [SBSRL: Sampling-Based Safe Reinforcement Learning with Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19469) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Sampling-Based Safe Reinforcement Learning (SBSRL), a model-based RL algorithm that enforces safety constraints jointly across a finite set of sampled dynamics, approximating an intractable worst-case optimization. The paper derives high-probability safety guarantees throughout learning and a finite-time sample complexity bound, and validates the approach in simulation and on real robotic hardware. Safe exploration is a fundamental barrier to deploying RL in the real world, especially in robotics where unsafe actions can cause harm. SBSRL's theoretical safety guarantees and real-robot validation could make RL more viable for safety-critical applications such as autonomous systems and human-interacting robots. SBSRL introduces an exploration strategy based on constraining epistemic uncertainty, eliminating the need for explicit exploration bonuses, and extends to deep-ensemble implementations that scale to high-dimensional continuous control. The safety guarantees hold under regularity conditions, and the sample complexity bound is finite-time for recovering a near-optimal policy.

**Background**: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) learns a model of environment dynamics to plan and optimize actions, reducing the need for costly real-world interactions. Safe RL aims to ensure agents avoid unsafe states during learning, often formalized as constrained RL where safety requirements are separate from the task objective. Epistemic uncertainty reflects the agent's lack of knowledge about the environment and is commonly used to guide safe exploration.

## [Stochastic MeanFlow Policies Enable One-Step Entropy-Regularized RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21282) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.21282v3) introduces Stochastic MeanFlow Policies (SMFP), a one-step generative policy class that combines MeanFlow-based noise-to-action mappings with Gaussian reparameterization. The method provides an analytic entropy lower bound that enables entropy-regularized mirror-descent reinforcement learning, and it outperforms Gaussian and generative baselines across seven MuJoCo benchmarks with single-step inference. This work bridges two active research areas—one-step generative modeling and entropy-regularized policy optimization—by showing that expressive multimodal policies can be trained with tractable entropy control and efficient single-step sampling. It could benefit reinforcement learning practitioners who need both the expressiveness of generative policies and the stability of entropy-regularized mirror descent. SMFP uses a latent mapping to capture diverse action modes while a learnable conditional Gaussian scale provides an analytic entropy lower bound, which regulates stochasticity alongside direct value guidance and advantage-weighted MeanFlow regression. Additional experiments demonstrate multimodal action modeling and effective exploration in sparse-reward navigation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: MeanFlow is a recent one-step generative modeling framework that replaces the instantaneous velocity used in Flow Matching with an interval-averaged (mean) velocity, enabling direct noise-to-data mapping in a single step. Entropic mirror descent is a policy optimization approach that regularizes the policy update with an entropy term to encourage exploration and stability. MuJoCo is a widely used physics simulation benchmark suite for continuous control reinforcement learning. This paper combines these ideas to create a policy class that is both expressive and efficiently trainable.

## [VITAL: Dual-Supervised Latent Reasoning for Medical Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28422) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose VITAL, a latent-space reasoning framework for medical multimodal LLMs that uses visual-semantic dual supervision: an auxiliary text decoder reconstructs reasoning chains from latent states, while a visual projector regresses ROI features from a frozen medical vision encoder. Both modules are discarded at inference for zero overhead but can be re-attached post-hoc for interpretability, and the team built a 61K dataset spanning 9 imaging modalities, outperforming baselines on 7 benchmarks. Medical visual question answering demands both accuracy and interpretability, yet existing latent reasoning methods suffer from modality collapse and opaque hidden states that clinicians cannot trust. VITAL's dual textual and visual explanations, achieved without inference overhead, could make latent reasoning practical for clinical deployment and set a new standard for interpretable medical AI. The framework addresses modality collapse, insufficient visual supervision, and train-inference mismatch by supervising latent states with both a text decoder and a visual projector tied to a frozen medical vision encoder. Its 61K dataset exceeds prior medical visual latent reasoning datasets by an order of magnitude, and results are competitive with trillion-parameter proprietary models, though the work remains a preprint without peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Latent reasoning lets a model reason over continuous hidden states instead of generating explicit chain-of-thought tokens, which avoids the language bottleneck and inference cost of token-by-token reasoning. Multimodal LLMs combine vision and language for tasks like medical VQA, but they often suffer from modality collapse, where one modality dominates and unimodal models can outperform multimodal ones. VITAL builds on these ideas by adding dual supervision so the latent reasoning process can be explained after the fact.

## [MyoSem aligns EMG signals with natural-language action semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00174) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MyoSem introduces an EMG-action semantic alignment framework that maps hand electromyography (EMG) signals and multi-view natural-language action descriptions into a shared feature space, enabling bidirectional EMG-text retrieval. It was evaluated under seven protocols on NinaPro DB2, EMG2Pose, and NinaPro DB2→DB3, covering random splits, user shifts, held-out action classes, and able-bodied-to-amputee transfer, achieving the strongest overall bidirectional retrieval performance among evaluated methods. This work moves EMG-based hand action understanding from fixed-label recognition toward language-queryable semantic retrieval, which could improve prosthetic control, wearable interaction, and human-computer interfaces by allowing users to query actions in natural language. It also connects EMG signal processing with multimodal learning and NLP, opening new directions for cross-modal representation learning in biomedical sensing. MyoSem builds semantic prototypes from multi-view descriptions, encodes EMG with local activation-aware representations, and uses semantic queries to aggregate local EMG evidence for bidirectional retrieval. Ablations and representation analyses show how semantic prototypes, activation-aware encoding, and semantic queries shape the learned space.

**Background**: Electromyography (EMG) records the electrical activity produced by skeletal muscles and is widely used for hand gesture recognition, prosthetic control, and wearable interaction. Existing EMG methods typically treat hand action understanding as prediction over fixed gesture labels, leaving EMG signals and action-semantic text unaligned in a common feature space. NinaPro DB2 and EMG2Pose are public datasets providing surface EMG recordings, with EMG2Pose also including ground-truth hand motion capture data.

## [Teacher Freezing Enables Stable Self On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03532) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.03532v2) systematically studies teacher update schedules in self on-policy distillation, showing through a controlled sweep on Qwen3-8B that isolation periods — complete teacher freezing between updates — are the key structural property enabling stable learning, rather than teacher age. The authors introduce a diagnostic framework of temporal KL structure, refresh shock, and length-tail risk that uncovers a failure mode called state-oblivious collapse, and propose Consolidation-Gated Teacher Refresh (CGTR), which achieves zero collapse and the best final score on all four tasks (Chemistry, Biology, Physics, ToolUse). Self on-policy distillation is increasingly used to train LLMs without external supervision, so understanding which teacher update schedules remain stable is directly relevant to anyone building self-improving training pipelines. The identification of state-oblivious collapse — a failure invisible under short-horizon evaluation — warns practitioners that schedules tuned on short runs can catastrophically fail in long-horizon training, and CGTR offers a concrete, retuning-free remedy. The paper shows that optimal short-horizon fixed schedules fail catastrophically under long-horizon training because a clock-driven refresh can copy a transiently drifting student into the teacher in a single irreversible step, a mechanism distinct from EMA's chronic contamination. CGTR preserves isolation periods while gating each refresh on joint evidence of reward improvement and length-tail safety, using a single shared parameter set with no per-dataset retuning and self-regulating its refresh frequency to each task's learning dynamics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Self on-policy distillation trains a student policy against a teacher derived from its own parameter history, meaning the teacher is periodically refreshed from the student rather than being a fixed external model. The teacher's update schedule governs the temporal coupling between teacher and student, but this schedule had not previously been systematically studied as a stability variable. Qwen3-8B is the specific model used in the controlled schedule sweep, and EMA (exponential moving average) is a common alternative teacher-update mechanism whose chronic contamination the paper contrasts with state-oblivious collapse.

## [ADE-FEMU: Differentiable FEM Learns Constitutive Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05199) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present ADE-FEMU, an end-to-end differentiable finite element framework that embeds a constitutive neural network inside a GPU-accelerated JAX-FEM nonlinear solver and identifies its parameters via gradient-based minimization of a measurement-mismatch loss. It is demonstrated on two architectures — a grey-box polyconvex Constitutive Artificial Neural Network (CANN) and a white-box, interpretable CANN — and evaluated on three open experimental datasets, including full DIC of a perforated tensile specimen and a heterogeneous matrix-inclusion system generalized to twenty-two unseen samples. This work removes the need for analytic adjoints or offline surrogate models by computing Newton tangent stiffness and loss gradients automatically through forward- and reverse-mode automatic differentiation, enabling constitutive model identification from full-field deformation data rather than homogeneous stress-strain experiments. It sits at the intersection of computational mechanics and machine learning, and could improve how hyperelastic material models are calibrated and validated in structural engineering. The framework focuses on incompressible isotropic hyperelasticity and supports training on structural data with limited kinetic information, including a reduced-data scenario using only a one-dimensional stretch profile and a global force-displacement curve. The white-box CANN provides phenomenologically interpretable strain-energy terms, while the grey-box CANN offers high flexibility as a polyconvex fully connected model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Finite element model updating (FEMU) is an inverse parameter identification technique that iteratively refines parameters of a finite element model — such as material properties, geometry, or boundary conditions — to minimize discrepancies with measured data, forming part of verification and validation of numerical models. Constitutive neural networks are neural network representations of constitutive laws that map kinematic inputs to stress, flux, or stiffness outputs while honoring physical principles, an idea dating back to the early 1990s. JAX-FEM is a differentiable finite element package based on JAX that enables gradient-based optimization and GPU acceleration for problems such as topology optimization and crystal plasticity.

## [LLM Agents Peer-Review Each Other to Boost Medical QA Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15419) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.15419v2) proposes a multi-agent peer-reviewed reasoning method in which multiple LLM agents independently generate chain-of-thought reasoning and candidate answers, then act as peer reviewers to evaluate each other's reasoning for factual correctness and logical soundness, selecting the highest-rated chain to produce the final answer. Tested with five state-of-the-art LLMs (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Phi-4, DeepSeek-LLM-7B, GPT-oss-20B) on HeadQA, MedQA-USMLE, and PubMedQA, the method achieved an average accuracy of 0.820, beating the strongest single model (0.777) and majority voting ensembles (up to 0.789). This work addresses a critical challenge in high-stakes medical question answering: LLMs can produce plausible but incorrect reasoning, and simple majority voting only aggregates answers without evaluating reasoning quality. By having models act as both solvers and evaluators, the approach improves accuracy, interpretability, and robustness, offering a promising direction for trustworthy biomedical AI systems where reliability is paramount. The method scales effectively with more participating models, and peer assessments reliably distinguish high- from low-quality reasoning chains. However, the abstract does not detail computational costs, latency, or potential failure modes of the peer-review process, and the approach is an incremental methodological advance rather than a fundamental breakthrough.

This paper combines these ideas into a peer-review framework specifically for medical QA.

## [PACT Uses Expert Traces as Training Signals for Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16215) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2606.16215v2) introduces PACT, a Privileged Trace Co-Training framework that uses expert traces only as training-time optimization signals rather than rollout-time hints for multi-turn tool-use agents. PACT combines a trace-conditioned RL surrogate with a component-aware SFT loss and prompt-only anchoring, and experiments on FTRL, BFCL, and ToolHop show it consistently outperforms strong SFT- and RL-based baselines. Post-training multi-turn tool-use agents is a central challenge for building capable AI assistants, and PACT offers a way to get dense supervision from expert traces without over-constraining the model to fixed trajectories. If validated, this could influence how future agent training pipelines balance reinforcement learning and supervised fine-tuning. PACT keeps rollout generation prompt-only, then guides optimization through two complementary signals: a trace-conditioned RL surrogate that evaluates prompt-only rollouts under expert-trace context, and a component-aware SFT loss that provides annealed process supervision over reasoning and tool-call components. To reduce over-reliance on the training-only trace context, it incorporates prompt-only anchoring with standard prompt-only RL updates.

Reinforcement learning for such agents often suffers from sparse rewards and weak credit assignment, while supervised fine-tuning on expert traces provides dense process supervision but can over-constrain the model to fixed trajectories. PACT is a preprint and has not yet been peer-reviewed or validated by community discussion.

## [w²VLA decouples declarative and procedural knowledge in robot policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21496) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.21496v2) proposes w²VLA, a Vision-Language-Action model that restructures information flow so that visual, spatial, and skill information modulates the robot state sequence compositionally instead of feeding all multimodal tokens into a single opaque transformer action expert. The authors report that this modular design decouples knowledge representations and enables robust behavior cloning plus zero-shot skill transfer to dissimilar, unseen objects. Current VLA models achieve strong in-distribution manipulation but remain brittle to minor spatial, semantic, and task variations, which makes data collection for every new object intractable. If decoupling declarative semantics from procedural skills truly generalizes, it could substantially reduce the demonstration burden for generalist robotic agents and shift VLA design away from monolithic action experts. The core technical change is architectural: rather than passing all VLM encoder tokens into a large transformer-based action expert, w²VLA modulates the robot state sequence with visual, spatial, and skill information in a compositional and interpretable manner. The paper is a preprint (v2, replace-cross) and has not been peer-reviewed, and no community discussion was provided.

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take an image observation and a text instruction and directly output low-level robot actions, typically built by fine-tuning a pre-trained Vision-Language Model (VLM) first on large-scale robot datasets and then on scenario-specific demonstrations. The concept was pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in July 2023, and the dominant paradigm feeds VLM tokens into an action decoder. The declarative/procedural distinction comes from cognitive science: declarative knowledge covers concepts and entity semantics, while procedural knowledge covers how to perform a task.

## [RoboMME-Interference Benchmarks Robot Memory Under Distractor Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22338) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced RoboMME-Interference, a cross-session benchmark built on RoboMME that inserts a controlled number of unrelated distractor sessions between a query episode and its relevant prior demonstration. Testing RoboMME's released memory-augmented π0.5 variants unmodified, they found perceptual memory variants decay steadily as distractors accumulate, while subgoal variants retain more of their smaller gains, and adding a visual-similarity retrieval step restores the strongest perceptual variant's no-distractor success rate at every interference level. Most robot-memory benchmarks rely on single episodes or short contexts, so this work exposes a real gap between benchmark performance and the long-context, distraction-heavy conditions of real deployments. It signals that current memory-augmented VLA systems largely fail at long-context robustness, which matters for anyone building robots expected to remember information across many sessions. The benchmark is built on RoboMME (Dai et al., 2026) and evaluates memory-augmented π0.5 variants by feeding session history to the VLA and measuring accuracy. The retrieval approach selects the section of history most visually similar to the robot's current view and passes only that section to the policy, which fully restores no-distractor success at all interference levels.

RoboMME-Interference extends this by adding distractor sessions to test whether memory systems can retrieve the right past experience amid irrelevant history.

## [Spec Growth Engine Targets Context Explosion and Spec-Code Drift in AI Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27045) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2606.27045v2) introduces the Spec Growth Engine, a lightweight framework combining a machine-readable spec graph, a Spine context assembler, a vertical-slice growth protocol, and a drift gate. It explicitly targets two failure modes of AI coding agents: context explosion and silent spec-code drift. As AI coding agents accelerate implementation, the gap between specification and code can widen invisibly, and ever-larger repositories strain finite context windows. A framework that makes spec-code divergence a blocking merge condition could improve reliability and trust in AI-assisted development workflows. The spec graph separates contract from design at each node, the Spine assembler scopes agent context to an ownership path, and the growth protocol enforces hardest-first ordering. The design synthesizes established principles such as Parnas information hiding, C4, ADRs, Walking Skeleton, Reflexion Models, and Fitness Functions, while avoiding heavyweight frameworks like RUP or MDA.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Spec-driven development treats a formal, machine-readable specification as the authoritative source of truth from which implementation, testing, and documentation are derived. AI coding agents can generate code quickly, but they must reason over an entire repository, and as the context window fills, output quality degrades; meanwhile, code can evolve while the specification does not, creating invisible drift. The Spec Growth Engine proposes a lean, code-coupled, machine-enforced architecture to address both problems.

## [105 Language Models Converge on Identical Answers, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12796) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv study (2607.12796v4) tested 105 language models from over twenty labs with 96 single-turn prompts asking them to name a category word (e.g., "Name a tree"), repeated eight times per model and scored by exact match. When asked to "pick a word," 46% of models chose "serendipity," and in 28 of 96 categories a single answer accounted for at least 80% of all responses. The finding that model size and tuning do not reduce this conformity suggests that answer-choice convergence is a systemic property of current LLMs, which has implications for diversity, bias, and how we evaluate model outputs. It also raises concerns that models may be less diverse than humans in creative or open-ended tasks. The study introduces "answer-choice surprisal," the average -log2 probability of a model's answers under the pooled answers of all other models, and finds that heavily post-trained assistants from major labs are the most conformist, while lightly post-trained and persona-tuned models are the most divergent. Against human norms, the field is more concentrated than people in 18 of 20 shared categories.

A new arXiv paper (2607.16004v2) decouples congestion detection from control by pairing a random-forest violation pre-classifier with an actor-critic controller, rather than using a single end-to-end reinforcement-learning policy. Tested on a real low-voltage grid under synthetic low-observability scenarios, the framework reduced total violation magnitude by 98.9% with accurate grid parameters, and performance stayed nearly unchanged under measurement noise. Rising photovoltaic generation, EV charging, and heat-pump demand are straining low-voltage distribution grids, so curative curtailment methods that work under sparse observability and imperfect models are increasingly critical for grid operators. This decoupled design offers a more robust alternative to end-to-end RL, which could make RL-based congestion management more practical for real smart-grid deployments. The approach is evaluated on a real low-voltage grid using synthetic future operating scenarios with low observability and controllability. While measurement noise barely affected performance, grid-model mismatch proved more challenging, though the controller still mitigated most violations under the tested mismatch assumptions.

Reinforcement learning, particularly actor-critic methods that combine a policy network with a value estimator, has been explored for such control tasks, but end-to-end policies can be brittle under partial observability. ## [Medical foundation models converge less under label supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20274) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A large-scale arXiv study evaluated over 750,000 images from 14 datasets across 5 imaging modalities, 18 public models, and 101 trained models, finding that medical foundation models share surprisingly few nearest neighbors in their image representations. Two public models shared on average only 0.091 of the 10 nearest neighbors of a chest radiograph, and label-supervised models converged least, sharing just 0.062 to 0.100 when differing only in random seed. This challenges the common assumption that different medical foundation models represent images alike, which underlies the practice of swapping models when new versions appear. The finding has significant implications for model selection, reproducibility, and clinical deployment, since classifiers and imaging biomarkers fitted on one model's embeddings may not transfer reliably to another. Agreement was measured as mutual k-nearest-neighbor overlap, and it was not significantly associated with accuracy; on chest radiographs it depended more on the source dataset and radiographic view than on the findings. A linear mapping fitted on 4,096 unlabeled radiographs transferred classifiers for 14 chest findings with 0.987 of their original AUROC, and convergence depended on the pretraining objective, with self-distillation, masked image modeling, or contrastive pretraining sharing 0.411 to 0.802.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Medical foundation models are large deep learning systems pretrained on broad medical data, and their embeddings are commonly used as features for downstream diagnostic classifiers and imaging biomarkers. Practitioners often replace one foundation model with a newer version assuming the representations are interchangeable. Mutual k-nearest-neighbor overlap measures how much two models agree by computing the share of an image's nearest neighbors that are common to both models.

## [CovGPO Breaks Credit Trap in Long-Horizon LLM Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22724) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.22724v2) identifies a sampling imbalance in group-based policy optimization for LLM agents, where repeated low-effect actions dominate the high-probability region and produce many all-failed rollout groups that yield no learning signal. To address this, the authors propose Coverage-conditioned Group Policy Optimization (CovGPO), which uses first-visit observation coverage only when every sample in a group receives zero outcome reward, assigning higher relative advantages to state-exploring actions. This work tackles a fundamental credit assignment problem in reinforcement learning for LLM agents on difficult long-horizon tasks, where sparse outcome rewards and failure-dominated sampling create a self-perpetuating credit trap. By breaking that trap without corrupting the original reward signal, CovGPO could make RL training more sample-efficient and effective for real-world agentic benchmarks such as ALFWorld and WebShop. CovGPO applies first-visit observation coverage as an auxiliary advantage signal only inside all-failed groups, leaving the original reward signal intact elsewhere; experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show consistent improvements over group-based baselines, with particularly large gains on hard tasks. The paper is a replace-cross submission, and its abstract is truncated in the provided content, so full technical details such as exact coverage definitions and hyperparameters are not yet visible.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Group-based policy optimization, such as GRPO-style methods, trains LLM agents by comparing trajectories or steps within a group and using relative advantages to update the policy, which works well when rewards are sparse but outcomes vary. In long-horizon agentic tasks, however, the agent may repeatedly take low-effect actions, so many groups contain only failures and provide no comparative signal. First-visit coverage is a classic reinforcement learning concept that measures whether an agent reaches new states or observations, and it is often used to encourage exploration and improve sample complexity.

## [First-Order In-Context Shapley Enables Scalable LLM Data Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22766) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces an inference-only valuation pipeline that computes the first-order term of an in-context cooperative game over semantic k-NN neighborhoods in O(k) forward passes without parameter updates, achieving a 19x compute reduction while correlating positively with multi-shot Monte-Carlo in-context Shapley on 100% of targets. It also defines three per-record signals—Exemplifying Advantage, Exemplified Advantage, and a One-of-a-Kind Score—and shows that pre-filtering restricts LLM twin arbitration to 1%-5% of records while enriching confirmed errors 4.5x on HelpSteer2 and 2.2x on Anthropic HH-RLHF. Data quality is a growing bottleneck for post-training alignment, and this method offers a practical, inference-only way to audit preference and instruction-tuning corpora for label noise, safety inversions, and inter-annotator contradictions that deduplication misses. By catching contradictions that zero-shot likelihood and margin filters miss, it can help practitioners improve reward accuracy and clean alignment datasets at a fraction of the cost of full-corpus LLM judging. During DPO training, confirmed contradictions oppose the update far more often than clean records (56.7% vs. 37.0% in LoRA gradients) and stay below chance in reward accuracy; auditing HH-RLHF exposes label errors where aligned models disagree with human labels 73%-83% of the time, and retraining DPO after repairing or filtering flagged records improves reward accuracy by +0.5%-0.6% overall and +3.1%-3.5% on repaired inversions even without LLM arbitration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Data Shapley applies the game-theoretic Shapley value to assign each training example a fair value based on its contribution, but exact computation requires intractable retraining and embedding surrogates like KNN-Shapley fail to separate high- from low-utility demonstrations. In-context learning lets an LLM condition on a prompt containing examples without parameter updates, and this paper builds a cooperative game over such in-context demonstrations to audit alignment data efficiently.

## [STAIR: Agentic Planning Framework for Multi-Stage Security Incident Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09524) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present STAIR, an end-to-end agentic incident response framework that maintains incident evidence, response actions, and execution feedback in a shared Graph-as-State (GAS) representation, using a Stage Router to dispatch planning to stage-specialized agents and retrieving effect-validated historical experience to guide action selection. They also build STAIR-Bench, an executable benchmark of 44 Docker-based cyber-range cases, on which STAIR achieves an overall defense score of 0.95 (about 10.47% above the strongest baseline) and cuts cumulative penalties by roughly 28.8% in the independent CAGE4 environment. Existing AIOps methods automate fault diagnosis and remediation but lack a unified decision basis across planning and execution, which limits their ability to track incident state and align actions with evolving recovery objectives during long-horizon, multi-stage security incidents. STAIR's shared-state design and effect-validated experience reuse point toward more reliable automated security operations, potentially reducing the manual burden on incident responders. STAIR's Execution Harness executes actions, feeds results back to update the GAS, and validates action effects so that successful experience can be reused later; the framework formulates incident response planning as a state-driven, stage-aware, and experience-supported workflow automation problem for live software systems. The evaluation is based on an arXiv preprint with a 44-case Docker cyber-range benchmark and the CAGE4 environment, and no community discussion or independent replication is yet available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Incident response is the process of detecting, containing, and recovering from attacks on software systems, and AIOps applies AI and automation to IT operations tasks such as fault diagnosis and remediation. Agentic planning refers to AI systems that decompose goals into multi-step plans and execute them with tools, while a Graph-as-State representation encodes the current incident—evidence, actions, and feedback—as a graph that serves as the single shared state for both planning and execution. Benchmarks like STAIR-Bench and CAGE4 provide controlled cyber-range environments where such automated responders can be tested against ongoing attacks and benign activity.

<a id="item-253"></a>
## [DASC Compresses Recurrent State Checkpoints in Hybrid Linear-Attention Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30386) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DASC (Decay-Aware State Compression) analyzes the decay structure of Gated DeltaNet (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA) and finds that different heads and channels retain prefix information over markedly different timescales, which the authors call retention horizons. It derives these horizons from model weights, selects long-horizon state units, packs them into a ragged checkpoint layout, and balances them across tensor-parallel ranks, compressing KDA recurrent state checkpoints by 2.63x while reducing mean TTFT by 42.6% and improving input throughput by 68.4% under fixed memory budgets. Hybrid linear-attention models such as Kimi Linear and Qwen with GDN are being scaled to large open-weight models to cut KV-cache growth, but their in-place recurrent-state updates force serving systems to store state checkpoints for prefix reuse, creating a new memory bottleneck. DASC directly attacks this bottleneck, showing that state checkpoints can be compressed substantially with little quality loss, which could make long-context prefix caching cheaper and more practical for production inference engines. On reuse, DASC either zero-fills omitted state units or refreshes them from a bounded suffix at extra compute cost; conservative configurations stay close to full caching on retrieval and end-to-end reasoning benchmarks on Kimi-Linear, while more aggressive compression loses accuracy that suffix refresh can partially recover. The approach generalizes from channel-wise KDA to head-wise GDN, as shown by a similar quality-efficiency trend on Qwen with GDN.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Hybrid linear-attention architectures combine linear-attention or state-space layers, which use a fixed-size recurrent state instead of a growing KV cache, with a smaller number of full softmax attention layers that still need a KV cache. Gated DeltaNet (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA) are two such linear-attention mechanisms that use gating and the delta rule to control how information is retained or forgotten over long contexts. Because their recurrent state is updated in place, serving systems that want to reuse a prefix must save state checkpoints alongside the full-attention KV cache, and storing every checkpoint in full increases memory pressure, causing more evictions and repeated prefill.

## [Defense-as-Skill: A Runtime Guard Skill for Skill-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01487) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Defense-as-Skill, a paradigm in which the runtime guard itself is implemented as an installable, inspectable, and editable skill called SkillSonar, which checks sensitive actions against the user's task boundary and routes each action to allow, replan, or confirmation without modifying the underlying agent runtime. The authors also introduce SCOPE-R, a task-conditioned dataset covering 6 risk families and 21 sub-categories with 206 attack-confirmed malicious instances and 43 benign tasks, and use a Monte-Carlo Tree Search procedure called runtime guard-skill evolution to improve SkillSonar, reporting that on repeated GLM-5 runs in-distribution ASR drops from 0.482 to 0.104 and out-of-distribution ASR from 0.606 to 0.115 across Claude Code and OpenClaw. Skill-augmented agents load reusable skills as persistent runtime context, which gives malicious skills a durable channel to steer future actions, so pre-install vetting alone is insufficient and runtime, task-conditioned protection becomes necessary. This work shows that runtime guards can be skill-native, transparent, and evolvable policy artifacts, offering a practical security layer for agent platforms such as Claude Code and OpenClaw while preserving a favorable safety-utility trade-off. SkillSonar runs alongside untrusted task skills and makes allow, replan, or confirmation decisions without modifying the underlying agent runtime, and its evolution uses Monte-Carlo Tree Search over rollouts to improve the on-disk guard skill. The reported gains transfer across victim models, held-out risk families, and external benchmarks, though the evaluation is limited to the SCOPE-R dataset and the tested harnesses.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Skill-augmented agents extend large language model agents by loading reusable skills, such as instructions, scripts, and resources, as persistent runtime context to improve task performance. Because these skills remain active across tasks, a malicious skill can leak secrets, corrupt code, bypass approvals, or stage data for exfiltration once a concrete user task and workspace state make the unsafe action appear useful. Runtime guards are security components that inspect agent actions, such as shell commands, file changes, and tool calls, and can allow, ask for approval, or block them before execution.
