## [Trust-Hub Trojan benchmarks overestimate detector performance due to host-circuit reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07199) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.07199v2) measures how the choice of test boundary affects feature-based hardware-Trojan detection on the Trust-Hub corpus of 49,124 gates from 16 netlists in five host families. Keeping the parser, 36 gate features, class weighting, model settings, threshold, and family-level aggregation fixed, random forest F1/AP drops from 0.914/0.978 with pooled gates to 0.636/0.851 with one netlist held out and 0.460/0.577 with a whole host family held out, while XGBoost falls from 0.946/0.976 to 0.464/0.544. The result shows that widely reported Trust-Hub detection scores are inflated by data leakage: sibling Trojan variants share host logic, so gates from the same host can appear in both training and test folds. This matters for hardware-security researchers and practitioners who compare detectors on Trust-Hub, because it suggests benchmark protocols should report family-aware holdouts and all five family results alongside pooled scores. The gap persists under feature removal, repeated model and simulator seeds, score normalization, parser-related exclusions, and a smaller sample, though its size changes; aggregation also matters because a gate-weighted average is dominated by the larger ISCAS files, so the headline values give each host family one vote. The authors caution that five host families are too few for a population claim and that the experiment says nothing about transfer to a new cell library or an industrial design, supporting only the narrower conclusion that sibling benchmark variants can inflate apparent transfer.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Trust-Hub is a widely used public benchmark suite that provides circuits with hardware Trojans inserted, letting researchers compare detection methods. Many recent detectors are feature-based machine-learning models that classify individual gates or nets as Trojan or benign, and they are typically trained and tested on gates pooled from the whole benchmark corpus. Data leakage occurs when information unavailable at prediction time, such as host logic seen during training, is used during evaluation, producing overly optimistic performance estimates.

## [TDDN Fuses DINOv3 and CleanDIFT for Fine-Grained Vision-Language Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07937) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce TDDN (Text-aligned Diffused DINO Network), which fuses DINOv3 and CleanDIFT representations into a perception encoder called DiffusedDINO and aligns it with RoBERTa-L using only about 590K alignment pairs with frozen backbones. TDDN surpasses CLIP ViT-L/14 on image-text retrieval (text-to-image on Flickr30K and both directions on MS-COCO-2014) and more than triples CLIP's dense-prediction accuracy (ADE20K 5.20 to 18.11 mIoU, COCO-Stuff 7.35 to 24.44), while also doubling CLIP's segmentation accuracy on the new PuzzlePerception benchmark (11.04 to 22.51 mIoU). This work shows that CLIP-style ViT backbones sacrifice fine-grained visual detail for high-level semantics, a deficit that propagates into downstream vision and language tasks. By demonstrating that a fused DINOv3-CleanDIFT encoder can beat CLIP and even SigLIP 2 on segmentation among general-purpose contrastive encoders, TDDN suggests a practical path toward stronger fine-grained perception in vision-language models without massive training corpora. The approach keeps backbones frozen and relies on only ~590K alignment pairs, far less than CLIP's massive training corpus, yet still leads on segmentation benchmarks among general-purpose contrastive encoders including SigLIP 2. The authors also release PuzzlePerception, a segmentation and visual question answering dataset probing fine-grained spatial understanding, with code and data available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models typically use CLIP-style ViT backbones, which are trained on huge image-text corpora but tend to lose fine-grained spatial detail. DINOv3 is Meta's self-supervised vision transformer known for strong dense visual features, while CleanDIFT extracts noise-free, timestep-independent features from diffusion models for tasks like segmentation and depth estimation. TDDN combines these two perception-oriented encoders and aligns the fused representation with a RoBERTa-L text encoder to create a text-aligned model that retains fine-grained visual ability.

## [SUN Framework Jointly Scores Novelty and Reachability in RL Goal Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08642) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces SUN (SUccessor-to-Novelty), a reachability-aware goal-selection framework for reinforcement learning that scores candidate goals jointly by novelty and reachability using successor value functions. The authors prove SUN equals the value of a count-scaled goal-reaching reward, bounds short-horizon hitting probabilities, and rejects unreachable goals, and they pair it with an adaptive goal-selection strategy and a lightweight pseudocount. Goal-conditioned RL exploration methods typically trade off novelty and reachability by hand, apply them in sequence, or ignore one signal entirely, which can waste samples on unreachable goals. By providing theoretical guarantees and a drop-in module for any off-policy RL algorithm, SUN could improve exploration efficiency in hard environments such as mazes, obstacles, and irreversible transitions. SUN is derived from successor value functions and is shown to bound short-horizon hitting probabilities while rejecting unreachable goals; an adaptive goal-selection strategy uses these properties with an accurate yet lightweight pseudocount to avoid the overhead of classic count-based methods. The authors report consistent outperformance over state-of-the-art methods in standard and novel environments with unreachable or hard-to-reach states, irreversible transitions, obstacles, mazes, and unbounded spaces, with code released at https://github.com/sparisi/rl-anvil.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning agents learn by trial and error, and exploration—deciding which states or goals to pursue—remains a fundamental challenge, especially with sparse rewards. Goal-conditioned RL addresses this by selecting intermediate goals to encourage broader state coverage, while successor features/value functions are a value-function representation that decouples environment dynamics from reward structure, enabling efficient generalization. SUN combines these ideas by using successor value functions to identify goals that are both novel and reachable.

## [Study maps how source-target compatibility drives adversarial transfer in deepfake detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10002) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled arXiv study evaluates adversarial transferability across 60 deepfake detectors spanning six backbones, two pretraining regimes, and five training-data configurations, using AutoAttack (AA) and Carlini-Wagner with Expectation over Transformation (CW-EOT). It finds significantly higher transfer when source and target share an exact backbone, architecture family, pretraining regime, or training data, and that this compatibility structure is attack-dependent. The results show that source-model selection and source-target compatibility are central to credible black-box robustness evaluation, meaning prior deepfake-detector robustness claims based on limited detector pools may be misleading. This affects security researchers, ML robustness practitioners, and anyone deploying deepfake detection in adversarial settings. Averaged across non-target sources, mean attack success rate (ASR) is 7.21% under AA and 19.52% under CW-EOT, but a multi-source oracle combining both attacks reaches 64.48% mean ASR after excluding exact backbone and training-data matches. Exact backbone compatibility has the largest effect under AA, whereas shared pretraining and training data dominate under CW-EOT, and the authors release 240,000 adversarially perturbed images, pairwise transfer results, detector configurations, and evaluation code.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Transfer-based black-box attacks generate adversarial examples on a source surrogate model and apply them to a target model the attacker cannot query, exploiting the phenomenon that adversarial perturbations often transfer between models. Deepfake detectors are neural networks trained to distinguish real from manipulated media, and prior transferability studies typically used small detector pools without separating architectural factors from training factors. AutoAttack is a parameter-free ensemble of attacks used as a reliable robustness benchmark, while the Carlini-Wagner attack is an optimization-based method that finds minimal perturbations, with Expectation over Transformation making it robust to input transformations.

## [AI-Generated RTL Verification Plans Fail Execution: Only 9 of 1,857 Passed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19844) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SecTB-RTL, an auditable framework covering 31 tasks and 124 authored hardware-security regressions, and reports a critical instrument-validation incident: after a schema-only repair, a frozen follow-up run (C1-R3) completed 1,860 calls, with the provider accepting 1,857 responses, yet only nine passed the production semantic validator. Because generation and execution rules did not match, the authors preserve the run as an instrument-validation incident and report no prompt-effect estimate. This negative result shows that provider or schema acceptance does not establish execution validity, a caution that matters for anyone using LLMs to generate hardware verification artifacts and for AI reliability research more broadly. It also provides a reusable benchmark, failure-preserving contract, and governance controls to prevent infrastructure behavior from being misreported as model behavior. A deterministic non-AI baseline killed 36, 75, and 78 mutants at increasing resource limits, while the first confirmatory run (C1-R2) failed before model execution because the provider rejected its response schema. A subsequent follow-up is excluded because it did not satisfy the preregistered evidence-completeness gate and is treated only as future work.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: RTL (register-transfer level) verification plans define how hardware designs are checked for correctness and security, and LLMs are increasingly used to draft these plans and related assertions. Mutation testing injects faults into RTL to measure whether a verification flow actually detects bugs, since high code coverage alone does not prove test strength. This paper audits whether AI-generated verification plans that pass a provider's schema can actually be executed and validated in a production pipeline.

## [RLVR²: Ranking-Based Rewards for Rubric RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23457) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes RLVR², a verifiable ranking paradigm for rubric-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) that replaces the standard linear combination of normalized criterion scores with criterion-specific within-group ordinal outcomes. It recovers a latent utility from the resulting comparison matrix and merges these utilities into a single training signal, and reports consistent gains over representative rubric-based baselines across three model scales and 16 benchmarks. As RLVR moves beyond math and code into multi-dimensional quality requirements, how rubric scores are aggregated into a scalar reward silently determines which quality dimensions the model trades off during training. RLVR² shows that discarding raw score magnitudes in favor of within-group ordering can improve overall performance, which matters for anyone building rubric-based post-training pipelines for LLMs. RLVR² avoids calibrating heterogeneous rubric scales by retaining only within-group ordering and discarding raw score magnitudes, and it supports objective-preserving attribute adjustment, allowing auxiliary attributes correlated with observed rankings but not themselves training objectives to enter the estimation without expanding the rubric or rewarding them directly. Analysis indicates it controls systematic effects tied to reasoning efficiency and response formatting while preserving the quality objective.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) is a post-training method that fine-tunes language models using rewards from automatic, rule-based checkers rather than learned reward models or human raters, and it has proven effective on tasks with well-defined correctness signals such as mathematics and code. Because policy optimization consumes one scalar per rollout, rubric-based pipelines that score outputs on multiple criteria must aggregate those criterion scores into a single reward. The prevailing practice normalizes each criterion and takes a linear combination, which implicitly assumes that cardinal score differences are comparable across criteria and that gains on one criterion compensate for failures on another.

## [EpiMind: Graph World Models for Constrained Epidemic Policy Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35545) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present EpiMind, a graph world model framework that combines a graph-factored recurrent state-space model (GF-RSSM) with graph-temporal ADMM for constrained epidemic policy planning across regions. EpiMind reduces admission RMSE by 29% relative to graph-free dynamics modeling and plans within 1-5% of the best feasible constant policy while guaranteeing shared-budget feasibility. Epidemic policy planning requires coordinating regions that are coupled through mobility-driven spillovers under limited shared resources, and existing methods either lack action-conditioned models of coupled dynamics or cannot guarantee per-period feasibility. EpiMind's combination of learned graph-structured dynamics with explicit constrained coordination could make AI-driven public health interventions more accurate and reliably deployable. The GF-RSSM shares neural parameters across regions while each region maintains its own recurrent belief, generating joint policy-conditioned rollouts from regional latent beliefs. Graph-temporal ADMM optimizes regional interventions, enforces shared-resource feasibility through projection, and evaluates temporal specifications under the learned model; EpiMind outperforms all deployable baselines across three resource budgets in real-context evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that let an agent imagine the consequences of actions before taking them, and recurrent state-space models are a common architecture for such learned dynamics. Graph neural networks capture relationships between entities, here geographic regions linked by mobility, while ADMM (Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers) is a classical optimization technique for decomposing constrained problems into coordinated subproblems. EpiMind combines these ideas so that policy planning can respect shared resource budgets while accounting for cross-region spillovers.

## [FACT builds fidelity-aware articulated digital twins via agentic loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37067) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released FACT (Fidelity-Aware Construction of Articulated Twins), an agentic framework that progressively builds articulated digital twins by running an evidence–diagnosis–revision loop on a shared editable representation. It reconstructs editable articulated geometry from images, repairs collision proxies through task-aware local repartitioning before fidelity-constrained compression, and builds response models from passive-response videos using simulation residuals to guide model revision and constrained physical parameter fitting. Visually plausible articulated assets often fail during contact interactions or produce inaccurate motion, so FACT's focus on geometric, contact, and dynamic fidelity could improve simulation reliability for robotics, embodied AI, and graphics pipelines. Experiments show it improves geometric reconstruction over baselines, enables more reliable interaction with simpler collision proxies, and better reproduces held-out physical responses than direct parameter inference. The agent selects measurements and model edits using quantitative feedback while numerical tools execute and validate updates, and the pipeline includes feature planning, targeted measurements, and diagnostic refinement for geometry. Collision proxies are handled by task-aware local repartitioning followed by fidelity-constrained compression, and response models rely on simulation residuals plus constrained physical parameter fitting.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Articulated digital twins are editable virtual replicas of objects with movable joints, such as drawers, doors, or robot links, and they are used in robotics, embodied AI, and simulation. Building them typically requires carefully captured data and separate steps for geometry, collision handling, and dynamics, and visually plausible assets can still behave incorrectly in contact-rich tasks. FACT addresses this by using an agentic loop that treats construction as an iterative diagnosis-and-repair process rather than a one-shot reconstruction.

## [OpenCollab: Multi-Agent Coding Framework with Adherence Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38345) ⭐️ 7.0/10

OpenCollab is a new multi-agent coding framework that unifies programmable collaboration design with a controllable shared runtime and fine-grained event tracking. It introduces a metric called Adherence to measure whether a declared agent organization is actually realized, with experiments showing Adherence shifts from 47.2% to 97.2% when any single configuration dimension changes. The work exposes a behavioral gap between how multi-agent systems are configured and how they actually operate, which undermines the attribution of performance gains in existing evaluations. Its benchmark results, where the OpenCollab (Duo) configuration reaches new SOTA against harnesses like Mini-SWE-agent, OpenHands, Codex CLI, and Claude Code, suggest that organization design itself can be a decisive factor in agentic coding performance. OpenCollab enforces experimental control on a shared runtime and tracks execution through fine-grained event streams, enabling controlled causal evaluation of collaboration configurations. Its single-agent configuration uses the fewest tokens across all evaluated suites, though the paper's impact is currently limited to academic research rather than immediate practical adoption.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent coding systems split software engineering tasks among multiple LLM-based agents that communicate and coordinate to complete complex work. Existing evaluations typically assume the configured organization is followed faithfully, but in practice agents may deviate from their assigned roles, making it hard to tell whether gains come from the organization design or from other system components. OpenCollab addresses this by providing a unified infrastructure where collaboration is programmable and the runtime is controllable, so that differences in behavior can be measured rather than assumed.

## [Automatic Harness Evolution Works for Visual-Interface Robot Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39304) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.39304v2) shows that an optimizer coding agent can automatically improve the harness — prompts, tools, and control rules — of a visual-interface robot agent that observes a browser-based 3D interface via screenshots and acts by posing a virtual target gripper. The study reports that held-out success rises as the optimizer's training batch grows from 5 to 100 rollouts, and that a Champion-Challenger selection rule lifts LIBERO success from 51% to 67% over 30 rounds, reaching 71.2% on the RoboCasa365 leaderboard when combined with a pretrained policy. This work suggests that the hand-crafted harness around a robot agent can be automatically evolved rather than manually tuned, which could lower engineering costs and improve generalization for embodied agents. The findings on rollout scale and selection rules offer concrete guidance for researchers building self-improving agent pipelines, and the 13.1-point lead over the best published RoboCasa365 entry signals real benchmark impact. The paper identifies two critical factors: the number of rollouts the optimizer sees per round governs generalization, with small batches overfitting training tasks, and the optimizer must not be given free rein, since accepting every revision degrades the harness within a few rounds. The Champion-Challenger rule promotes a revision only if it beats the incumbent on the same fixed cases, and the evolved harness is combined with a pretrained policy through a single tool that hands over control.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: A robot agent harness refers to the prompts, tools, and control rules that shape how a language-model-based agent perceives an environment and acts within it; for visual-interface agents, the agent sees screenshots of a browser-based 3D interface and acts by posing a virtual gripper. Automatic harness evolution uses an optimizer coding agent to iteratively revise that harness based on agent-environment interactions, a technique related to recent work such as HarnessCompass and Harness Evolver. Champion-Challenger is a standard model-comparison approach in which a current production model (champion) is compared against a candidate (challenger) before replacement, here adapted as a revision-acceptance rule.

## [Sample Value Depends on the Learner, Not Just the Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00221) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.00221v2) shows through controlled selection experiments on ImageNet-100 that the crossover point between easy and coverage samples is set by the learner, not the data. Doubling ResNet-18 width moved the crossover from 57 to 85 samples per class, while increasing input resolution from 32 to 64 pixels moved it back from 85 to 57. This finding challenges the common assumption in data selection and curriculum learning that sample difficulty is an intrinsic property of the data, suggesting that selection strategies must be tuned to the specific model and input configuration that will consume the data. It could influence how practitioners design training subsets and how future research frames sample utility. The crossover shift shrinks as model width grows, and under the native ImageNet-1k protocol a 4x range of width moves the boundary by at most a few samples per class. On a Vision Transformer, coverage samples win at every measured budget, even with easy subsets that work well for the convolutional learner.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Data selection methods often rank training samples by difficulty, assuming easy samples are universally more useful early in training. Curriculum learning similarly assumes a fixed ordering of samples from easy to hard benefits all learners. This paper tests that assumption by freezing selected subsets and varying only the learner, using ResNet-18 width and input resolution as controlled variables on ImageNet-100, a 100-class subset of ImageNet-1k.

## [StepRS-GRPO: Stepwise Risk-Sensitive RL for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00661) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.00661v2) proposes StepRS-GRPO, a reinforcement learning method that varies the risk coefficient of the group-advantage transformation across denoising states in diffusion large language models (dLLMs). Across multiple dLLM backbones and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, it improves both pass@1 accuracy and pass@k coverage over centered GRPO while increasing answer diversity. Diffusion language models generate text by denoising blocks of tokens in parallel, so a single terminal reward is reused across many changing intermediate decisions — a mismatch that standard RLVR methods like GRPO do not address. StepRS-GRPO shows that a lightweight, trainer-compatible modification can yield consistent reasoning gains, suggesting a practical path for post-training the growing family of diffusion LLMs. For binary rewards, the authors prove the transformation is exactly a prompt- and state-dependent rescaling of centered outcome advantages, and a capability-based calibration suggests a coefficient scale while endpoint and interpolation ablations guide schedule selection. Mass-matched controls support the contributions of state allocation and schedule direction, and the gains persist even after matching the RMS of the advantages to that of centered GRPO; reasoning-trace diagnostics show diversity gains extend beyond final-answer strings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate text by iteratively denoising a sequence or successive blocks, allowing several tokens to be revealed in parallel rather than strictly left-to-right like autoregressive models. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains reasoning models using automatically checkable reward signals, and GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a popular RLVR algorithm that normalizes rewards within a group of sampled responses to cut compute costs. StepRS-GRPO adapts this group-advantage idea to the multi-step denoising process of dLLMs.

## [SSR Reformulates Multimodal Agent Reasoning as Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01892) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Selection-based Structured Reasoning (SSR), a framework that replaces free-form reasoning generation in multimodal agents with selection from pre-specified, reusable natural-language candidates. Evaluated on seven multimodal search benchmarks with 2B and 4B models, SSR reduces per-turn reasoning latency by over 90% and total per-question inference latency by 28-54% while matching the success rate of leading same-scale search agents. This work addresses a key efficiency bottleneck for small multimodal models, where lengthy free-form reasoning yields little actionable guidance while incurring substantial inference cost. By enabling parallel scoring of reasoning candidates, SSR could make capable multimodal search agents practical on resource-constrained hardware and in latency-sensitive deployments. SSR requires no auxiliary task head; instead, teacher-forced prefilling computes token likelihoods concurrently within and across candidates using a shared context KV cache. The method was tested across multiple reinforcement learning objectives and supervised fine-tuning, showing consistent efficiency gains without sacrificing task performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal agents typically generate free-form reasoning before each action, but small models with limited capacity often produce verbose reasoning that adds inference cost without improving decisions. Multimodal search benchmarks evaluate an agent's ability to find and reason over visual and textual information across tasks. KV caching and teacher-forced prefilling are standard inference techniques that reuse computation across tokens, which SSR leverages to score many candidate reasoning traces in parallel.

## [EXAQC Evolution Automates Quantum Circuits for Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03220) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper extends EXAQC, an evolutionary framework for automated quantum circuit discovery, to hybrid quantum-classical image classification, eliminating the need for manually designed parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs). The evolved models achieve 98.42% accuracy on MNIST, 90.62% on Fashion-MNIST, and 85.47% on CIFAR-10, while using comparable gate counts to other quantum architecture-search methods. This work addresses a key bottleneck in quantum machine learning: the reliance on hand-designed or fixed circuit ansätze that may not suit specific tasks. By automatically discovering compact quantum modules that can replace larger classical components, it points toward more practical and parameter-efficient hybrid vision architectures. Against classical networks, the evolved hybrid models maintain comparable accuracy with substantially fewer trainable parameters, reaching 85.68% on CIFAR-10 with over 25× fewer parameters than a 10-layer CNN. Encoding choice also matters: rotation-based encodings (RX, RY, U3) outperform amplitude encoding by 22-25 points on CIFAR-10.

**Background**: Hybrid quantum-classical neural networks combine parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) with classical deep learning layers. PQCs, also called ansätze, are quantum circuits with tunable parameters that can be optimized, and their architecture—circuit structure, gate composition, and qubit connectivity—strongly affects performance. EXAQC (Evolutionary eXploration of Augmenting Quantum Circuits) is an evolutionary algorithm that jointly optimizes circuit structure and parameters, previously applied to supervised learning tasks.

## [GOTT: Reusable Cross-Embodiment Primitive for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03861) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose GOTT, a reach-acquire-move framework built around a single reusable cross-embodiment contact-acquisition primitive that establishes stable contact from an approximate initialization given a robot-agnostic object trajectory. Simulation and real-world experiments show it works across diverse objects, arm-hand platforms, and seen and unseen hand morphologies, consistently improving end-to-end task success over open-loop grasp execution. Dexterous hands still lack a reusable low-level primitive that reliably establishes contact across tasks and embodiments, which is a key bottleneck for turning manipulation intent from foundation models and human data into real multi-fingered robot behavior. A cross-embodiment contact primitive could let the same low-level skill transfer across different robot hands, reducing the need to retrain or re-engineer grasping for each new platform. The framework separates concerns: reach specifications can come from future-aware planning, external models, or human demonstrations, while the contact-acquisition primitive and pose-conditioned tracking backend remain unchanged. The primitive is closed-loop and shared across embodiments, and the system is evaluated on both seen and unseen hand morphologies, though the abstract does not quantify success rates or specify the exact hardware used.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Cross-embodiment learning in robotics aims to design policies, representations, or skills that transfer across heterogeneous robot morphologies, sensor configurations, and action spaces, so that data and skills collected on one platform can benefit others. Dexterous manipulation with multi-fingered hands is a long-standing challenge because high-dimensional control and contact-rich dynamics make reinforcement learning sample-inefficient and hard to scale to real-world tasks. GOTT addresses this by introducing a reusable contact-acquisition primitive that sits between high-level manipulation intent and low-level finger control.

Researchers introduced Video2World, a benchmark of 222 reconstruction instances derived from 189 robot and human demonstration videos, which evaluates coding agents on autonomously building interactive simulated environments from embodied videos. Evaluating 9 frontier coding-agent systems showed task success rising from below 5% to over 15% beginning with Claude Opus 5, though substantial gaps to human-assisted reconstruction remain. It matters for embodied AI, robotics, and agent evaluation, since it connects foundation models, coding agents, and simulation pipelines in a single measurable benchmark. Video2World measures reconstructed worlds along three axes: geometric fidelity, dynamic fidelity, and functional correctness, capturing spatial perception, physical reasoning, and executable interaction. A notable finding is that worlds that look better can work worse, meaning higher visual fidelity does not always translate into higher task success.

## [Green-Routed Neural Operator Uses PDE Physics to Guide Feature Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05337) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced the Green-Routed Neural Operator (GRNO), which uses the governing PDE to determine where latent features are sampled rather than treating transport fields merely as input values. Across five two- and three-dimensional PDE systems, GRNO achieved the lowest mean final relative L2 error on four under 40-step autoregressive evaluation and remained competitive on Keller-Segel. This work addresses a fundamental mismatch between how PDEs use transport fields to select read coordinates and how neural operators typically treat them, potentially improving long-horizon forecasting accuracy for scientific machine learning surrogates. Better PDE surrogates could accelerate simulations in fluid dynamics, climate modeling, and other fields where repeated solves are computationally expensive. A parameter-free equation adapter evaluates the diagnostic relation and constructs a departure map whose values are the read coordinates, while a multiscale encoder-decoder combines centered and routed reads. Ablations show GRNO outperforms variants that supply the transport field only as an input feature, substitute a learned displacement, or apply the route with spatial misalignment, and it substantially beats directly advecting the physical state.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Neural operators are a class of deep learning models that learn mappings between function spaces, often used as fast surrogate solvers for partial differential equations (PDEs) that would otherwise require expensive numerical simulation. In many PDEs, transport fields (such as velocity in advection) determine which spatial locations influence a given point, a role analogous to Green's functions that relate sources to solutions. Autoregressive evaluation means the model predicts one step at a time and feeds its own predictions back as input, which is challenging because errors can accumulate over long horizons.

## [TRIAGE stabilizes native NVFP4 reinforcement learning for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07043) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.07043v2) introduces TRIAGE, a direction-aware stabilization method that mitigates policy optimization instability caused by learner-sampler mismatch in native NVFP4 reinforcement learning. Experiments on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-30B-A3B show stable optimization and full-precision-level performance across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, with up to 2.3x higher rollout throughput than BF16. Low-precision execution is a key lever for accelerating and reducing the cost of RL post-training of large language models, but numerical mismatch between the sampler and learner has been a major obstacle to stability. TRIAGE shows that native 4-bit (W4A4) execution can be made stable without sacrificing accuracy, which could make large-scale RL training substantially more efficient. TRIAGE distinguishes locally amplifying from contracting policy-gradient update contributions that mismatch magnitude alone cannot identify, and it uses segment-level diagnosis to selectively rebalance updates plus bounded repair for residual severe mismatch. It modifies the optimization objective while retaining native NVFP4 W4A4 forward execution on both the sampler and the learner.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: NVFP4 is a 4-bit floating-point format promoted by NVIDIA for ultra-low-precision training of large language models, using techniques such as 2D block scaling and Random Hadamard Transforms to cope with FP4's limited dynamic range. In reinforcement learning for LLMs, the sampler generates rollouts while the learner updates the policy; when these two run in different precisions, the resulting learner-sampler mismatch is a system-level discrepancy distinct from classical off-policy RL and can destabilize policy optimization.

## [ELF-BO exploits evaluation latency for practical fully Bayesian optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08969) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose ELF-BO, a Bayesian optimization algorithm that samples from the hyperparameter posterior while the objective function is being evaluated, then reweights those samples once the objective value is observed. Across synthetic functions and real-world applications, ELF-BO matches fully Bayesian methods while keeping decision latency on par with or better than standard BO. Fully Bayesian model averaging gives better uncertainty estimates in the low-data regime typical of Bayesian optimization, but it has been considered prohibitively expensive and thus rarely used. By hiding that computation inside the unavoidable objective evaluation latency, ELF-BO makes fully Bayesian optimization practical for expensive black-box problems such as hyperparameter tuning and engineering design. The key trick is that hyperparameter posterior samples are drawn during the objective evaluation and only need reweighting once the objective value arrives, so no substantial decision-time cost is added. The abstract reports that ELF-BO matches fully Bayesian performance while incurring decision latency on par with or better than standard BO, though it assumes the objective evaluation takes non-trivial time.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Bayesian optimization is a sequential strategy for globally optimizing black-box objective functions that are expensive to evaluate, such as training a machine-learning model or running a numerical simulation; it builds a probabilistic surrogate model, often a Gaussian process, and uses an acquisition function to pick the next point. Standard BO fits the surrogate's hyperparameters with a point estimate, whereas a fully Bayesian approach averages over the hyperparameter posterior, giving better-calibrated uncertainty but at much higher computational cost. Bayesian model averaging is the general statistical framework of weighting predictions by posterior model probability to account for model uncertainty.

## [Decoupling Logic from Persona Gives Edge LLM Agents Immunity to Context Pollution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09772) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.09772v2) introduces the Decoupling Architecture (AO-DA), which splits logical inference ('What') from persona expression ('How') into two inference paths running on a single INT4 base model with hot-swappable LoRA adapters. In 480 ablation runs on an Apple M2 laptop using Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Gemma-3-4B-it, the decoupled logic path stayed structurally invariant to pollution, with byte-identical outputs across all four pollution levels (40/40), while the mixed single-pass prompt grew from 242 to 1,203 tokens and its composite logic score collapsed from 0.669 to 0.150 on Llama. This work offers a practical architectural fix for a core failure mode of LLM agents — persona-logic interference, where long, misleading, persona-heavy history degrades reasoning — and shows it can be deployed on resource-constrained edge hardware. If the approach generalizes, it could make always-on personal agents on laptops and phones more reliable and enable instant persona switching without re-running expensive logic. The logic path receives only the core turn and emits a verifiable structured state (Micro-State), keeping its prompt fixed at 180 tokens (Llama) or 167 tokens (Gemma) regardless of pollution level, while the persona path renders that state in character using the full history. Separation costs one extra decode on a topic's first turn (28.2 s vs 18.2 s on Llama) but enables persona hot-swapping in 1.7 ms (warm median); notably, the robustness advantage held on the 8B model but not the 4B model, where both approaches collapsed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Edge LLM agents run on local devices like laptops and must simultaneously maintain a consistent persona and reason correctly, all within a single context window that accumulates conversation history and persona instructions. Context pollution occurs when this window fills with long, misleading, or persona-heavy content that degrades the agent's logical output, a problem related to prompt injection and memory contamination in agent frameworks. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that adds small trainable matrices to a frozen base model, allowing lightweight, swappable task-specific adapters instead of retraining billions of parameters.

## [PHRBench Evaluates How LLMs Handle Hallucinated Premises](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10455) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PHRBench, a controlled benchmark that behaviorally evaluates post-hallucination reasoning across four domains and 18 large language models using 4,820 controlled instances. Each reasoning trajectory is labeled as Hallucination Compliance, Hallucination Avoidance, or Heuristic Correction, and the study finds that successful correction remains relatively rare and correlates with more frequent belief updates. Hallucinated content can propagate through multi-stage LLM pipelines and contaminate downstream reasoning, so understanding whether models accept, avoid, or correct false premises is critical for AI safety and reliability. PHRBench shifts evaluation from final-answer accuracy to response-level behavioral trajectories, offering a more diagnostic view of model trustworthiness. The benchmark defines an insightful trajectory as successful correction that ultimately reaches the correct answer, and it finds that properties of the hallucinated prompt carry substantial predictive signal for recovery, with a lightweight predictor achieving an AUROC of 0.847. The behavioral labels are assigned independently of final-answer correctness, allowing analysis of reasoning quality even when the outcome is wrong.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models sometimes generate hallucinated information—plausible but false content—that can enter the context of later reasoning steps in multi-stage systems. Post-hallucination reasoning (PHR) studies what happens after such erroneous content is already present, but prior work mainly looked at final outcomes and aggregate dynamics rather than response-level behavior. PHRBench addresses this gap by systematically labeling how each model response treats a hallucinated premise.

## [Higher-Order Action Supervision Boosts Policy Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11175) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.11175v2) introduces a loss scheme that supervises both zeroth-order action labels and first-order action dynamics, providing formal theoretical guarantees and acting as a plug-and-play module for any off-the-shelf policy model. Evaluations on OGBench and D4RL show substantial improvements in performance, control robustness, and out-of-distribution generalization, especially in low-data regimes. This work addresses a critical real-world deployment issue—control instability in imitation learning and reinforcement learning—by enforcing temporal consistency through higher-order action supervision. It could significantly benefit robotics and autonomous driving, where robust and stable control is essential, and integrates seamlessly with existing offline RL frameworks. The method requires no structural modifications to policy models (deterministic, stochastic, or flow-based) and can be added as a lightweight module to a broad spectrum of offline RL frameworks. It notably improves out-of-distribution generalization in challenging low-data regimes, though the paper does not specify computational overhead or hyperparameter sensitivity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Imitation learning (IL) and reinforcement learning (RL) train policies by supervising or optimizing action labels, known as zeroth-order actions. However, these methods often ignore higher-order action dynamics—such as velocity or temporal derivatives—leading to unstable control in real-world applications. This paper proposes supervising both zeroth- and first-order actions to enforce temporal consistency and improve robustness.

## [ComCLIP: Rethinking Contrastive Loss in CLIP Post-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11374) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.11374v2) revisits the claim that contrastive loss is unsuitable for CLIP post-training, showing that catastrophic forgetting under small batches is driven mainly by an inappropriate contrastive temperature τ rather than insufficient negatives. The authors propose ComCLIP, a single-epoch recipe that freezes the text encoder and trains the vision encoder with a properly-tempered InfoNCE loss, an MSE anchoring loss, and a relational distillation loss from DINOv2. This challenges a recent assumption that motivated abandoning contrastive objectives in favor of distillation for CLIP refinement, potentially reopening a simpler and more lightweight path for improving vision-language models. Since CLIP is the de facto vision encoder for downstream VLMs like LLaVA, a drop-in refined encoder with unchanged architecture and inference cost could benefit a wide range of multimodal applications. ComCLIP matches the self-distillation baseline CLIP-Refine on zero-shot classification while improving linear probing transferability (48.99 vs. 42.28 on ViT-B/16) and MMVP on ViT-L/14 (24.20 vs. 19.01), though CLIP-Refine remains stronger on image-text retrieval. Used as a drop-in vision encoder for LLaVA-1.5-7B without re-aligning the projector or LLM, ComCLIP yields no net change across 8 VLM benchmarks, and code/models are released on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: CLIP is a foundational vision-language model trained with a contrastive objective (InfoNCE) that aligns images and text in a shared embedding space, and it serves as the standard vision encoder for downstream VLMs such as LLaVA. Post-training is a lightweight way to further refine CLIP, but recent work argued that the standard contrastive loss causes catastrophic forgetting under small batches, leading to distillation-based alternatives. The temperature τ in InfoNCE controls the sharpness of the similarity distribution and thus the gradient behavior, which this paper identifies as the key overlooked factor.

## [Onboard YOLOX-S Vessel Detection on Versal Hardware Cuts Satellite Downlink Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12182) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12182v2) develops an onboard vessel detection system that trains a YOLOX-S detector on a controlled dataset of 68 annotated Maxar scenes covering 43 vessel classes, then quantizes and deploys it on the DPU of a Versal VC1902 adaptive SoC. The authors propose several content-aware downlink modes and report that on dense harbor and coastal scenes, tiles retain 98% of vessels using 29% of scene volume, while crops retain 83% using just 3%. Very-high-resolution Earth-observation satellites routinely acquire more data than they can store or downlink, and vessels occupy only a tiny fraction of each maritime scene, so content-aware downlink could substantially relieve bandwidth bottlenecks. This work shows a practical path to deploying such detection on radiation-tolerant embedded hardware, which matters for satellite operators, maritime surveillance agencies, and the broader trend toward edge AI in space. The quantized detector runs on the Versal VC1902 DPU with only a limited loss of detection quality and a processing time of a few seconds per scene, and the downlink modes range from metadata only (box, class, and score) to image crops, detection tiles, or whole scenes with degraded backgrounds. The reported trade-offs are estimated from measured detection errors, so the vessel-retention figures depend on the specific dense harbor and coastal scenes evaluated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Earth-observation satellites capture imagery far faster than they can transmit it to ground stations, so onboard compression is standard, but it treats every pixel equally regardless of content. YOLOX-S is an anchor-free, lightweight variant of the YOLO object-detection family designed to balance accuracy and efficiency, making it suitable for embedded inference. AMD's Versal VC1902 is an adaptive SoC that combines programmable logic with AI engines, and its Deep Learning Processor Unit (DPU) is a programmable IP core optimized for convolutional neural network inference, allowing quantized models to run efficiently at the edge.

## [Who Is Vfmaddrnd231pd? Decoding Cryptic x86 Instructions](https://purplesyringa.moe/blog/who-is-vfmaddrnd231pd/) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A blog post titled "Who Is Vfmaddrnd231pd?" examines the obscure x86 FMA instruction vfmadd231pd and uses it as a case study for the broader problem of machine-generated code that is technically correct but nearly incomprehensible to humans. The piece argues that as compilers and AI code generators produce increasingly cryptic low-level output, developers lose direct, intuitive understanding of the code they ship. This matters because AI-assisted and compiler-optimized code is becoming the norm, and studies show readability issues in AI-generated code have increased more than threefold. If developers cannot easily parse the instructions their tools emit, debugging, auditing, and maintaining software becomes harder, with potential consequences for security and reliability. vfmadd231pd is a Fused Multiply-Add (FMA) instruction operating on packed double-precision floating-point values, where the "231" encodes the operand ordering (dest = src2 * src3 + dest). Such instructions are emitted by compilers like GCC and Clang when FMA support is enabled (e.g., -mfma), and the cryptic mnemonic requires a mental translation step even for experienced assembly programmers.

hackernews · pngmangi · Oct 12, 21:12 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50063138)

**Background**: FMA (Fused Multiply-Add) instructions compute a*b+c in a single operation with only one rounding step, improving both speed and numerical accuracy compared to separate multiply and add instructions. On x86, compilers only emit FMA instructions when explicitly told it is safe to do so, typically via the -mfma flag, because FMA changes floating-point results and may break code that assumes strict IEEE 754 semantics. The naming scheme of these instructions (e.g., vfmadd231pd) encodes operand order and data type, making them notoriously hard to read at a glance.

## [EliteTable: LLM-Guided Program Search for Control Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12536) ⭐️ 6.0/10

EliteTable is a new LLM-guided program-search algorithm that maintains a fixed-capacity table of high-scoring solutions to propose new programs, edit existing ones, and remix multiple solutions. Evaluated on six Gymnasium control tasks with ten generations and fifty candidate slots per generation, candidate-score medians improved in all six tasks, but held-out performance declined in Acrobot-v1 and fell short of reporting targets in BipedalWalker-v3 and CarRacing-v3. This work explores combining LLM-guided program search with evolutionary elitism for reinforcement learning control tasks, a direction that could reduce reliance on gradient-based policy optimization. However, the mixed transfer results and lack of baselines or ablations mean its practical advantage over existing methods remains unproven. Each task was run with ten independently initialized searches, and selected-winner search-score medians rose in five of six tasks; CartPole-v1 reached the search-score ceiling in the first generation of all ten searches. Six of ten Acrobot-v1 searches declined on held-out episodes, and the paper explicitly notes that comparisons and ablations are needed to establish relative performance and component contributions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Program search is a family of algorithms that explore a space of candidate programs to find one that solves a given problem, and here large language models are used to propose and edit those programs. Gymnasium is the maintained fork of OpenAI Gym, providing standardized reinforcement learning environments such as CartPole-v1, Acrobot-v1, BipedalWalker-v3, and CarRacing-v3, where agents learn control policies by interacting with simulated tasks. An elite table is a fixed-capacity archive that keeps only the highest-scoring solutions found so far, which then guide future proposals.

## [DeFiRank Uses Federated Learning for DeFi Governance Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12916) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.12916v1) proposes DeFiRank, a federated learning-based pairwise ranking framework that lets multiple institutions collaboratively train a model to rank DeFi providers by the authenticity of their on-chain behavior without sharing private transaction data. Evaluated on a real-world blockchain dataset, it achieves NDCG@15 of about 0.87 versus about 0.92 for a centralized baseline, while matching the baseline's ranking stability (Kendall τ ≈ 0.70) and producing 648 provider promotions and 39 demotions. Current DeFi governance often relies on manual review by human committees, which is subjective, hard to scale under high transaction volume, and unable to capture complex behavioral patterns. A privacy-preserving, automated ranking approach could make governance decisions more consistent and data-driven, which matters for DeFi protocols, DAOs, and the researchers studying decentralized governance and federated learning. The federated model performs modestly below the centralized baseline (NDCG@15 ≈ 0.87 vs. ≈ 0.92) but preserves data privacy and matches its ranking stability, suggesting a small accuracy trade-off for decentralized training. The framework ranks providers using signals such as counterparty diversity, use of multiple applications, and transaction frequency, and the paper is still a preprint with no peer review or community discussion yet.

**Background**: Federated learning is a machine learning technique in which multiple entities collaboratively train a model while keeping their data decentralized, motivated by privacy, data minimization, and data access concerns. Pairwise ranking is a method that compares entities in pairs to determine which is preferred, and it is widely used to produce ordered lists or scores. DeFi governance refers to the decision-making processes in decentralized finance protocols and DAOs, where token holders or committees decide on protocol changes, incentives, and participant eligibility.

## [SWEAR: Difficulty-Aware Adaptive Reasoning for Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13081) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers propose SWEAR, a difficulty-aware reasoning framework for large audio-language models (LALMs) that derives five-dimensional difficulty scores and compressed reasoning traces from offline assessments, then uses supervised fine-tuning plus reinforcement learning to adaptively allocate reasoning effort across stages. Experiments on the MMAU-test-mini and MMAR benchmarks show improved accuracy alongside substantially fewer generated tokens, shortening reasoning on easy instances without hurting performance on hard ones. Adaptive reasoning is a key lever for making multimodal models both accurate and cheap to run, and SWEAR's finer-grained difficulty modeling could make audio-language assistants more responsive and less compute-hungry in real deployments. It also signals a broader trend of replacing coarse, one-size-fits-all reasoning budgets with learned, per-instance control policies across multimodal AI. The framework relies on five-dimensional difficulty scores and compressed reasoning traces obtained from complementary offline assessments, and it learns difficulty signals during supervised fine-tuning before reinforcement learning adjusts reasoning allocation across stages. Evaluation is limited to two benchmarks, MMAU-test-mini and MMAR, so generalization to other audio tasks and model scales remains to be verified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Large audio-language models (LALMs) are multimodal systems that combine audio encoders with language model backbones to jointly process speech, environmental sounds, and music. Recent work has explored adaptive reasoning to balance accuracy against efficiency, but many approaches estimate difficulty coarsely and ignore the varied factors that determine how much reasoning an instance actually needs. Benchmarks such as MMAU and MMAR evaluate audio understanding and complex reasoning across speech, sound, and music domains, providing the testbed for this work.

## [VETR: Execution-Grounded Self-Improving Agent for Symbolic Graphics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13098) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13098v1) introduces VETR (Verified Execution Trajectories for Refinement), a framework that enables agentic recursive self-improvement over executable world representations for symbolic graphics reasoning. VETR retains aligned program/render records with bounded runtime observations and adds an execution refiner that compiles observed writes and state changes into constrained alternative programs, validating their endpoints and preservation conditions against captured runtime state before an ordinary visual verifier assesses goal relevance. Most agentic graphics systems refine scenes only from final rendered images, which provides ambiguous supervision; VETR instead mines execution traces to learn which symbolic edits survived, disappeared, or interfered, offering a more reliable self-improvement signal. This could improve visual agents that must manipulate executable world representations, a direction also pursued by paradigms such as Code-as-World. Evaluations on BlenderGym (280 tasks) and BlenderBench (27 released tasks) with GPT-5.6-luna and Qwen3.8-27B show VETR consistently outperforms refinement strategies including BlenderAlchemy and VIGA, with controlled ablations isolating the contributions of execution refinement and role-asymmetric evidence organization under fixed generator-interaction caps. Repair construction requires no additional language-model calls, but may require additional executions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Symbolic graphics reasoning means reasoning about scenes through programs or symbolic structures rather than raw pixels, and executable world representations express a scene's composition, dynamics, and appearance as runnable code. Prior agentic systems improve such scenes through repeated interaction, but they typically judge success only from the final render, ignoring what happened during program execution. VETR addresses this gap by treating execution traces themselves as evidence for refining the symbolic program.

## [Foresight: Anticipatory Generative Modeling for Road-Network Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13131) ⭐️ 6.0/10

This paper introduces Anticipatory Generative Modeling, a new graph-constrained generative paradigm for road-network trajectory generation, and proposes a model named Foresight that formulates a probabilistic 'anticipate-select-update-re-anticipate' loop to resolve next transition selection at intersections. Extensive experiments show Foresight outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Existing generative methods such as diffusion models simulate location sequences and often fail at next transition selection, sometimes producing trajectories that drift off the road network. By shifting from location simulation to behavior modeling at intersections, this work could improve the realism and usability of generated trajectories for urban applications like traffic management and urban planning. The core idea is to incorporate anticipation of how the future route may unfold toward the destination, enabling longer-term foresight while continuing sequential generation along the historical trajectory. The approach is graph-constrained, meaning generated trajectories are kept consistent with the road network structure, though the abstract does not report specific quantitative metrics or dataset details.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Trajectory generation is a foundational task for urban applications such as traffic management and urban planning. Within a road network, vehicle movement along a road segment is largely deterministic, but at intersections multiple outgoing paths may appear equally plausible and can lead to very different downstream routes, making next transition selection the critical challenge. Graph-constrained generative models aim to enforce domain-specific structural properties, such as road-network connectivity, on generated outputs.

## [RoleWorld: Symbolic World Models via Functional Role Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13172) ⭐️ 6.0/10

RoleWorld introduces a symbolic world modeling method that represents how objects behave in interactions using reusable role prototypes plus environment-specific parameter values, rather than organizing knowledge around environment-specific objects. A teacher LLM extracts role prototypes, executable transition rules over role instances, and a skill guiding the actor LLM; in a target world the actor uses this skill with online-refined role assignments and parameter estimates, achieving robust generalization on the MARS benchmark without any LLM parameter updates. This work targets a core weakness of LLM-agent world models: poor transfer to new environments whose objects differ but whose interaction structure is similar. By decoupling reusable role prototypes from environment-specific parameters, it offers a practical route to generalization and knowledge reuse for model-based planning agents, a long-standing challenge in reinforcement learning and agent research. The method keeps transition rules fixed and performs no LLM parameter updates; adaptation happens online through feedback that refines role assignments and parameter estimates, and the actor actively tests a role-instance hypothesis when its uncertainty affects the current decision. Evaluation is limited to the MARS benchmark, and the approach assumes target environments share interaction structure and underlying dynamics with the source.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: World models let LLM agents predict the effects of their actions so they can plan and decide. Recent approaches encode world dynamics as symbolic rules or executable programs, whose compositional structure in principle supports transfer, but they still tie knowledge to environment-specific objects. RoleWorld instead abstracts behavior into functional roles, so the same role prototype can be reused across environments while only its parameters change.

## [RegulaTutor: Theory-Informed Framework for Long-Term Personalized Tutoring with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13399) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced RegulaTutor, a theory-informed framework that connects historical learner evidence, learner interpretation, and instruction to enable long-term personalized tutoring with large language models. The framework is guided by self-regulated learning (SRL) research and operationalizes a cognitive, affective, metacognitive, and motivational (CAMM) framework, with an offline refinement procedure called PairRoute that distinguishes harness, model, and mixed failures. This work addresses a key gap in LLM-based tutoring systems, where memory and retrieval are used for personalization but the pedagogical rationale linking historical evidence to teaching strategies is often implicit. By making the reasoning explicit and evidence-linked, RegulaTutor could improve the transparency and effectiveness of AI tutoring, benefiting developers and learners in AI in education. RegulaTutor's tutoring harness derives evidence-supported learner interpretations and teaching policies that retain links to supporting evidence and update as new observations arrive. PairRoute uses intermediate execution records to propose and evaluate targeted harness revisions, then constructs reviewed stage-level supervision for confirmed model failures; results show improved historical-evidence accuracy, learner diagnosis, and judged teaching quality across evaluated model configurations.

The CAMM framework extends SRL by considering cognitive, affective, metacognitive, and motivational processes that learners navigate during study. ## [Cross-Teacher Guarding Tackles Gradient Conflicts in Multi-Teacher Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13521) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes Cross-Teacher Guarding for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (Road MOPD), which uses non-routed teachers as auxiliary cross-domain critics on the same student trajectories. It coordinates supervision via token-level credit assignment and preventive multi-teacher gradient projection, reporting over 5 percentage points gain under high conflict and improved overall performance in low-conflict and three-domain settings. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation is increasingly used to merge specialized capabilities into a single student model, but gradient conflicts can degrade performance in disadvantaged domains. Road MOPD offers a practical way to mitigate this degradation, which could benefit researchers and engineers building unified models from multiple domain experts. The method uses teacher agreement to strengthen jointly supported token-level corrections and attenuate conflicting signals, while auxiliary gradients from the same trajectories constrain potentially conflicting updates with adaptive projection strength. Experiments show over 5 percentage points gain under high conflict, but the approach is evaluated as a preprint without community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on its own generated trajectories while a teacher provides dense token-level supervision, addressing the distribution mismatch of off-policy distillation. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) extends this by routing domain-specific data to specialized teachers, but updates from all domains still act on shared parameters, causing gradient conflicts when teachers differ substantially. Gradient conflict is a well-known issue in multi-task learning, where conflicting gradients can destabilize training and degrade performance.

## [Tool Learns Size-Independent Constraint Models from Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13640) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13640) presents a Constraint Acquisition Tool that learns size-specific constraint models from input data and positive/negative example solutions, then generalizes them into a size-independent model that can run on unseen instances. The system outputs MiniZinc code plus a textual model description, and its feasibility was demonstrated on the PTHG21 Challenge problems. Automating the jump from instance-specific models to reusable, size-independent constraint programs could significantly reduce the manual modeling effort required in constraint programming. If the approach scales beyond the challenge benchmarks, it may help both researchers and practitioners generate solver-ready MiniZinc models directly from example solutions. The tool first identifies size-specific models using typical patterns over global and binary constraints, then generalizes them to a size-independent form; it produces both MiniZinc code and a textual description. Evaluation is limited to the example problems of the PTHG21 Challenge, so broader scalability and robustness remain open questions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Constraint acquisition is a subfield of AI and machine learning that infers constraint theories from examples of feasible (positive) and infeasible (negative) assignments. MiniZinc is a free, open-source, solver-independent modeling language for specifying constraint satisfaction and optimization problems. The PTHG21 Challenge, organized around the 'Progress Towards the Holy Grail' workshop at CP 2021, provides a series of constraint acquisition problems with standardized data and result formats.

## [Staged Curriculum Difficulty Progression in GRPO Boosts GSM8K Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13669) ⭐️ 6.0/10

This paper re-estimates sample difficulty under the current policy and designs a staged curriculum that progressively shifts the active difficulty band toward harder samples in GRPO. On GSM8K, progressing to a moderately harder band improves test accuracy to 0.6591 ± 0.0077 across three seeds, compared with 0.6449 ± 0.0024 for a fixed-medium curriculum, while further progression drops accuracy to 0.6405 ± 0.0053. The results show that persistently selecting medium-difficulty samples, a common heuristic in GRPO online selection, may not yield the strongest final performance, and that effective curricula can benefit from progressing beyond medium difficulty. This has practical implications for RL fine-tuning of LLMs, where sample selection directly affects training efficiency and final model quality. The benefit of difficulty progression is non-monotonic: moving to a moderately harder band helps, but pushing too far toward overly difficult samples hurts accuracy. The study is limited to GSM8K and three random seeds, and the abstract is truncated, so broader generalization remains to be validated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm for fine-tuning LLMs that reduces compute by using group-relative rewards instead of a separate value model. Curriculum learning is a training paradigm that orders examples from easy to hard to improve convergence and generalization. GSM8K is a dataset of 8.5K grade-school math word problems used to evaluate multi-step mathematical reasoning in language models.

## [UkrMeme Dataset Enables Explainable Persuasion Detection in Ukrainian Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13783) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces UkrMeme, a dataset of 200 organically produced Ukrainian wartime memes annotated for persuasion techniques, along with PTM-UA, a Ukrainian translation of a subset of the Propaganda 950 benchmark that preserves original visuals while translating meme text. It evaluates vision-language models in a zero-shot prompting setup across image, text, and multimodal inputs using hierarchical F1 metrics, and compares Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) with model-generated token attributions to analyze the semantic categories of evidence emphasized by each explanation method. This work addresses an understudied area—persuasion detection in Ukrainian memes—which is crucial for disinformation research in conflict contexts. By providing a novel dataset and benchmark, it enables more systematic evaluation of vision-language models on multimodal persuasion detection and explainability, potentially aiding fact-checkers and researchers working on Ukrainian information operations. The dataset consists of 200 organically produced memes, and the benchmark PTM-UA is a translated subset of Propaganda 950 that preserves original images. Evaluation uses hierarchical F1 metrics, and explainability analysis compares LRP with model-generated token attributions to understand which semantic evidence each method highlights.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Memes are a central form of online communication, especially in political conflicts, where they combine visual and textual cues to persuade. Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that jointly interpret images and text, extending large language models to multimodal tasks. Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) is an explainable AI technique that attributes a model's prediction to input features by propagating relevance backward through the network. Hierarchical F1 is a variant of the F1 score that accounts for hierarchical relationships between classes, useful when persuasion techniques are organized in a taxonomy.

## [Kairos proposes byte-level foundation model architecture with convergence sanity checks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12495) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.12495) introduces Kairos, a byte-level foundation model architecture that combines bidirectional gated-DeltaNet linear attention with sliding-window attention over shared projections (LiZAttention2), block-wise Attention-Residual aggregation, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts feed-forward layer, and a multi-scale byte-level codec. The report runs convergence sanity checks by overfitting a single 440-byte sentence at 1–15M parameters with three seeds, showing minimum losses near 10^-3 at 3,000 steps and a codec ranking from 0.018 (patch codec) to 0.354 (depthwise convolutional codec) after 600 steps. This work explores a unified byte-level modeling approach that could eventually handle text, image, audio, video, lidar, and control streams in one shared byte space, potentially simplifying multimodal foundation model design. However, the current results are only convergence sanity checks, not evidence of generalization or multimodal capability, so the impact remains speculative until the planned pretraining run is completed. The two attention variants tested (LiZAttention2 and window attention alone) reach a minimum loss near 10^-3 at 3,000 steps, and the apparent advantage of shared over separate attention projections disappears with more steps or more diverse data and reverses when projections are frozen, indicating a convergence-speed effect rather than a capacity effect. The discussion analytically sizes a 200M-total/25M-active target, but none of its throughput figures are measured, and the authors explicitly state that the checks say nothing about generalization, multimodal training, or comparison with existing models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Byte-level language models avoid tokenization by operating directly on raw bytes, which is attractive for multimodal data but produces much longer sequences and requires efficient architectures. Gated DeltaNet is a recent linear-attention variant that compresses the KV cache into a fixed-size recurrent state, while sliding-window attention restricts interactions to a local window for linear complexity; hybrid models often combine both. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) layers activate only a subset of parameters per token to increase capacity without proportional compute, and multi-scale byte codecs hierarchically encode byte streams to reduce sequence length.

## [TimesFM 3.0 Tops Day-Ahead Electricity Forecasting Benchmark for University Building](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12638) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks statistical, machine-learning, recurrent neural network, and pretrained TimesFM models for day-ahead electricity forecasting in a university building, evaluating all configurations on the same 57 test days using chronological validation. TimesFM 3.0 with covariates achieved the lowest overall errors, reducing mean absolute error by approximately 43% relative to weekly seasonal-naive forecasting, though paired daily comparisons did not establish a clear MAE advantage over XGBoost. This work provides concrete evidence that pretrained time-series foundation models can serve as strong zero-shot baselines for building energy management, potentially reducing the need for building-specific model training. It also highlights that locally fitted alternatives like XGBoost remain competitive, giving building operators a practical trade-off between accuracy, setup effort, and inference speed. TimesFM performed strongly using only past electricity consumption, and adding covariates improved holiday forecasts but slightly worsened workday and weekend forecasts, leaving overall MAE almost unchanged; weather covariates used retrospective observations. TimesFM requires no building-specific weight training, while XGBoost offers rapid local fitting and inference, and the evaluated LSTM took longer to fit without outperforming either approach.

**Background**: Day-ahead electricity forecasting helps building operators schedule heating, ventilation, and other services around anticipated demand. TimesFM is a decoder-only time-series foundation model from Google Research pretrained on roughly 100 billion real-world time points, with TimesFM 3.0 adding native multivariate forecasting and flexible covariate support. ## [iRAG: Incremental RAG Builds Knowledge Base from Human Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12666) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12666) introduces iRAG, a human-in-the-loop decision-support system that combines Retrieval-Augmented Generation with hybrid decision-making, starting from an initially empty knowledge base that is progressively populated by storing prior human decisions as retrievable precedents. The system progresses from silent observation to evidence-grounded contestation and finally to autonomous handling with abstention, matching baseline final-decision error under 10% drift and reducing it under 40% drift across all configurations. This work addresses a practical gap in conventional RAG systems, which typically assume a pre-existing static knowledge base, by showing how a system can bootstrap domain knowledge from human decisions in dynamic environments. It is relevant to anyone building decision-support tools in domains where labeled data is scarce and conditions change over time, such as clinical triage, compliance review, or customer operations. The evaluation reveals a stability-plasticity trade-off rather than a general advantage: retrieval decay improves performance on changed knowledge but increases error on stable knowledge. The authors also note that human oversight is an architectural provision of the workflow, and its usability with real human users remains to be evaluated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 12, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique, first proposed in 2020, in which a large language model retrieves relevant documents from an external source before generating a response, reducing hallucinations and avoiding the need to retrain the model on new data. Concept drift refers to the evolution of data over time that invalidates a model's learned relationships, while the stability-plasticity trade-off describes the tension between retaining prior knowledge and incorporating new information. iRAG sits at the intersection of these ideas, applying incremental learning to a RAG knowledge base that grows from human decisions.
